"Haste to the wedding!"

"Wooed and married and a'."

"Edith!" said Margaret, softly, "Edith!"

But as Margaret half suspected, Edith had succumbed to sleep. She lay curled upon the sofa in the back drawing-room of Harley Street, appearing exquisite in her white muslin and blue ribbons. If Titania had ever been attired in white muslin and blue ribbons, and had fallen asleep upon a crimson damask sofa in a rear chamber, Edith might easily have been mistaken for her fairy counterpart. Margaret was once again struck by her cousin's beauty. Having grown up together since childhood, Edith had long been remarked upon by everyone save Margaret for her prettiness; yet Margaret had never truly considered it until recent days, when the looming prospect of separation seemed to amplify every sweet quality and charm Edith possessed. They had been discussing wedding dresses, wedding ceremonies, Captain Lennox, and the tales he had shared regarding Edith's future life on Corfu, where his regiment was stationed. They had also debated the formidable challenge of maintaining a piano in tune—a difficulty Edith regarded as a primary threat to her marital happiness—and planned the gowns required for the Scottish visits that would follow her marriage. However, the whispered tones had gradually grown drowsier. After a pause of several minutes, Margaret realized that, despite the hum of conversation in the adjacent room, Edith had rolled herself into a soft sphere of muslin, ribbon, and silken curls, drifting off into a peaceful post-prandial slumber.

Margaret had intended to confide in her cousin some of the plans and visions she harbored concerning her future life in the country parsonage, where her parents resided. It was there, amidst her bright holidays, that she had spent her youth, although for the last decade Aunt Shaw's house had effectively served as her home. Deprived of a listener, however, she was forced to brood silently on the impending changes. It was a happy brooding, albeit tinged with regret at the indefinite separation from her gentle aunt and beloved cousin. As she contemplated the joy of fulfilling the significant role of sole daughter in Helstone parsonage, fragments of conversation drifted from the next room. Aunt Shaw was speaking to the five or six ladies who had dined there, whose husbands remained in the dining-room. These were the familiar acquaintances of the household; neighbours whom Mrs. Shaw termed "friends" simply because she dined with them more frequently than others, and because if either she or Edith required something from them—or vice versa—they did not hesitate to visit before luncheon. These ladies and their husbands had been invited specifically to consume a farewell dinner in honor of Edith's approaching nuptials. Edith had initially objected to this arrangement, as Captain Lennox was expected to arrive via a late train that very evening; yet, though she was a spoiled child, she lacked a strong will of her own due to her carelessness and idleness, and yielded when she discovered her mother had absolutely ordered those seasonal delicacies supposedly efficacious against excessive grief at farewell feasts. Contenting herself by leaning back in her chair, merely manipulating the food on her plate, Edith appeared grave and absent-minded, while those around her delighted in the witty remarks of Mr. Grey, the gentleman who invariably sat at the bottom of the table at Mrs. Shaw's gatherings and requested that Edith provide musical entertainment in the drawing-room. Mr. Grey proved particularly agreeable during this farewell dinner, causing the gentlemen to linger downstairs longer than usual. It was fortunate they did so, judging by the fragments of conversation Margaret overheard.

"I suffered too much myself; not that I was not extremely happy with the poor dear General, but still, disparity of age is a drawback; one that I was resolved Edith should not have to encounter. Of course, without any maternal partiality, I foresaw that the dear child was likely to marry early; indeed, I had often said that I was certain she would be wed before she was nineteen. I had quite a prophetic feeling when Captain Lennox"—here the voice dropped to a whisper, yet Margaret could easily fill in the blank. The course of true love in Edith's case had run remarkably smoothly. Mrs. Shaw had succumbed to the presentiment, expressing it as such, and had rather urged on the marriage, although it fell below the expectations many of Edith's acquaintances had formed for her, a young and pretty heiress. Nevertheless, Mrs. Shaw declared that her only child should marry for love—and sighed emphatically, as if love had not been her motive for marrying the General. Mrs. Shaw derived far more enjoyment from the romance of the current engagement than her daughter did. Though Edith was thoroughly and properly in love, she would certainly have preferred a substantial house in Belgravia to all the picturesque allure of the life Captain Lennox described at Corfu. Indeed, the specific aspects that caused Margaret to glow with excitement as she listened, Edith feigned shivering and shuddering at; partly for the pleasure she derived from being coaxed out of her dislike by her devoted lover, and partly because anything resembling a gypsy or makeshift existence was genuinely distasteful to her. Yet, had anyone offered a magnificent house, a vast estate, and a noble title in addition, Edith would still have clung to Captain Lennox so long as the temptation lasted. Once the allure faded, it was possible she might harbor little qualms of ill-concealed regret that Captain Lennox could not embody everything desirable in himself. In this regard, she was but her mother's child: Mrs. Shaw, after deliberately uniting with General Shaw driven solely by respect for his character and establishment, constantly, though quietly, lamented her hard lot in being joined to one whom she could not love.

"I have spared no expense in her trousseau," were the next words Margaret heard. "She possesses all the beautiful Indian shawls and scarves the General gave to me, which I shall never wear again."

"She is a lucky girl," replied another voice, which Margaret recognized as belonging to Mrs. Gibson, a lady who was taking a double interest in the conversation due to the fact that one of her daughters had been married within the last few weeks. "Helen had set her heart upon an Indian shawl, but truly, when I found what an extravagant price was demanded, I was obliged to refuse her. She will be quite envious when she hears of Edith possessing Indian shawls. What manner are they? Delhi? With the lovely little borders?"

Margaret heard her aunt's voice again, but this time it seemed as if she had risen from her half-recumbent posture and was gazing into the more dimly lit back drawing-room. "Edith! Edith!" cried she; and then she sank as if wearied by the exertion. Margaret stepped forward.

"Edith is asleep, Aunt Shaw. Is there anything I can do?"

All the ladies exclaimed "poor child!" upon receiving this distressing intelligence regarding Edith; and the tiny lap-dog in Mrs. Shaw's arms began to bark, as if agitated by the outburst of pity.

"Hush, Tiny! You naughty little girl! You will wake your mistress. It was merely to ask Edith if she would instruct Newton to bring down her shawls; perhaps you would go, Margaret dear?"

Margaret ascended into the old nursery at the very summit of the house, where Newton was diligently arranging some laces required for the wedding. While Newton departed (not without a muttered grumble) to unlock the shawls—which had already been displayed four or five times that day—Margaret surveyed the nursery. It was the first room in that house with which she had become acquainted nine years prior, when she was brought, untamed from the forest, to share the home, play, and lessons of her cousin Edith. She recalled the dark, somber atmosphere of the London nursery, presided over by an austere and ceremonious nurse who was terribly particular about clean hands and torn frocks. She recollected the first tea ceremony held there—separate from her father and aunt, who were dining somewhere far below, an infinite depth of stairs; for unless she were situated in the sky (so the child thought), they must surely be buried deep within the bowels of the earth. At home—prior to her relocation to Harley Street—her mother's dressing-room had served as her nursery; and as they maintained early hours in the country parsonage, Margaret had always taken her meals with her father and mother. Oh! How vividly the tall, stately girl of eighteen remembered the tears shed with such wild, passionate grief by the little girl of nine, as she hid her face beneath the bedclothes on that first night; and how she was bid not to cry by the nurse, lest it disturb Miss Edith; and how she had wept as bitterly, yet more quietly, until her newly arrived, grand, and pretty aunt descended softly upstairs accompanied by Mr. Hale to reveal his little sleeping daughter. Then the little Margaret had hushed her sobs and endeavored to lie quiet as if asleep, fearing to render her father unhappy through her grief, which she dared not express before her aunt, and which she rather believed it wrong to feel at all after the long hopes, plans, and schemes they had undertaken at home before her wardrobe could be arranged to suit her grander circumstances, and before Papa could abandon his parish to ascend to London, even if only for a few days.

Now she loved the old nursery, though it was but a dismantled place; and she looked about with a kind of cat-like regret at the prospect of leaving it forever in three days.

"Ah, Newton!" said she, "I

"By the soft green light in the woody glade,
On the banks of moss where thy childhood played..."

The journey home was quiet, a gentle drift from the bustling hubbub of Harley Street back to the hushed solitude of Helstone. Margaret sat beside her father, wrapped in the comfortable silence of the carriage, though her mind churned with the whirlwind of the last forty-eight hours. They had traveled to assist at a wedding, a joyous occasion for the groom, yet for Margaret's family, it was merely a transit point. Her mother, Mrs. Hale, had stayed behind at home, a decision shrouded in "half-reasons" that baffled everyone save Mr. Hale himself. He knew the truth with painful clarity: he lacked the funds to purchase a fresh ensemble of silks and bonnets for his wife, and thus, the grey satin gown she wore to the wedding was a garment of compromise, caught between the fading of fashion and the demands of propriety. It was a grievance, however trivial, that Mrs. Shaw, her wealthy sister-in-law, could not fathom. To Mrs. Shaw, the tragedy was not financial; it was the sorrow of a disparity in ages, the lament of a woman who marries a man eight years her senior, possessing a temper as sweet as his blue-black hair. To the Shaw girl, marriage was a romantic triumph; to Mrs. Hale, it was a ledger of unfulfilled desires for finer fabrics and domestic splendor.

Margaret, however, did not dwell on the material lack. She viewed her mother's absence as a blessing. Returning to the Vicarage meant avoiding the chaotic theater of the wedding preparations, where she had played the frantic Figaro, juggling requests and farewells. As the train rattled onward, the physical ache of exhaustion gave way to a deeper, sadder emotion. The hurried goodbyes of the last few days, the faces of those she had known so intimately, pressed upon her with a weight of loss. The times were gone, irrevocably severed, and the grief of what was lost settled heavily in her chest. Yet, as the carriage slowed, her gaze fell upon her father, asleep.

Frederick Hale leaned back, his blue-black hair now streaked with a thin, premature grey that lay starkly over his brow. The bones of his face, usually softened by youth, were laid bare; the lines around his mouth and eyes spoke not of serenity, but of a weary, habitual depression. It was a repose earned through struggle, not born of contentment. Margaret watched him, her heart tightening. *Poor Frederick,* she thought, a sigh escaping her lips. *If he had become a clergyman, if he had not sailed away into the navy!* A shadow hung over his return, a "terrible affair" that Aunt Shaw had alluded to vaguely, a mystery that had cost them all so dearly. He looked so fragile, so burdened, and the thought of returning home, of being close enough to comfort both his father and his mother, filled her with a fierce, protective resolve.

When the carriage halted, Margaret greeted him with a bright, practiced smile, masking the fatigue that gnawed at her. He smiled back, faintly, as if the effort required more energy than he possessed. His lips parted slightly, a nervous habit that unsettled his features, giving him an air of indecision. Yet, beneath the worry, there lingered the same large, soft eyes that belonged to his daughter—eyes that moved slowly, grandly, veiled by pale lids. Margaret was his reflection, not in her beauty, which some whispered was lacking compared to her parents' handsomeness, but in her spirit. Her mouth, wide and curved in a soft line of rich red lips, framed a face that held a dignity rarely seen in one so young. When she spoke to her father, however, the reserve melted away, replaced by a brightness akin to morning, dotted with dimples and glances that burned with childish gladness and boundless hope.

They arrived in Helstone in the latter part of July. The air was thick, brooding, and the forest trees stood in a dark, dusky green, while the ferns below caught the slanting rays of the sun. For Margaret, this landscape was paradise. She walked with her father, crushing the ferns with a cruel, joyful glee, inhaling their unique fragrance. Out on the commons, amidst the warm, scented light, she saw a world teeming with wild, free creatures. Here, in the green shade, she found her true self. She was not the prim, well-mannered girl of Harley Street; she was a friend to the villagers, learning their dialect, nursing their babes, and carrying dainties to the sick. She dreamed of teaching at the school, treating education as an appointed task, though the temptation to wander into nearby cottages to visit individual friends often drew her away.

Yet, the idyll of the outdoors masked a discord within the walls of the Vicarage. Her mother, usually so kind, was plagued by a restless discontent. She fretted that the Bishop neglected his duties by failing to grant Mr. Hale a better living, and she subtly reproached her husband for his inability to resign his current parish to seek a larger charge elsewhere. Every time Mrs. Hale urged him to pursue preferment, Frederick shrank further, overwhelmed by a sense of duty and bewilderment that only deepened with time. Margaret, witnessing this friction, strove to reconcile her mother to the humble surroundings, trying to prove that the trees did not harm her health, tempting her onto the upland commons. For a time, the fresh air helped, but as autumn deepened and the weather turned fickle, Mrs. Hale's complaints resurfaced with renewed vigor. She railed against the isolation, wishing they lived closer to the Stansfields or the Gormons.

"Gormons!" Margaret exclaimed, wrinkling her nose. "Those shopkeepers who made their fortune in Southampton? Oh, I'm glad we don't visit them! I despise 'shoppy' people. We are far better off knowing only cottagers and laborers, folks without pretense."

"You must not be so fastidious, Margaret," her mother chided, secretly recalling a handsome young Mr. Gorman she had once glimpsed at a party.

"No!" Margaret insisted, her tone ringing with youthful conviction. "My tastes are comprehensive. I like those who till the soil, soldiers, sailors, and the learned professions. I certainly do not care to admire butchers, bakers, or candlestick-makers."

"But the Gormons were coach-builders, respectable tradesmen."

"Coach-building is a trade nonetheless, and a far more useless one than baking bread," Margaret retorted, her mind drifting to the endless drives in Aunt Shaw's carriage, a life she had longed to escape. "How I used to tire of it! How I longed to walk!"

And walk she did, whenever the heavens permitted. She danced across the heaths, the soft violence of the west wind bearing her forward like a fallen leaf, light and easy. But the evenings offered little solace. After tea, the silence descended. Mr. Hale retreated to his small library, leaving his wife and daughter alone. Books, once a treasure trove for young Margaret, offered scant amusement now. Only the well-bound classics remained, weeded from her father's shelves to fill the drawing-room racks. Thomson's *Seasons*, Hayley's *Cowper*, Middleton's *Cicero*—light, perhaps, but devoid of the intellectual fire that once animated her father's nights.

These quiet hours were spent listening to her mother recount tales of London life, stories of ease and comfort that served only to highlight the narrow means of Helstone. On such evenings, Margaret would fall silent, drawn to the rhythmic *drip-drip* of rain on the leaded windows. Twice, the urge rose within her to ask the question that haunted her: Where was Frederick? What was he doing? Why had they heard nothing? But then, the memory of her mother's delicate health and her profound dislike for Helstone—which stemmed directly from the trauma of Frederick's departure—would make her pause. The full account of his involvement in the Mutiny remained buried, a secret doom destined for sad oblivion.

Instead, she turned to her father, hoping he might offer answers. But the news was dry, skeletal. In a letter prior to their departure, he had written that Frederick was still at Rio, in good health, sending his love. It was a fact, but it lacked the living intelligence Margaret craved. Frederick was spoken of only as "Poor Frederick." His room, untouched since his departure, was meticulously dusted and arranged by Dixon, the maid. Dixon, who had once served the Beresford girls, considered Mr. Hale the blight that had ruined her young mistress's prospects. Loyal to a fault, Dixon treated Frederick's empty chamber as if he might return tonight, arranging his linens with a tenderness that betrayed her devotion to her fallen employer.

Margaret sensed a shift in the atmosphere, a tension that her mother failed to perceive. Her father's spirits, usually tender and responsive to the smallest tidings of others, had grown distant. He seemed preoccupied by a subject that no daily action could relieve. He stopped venturing into the garden, preferring to stand dreamily by the study window, watching the lane. He waited for the postman, his head bowed in a half-respectful, half-confidential nod as the carrier passed, disappearing into the room with the visible weight of a heavy heart and an occupied mind.

> "Learn to win a lady's faith
>   Nobly, as the thing is high;
> Bravely, as for life and death—
>   With a loyal gravity.
>
> Lead her from the festive boards,
>   Point her to the starry skies,
> Guard her, by your truthful words,
>   Pure from courtship's flatteries."

"Mr. Henry Lennox." The name had lingered in Margaret's thoughts but a fleeting moment prior, recalling his polite inquiry regarding her domestic duties. It was, as the French proverb suggests, *parler du soleil et l'on en voit les rayons*; and a sudden, radiant warmth spread across Margaret's countenance as she lowered her workbasket and stepped forward to greet him. "Tell Mamma, Sarah," she directed gently, "that Mamma and I have countless questions for you concerning Edith. I am deeply grateful for your visit."

"Was I not bound to return?" he asked, lowering his voice from the cheerful register he had previously employed.

"But news of you had traveled only as far as the remote Highlands," Margaret observed, "leading me to believe Hampshire itself remained untouched by your presence."

"Oh!" he replied with a lighter cadence, "our young pair were engaging in such whimsical pranks, courting every conceivable hazard—scaling mountains, navigating lakes—that I genuinely believed they required a guardian angel. And indeed, they did; they utterly surpassed Uncle Edmund's capacity for oversight, keeping the old gentleman in a state of perpetual alarm for sixteen hours daily. Upon realizing their inability to trust themselves alone, I deemed it my solemn duty to remain until I had safely escorted them aboard at Plymouth."

"You have visited Plymouth? Oh! Edith never mentioned that. Naturally, her letters have been a blur of haste recently. Did they actually depart on Tuesday?"

"They certainly sailed, liberating me from a heavy burden of responsibility. Edith entrusted me with numerous messages for you. I believe I retain a diminutive note from her somewhere... yes, here it is."

"Oh! Thank you," Margaret exclaimed. Yet, a fleeting impulse stirred within her—to read the letter privately, unseen by another gaze. Seizing this pretext, she excused herself, claiming she must inform her mother anew that Mr. Lennox was present, suggesting perhaps Sarah had erred in her initial notification.

Once Margaret had departed, Mr. Lennox commenced his customary scrutiny of the surroundings. The modest drawing-room appeared at its finest, bathed in the streaming light of the morning sun. The central casement of the bow window stood open, allowing clusters of roses and scarlet honeysuckle to spill teasingly around the sill; the small lawn below burst forth with verbenas and geraniums of dazzling hues. Yet, the brilliance without rendered the interior colors pale and somewhat diminished by comparison. The carpet showed signs of age, far from new; the chintz curtains bore the marks of frequent washing. The entire apartment, smaller and shabbier than anticipated, served merely as a humble backdrop and frame for Margaret, who stood before it with a queenly poise. He picked up one of the volumes resting on the table—it was the *Paradiso* of Dante, bound in proper antique Italian vellum with gold tooling. Beside it lay a dictionary and several pages filled with words transcribed in Margaret's handwriting. They formed a somber list, yet somehow, he found himself drawn to examine them. Lowering the book with a sigh, he remarked, "The living appears as limited as she described. It strikes me as peculiar, considering the Beresford lineage belongs to a respectable family."

Meanwhile, Margaret had located her mother. Today marked one of Mrs. Hale's fitful intervals, wherein existence seemed fraught with difficulty and hardship; consequently, Mr. Lennox's arrival cast a shadow over her composure, though she secretly felt honored that he deigned to pay a visit.

"It is most unfortunate!" she lamented. "We are dining early today, subsisting solely on cold meats so the servants may finish their ironing. Yet, naturally, we must invite him to dine—he is, after all, Edith's brother-in-law. And your father is in such a low spirits this morning regarding some matter—I know not what. I entered the study moments ago to find him with his face buried in the desk, hands covering it. I assured him the air of Helstone agrees with neither him nor myself, yet he suddenly raised his head and beseeched me to cease speaking ill of Helstone, declaring he could not endure it further; if there exists one place he loves upon earth, it is Helstone. Nevertheless, I am convinced the atmosphere remains damp and relaxing."

A sensation akin to a thin, cold cloud drifted between Margaret and the sunlight. She had listened with patience, hoping her mother's venting might bring solace, but now it was time to redirect attention back to Mr. Lennox.

"Papa holds a high regard for Mr. Lennox; they connected wonderfully at the wedding breakfast. I am certain his presence will lift Papa's spirits. And need not trouble yourself regarding the dinner, dear Mamma. Cold meat suits a lunch perfectly, particularly when viewed through the lens of Mr. Lennox, who will likely perceive a two o'clock repast as precisely that."

"But what shall we do with him until then? It is barely half-past ten."

"I shall request he accompany me for a sketching excursion. I am aware he possesses artistic talents, and this will remove him from your immediate vicinity, Mamma. Now, do enter promptly; he would find it exceedingly strange otherwise."

Mrs. Hale removed her black silk apron and smoothed her features. She presented a picture of genteel refinement as she welcomed Mr. Lennox with the cordiality reserved for one almost kin to the family. He evidently anticipated spending the day and accepted the invitation with a joyful alacrity that left Mrs. Hale wishing she possessed additional cold beef. He expressed delight at everything, particularly at Margaret's suggestion of a joint sketching trip, and declined with evident pleasure to disturb Mr. Hale, anticipating their reunion at dinner. Margaret produced her drawing supplies for his selection; once the paper and brushes were duly chosen, the pair departed in the merriest spirits imaginable.

"Now, pray, pause here for a brief moment," Margaret instructed. "These cottages haunted me relentlessly during those rainy fourteen days, reproaching me for failing to capture them."

"Before they collapsed and vanished forever. Truly, if they merit a sketch—and they are undeniably picturesque—we ought not delay until next season. However, where shall we sit?"

"Oh! You might have emerged directly from chambers in the Temple, rather than residing two months in the Highlands! Observe this magnificent trunk of a tree, abandoned by the woodcutters in the perfect position for illumination. I shall drape my plaid over it, creating a veritable forest throne."

"With your feet submerged in that puddle, serving as a regal footstool!" he interjected playfully. "Wait, I shall shift slightly, allowing you to approach closer from this direction. Who inhabits these cottages?"

"They were constructed by squatters fifty or sixty years hence. One stands uninhabited; the foresters intend to demolish it immediately following the demise of the remaining occupant, a poor old soul! Look—there he stands. I must address him. He is so deaf you will overhear all our confidences."

The elderly man stood bareheaded beneath the sun, leaning upon his cane at the threshold of his dwelling. His stern features softened into a slow, gentle smile as Margaret approached and addressed him. Mr. Lennox swiftly incorporated both figures into his sketch, concluding the landscape with a subtle nod to their presence—as Margaret later realized, amidst the gathering of waterskins and discarded papers, as they displayed their works to one another. She laughed and blushed; Mr. Lennox watched her countenance intently.

"Now, I deem that treacherous," she declared. "I scarcely imagined you intended to make Old Isaac and me the subject of your art when you requested I inquire about the cottages' history."

"It proved irresistible. You cannot conceive the strength of the temptation. I hardly dare confess how greatly I shall cherish this sketch."

He was uncertain whether she had caught the latter sentiment before she retreated to the brook to cleanse her palette. Returning, she appeared somewhat flushed yet radiated innocence and unconsciousness. He rejoiced in this, for the remark had slipped from his lips unbidden—a rarity for a man like Henry Lennox, who meticulously premeditated his actions.

The atmosphere of home resumed its brightness upon their return. The clouds hovering over her mother's brow had dissipated under the auspicious influence of a brace of carp, graciously presented by a neighbor. Mr. Hale had concluded his morning rounds and awaited his visitor just outside the wicket gate leading to the garden. He appeared a complete gentleman, clad in a somewhat threadbare coat and a well-worn hat. Margaret felt a profound, tender pride in her father; she always marveled at how favorably he impressed strangers. Yet, her keen eye detected traces of unusual disturbance upon his face, disturbances that were suppressed but not entirely dispelled.

Mr. Hale requested inspection of their sketches.

"I suspect you have rendered the tints on the thatch somewhat too dark, have you not?" he observed, returning Margaret's work and extending his hand for Mr. Lennox's, which the latter hesitated to accept for a fleeting instant.

"No, Father! I do not believe I have," Margaret interjected, peering over

"He cast me upon some naked shore,
Where I may track
Only the print of some sad wreck,
If thou be there though the seas roar,
I shall no gentler calm implore."

Gone. The house stood silent against the evening, the doors locked, the windows shuttered against the fading light. Outside, the sky had lost its deep blues and crimsons, yielding to a grey twilight. Inside, Margaret ascended to prepare for the early tea, finding Dixon in a foul mood. The interruption of a visitor on a busy day had evidently rankled, and he brushed away her hair with vicious, impatient strokes under the guise of haste to visit Mrs. Hale. Yet, despite his urgency, Margaret found herself waiting a long, interminable time in the drawing-room before her mother descended. Alone by the fire, surrounded by unlit candles on the table behind her, she replayed the day: the joyous walk, the pleasant sketching, the cheerful dinner, and finally, that terrible, miserable stroll through the garden.

"How different men are to women!" she thought, a bitter chill settling within her. She was distressed, her instincts screaming that refusal was impossible, leaving her shaken and unhappy. He, conversely, mere minutes after receiving what ought to have been the deepest, holiest rejection of his life, spoke as if briefs, success, and the superficial trappings of a good house and clever company were the sole objectives of his desire. *Oh dear!* How she could have loved him if he had been different, if only he lacked this specific flaw that she now sensed ran deep down into his soul. Perhaps, she reasoned, his lightness was merely a mask covering a bitterness of disappointment, a wound that would have branded her own heart if she had loved and failed.

Her mother entered before Margaret could untangle this whirlwind of speculation. She had to shake off the heavy recollections of the day's events to become a sympathetic listener to Dixon's complaints about a burnt ironing blanket and Susan Lightfoot's artificial flowers, evidence of vanity and giddiness. Mr. Hale sipped his tea in abstracted silence; Margaret carried the weight of the conversation alone. She marveled at how her parents could be so forgetful, so careless of their companion throughout the day, failing to mention his name. For a fleeting moment, she forgot that he had not made her an offer.

After tea, Mr. Hale rose, leaning his elbow on the chimney-piece, his head resting on his hand. He murmured deeply, sighing with a frequency that betrayed profound turmoil. Mrs. Hale stepped out to consult with Dixon regarding winter clothing for the poor. Margaret prepared her mother's worsted work, shrinking from the prospect of a long, empty evening and wishing desperately for bedtime, hoping to revisit the day's events in solitude.

"Margaret!" Mr. Hale called out, suddenly, in a voice that sounded desperate, causing her to start. "Is that tapestry of immediate consequence? Can you leave it and come into my study? I wish to speak to you about something very serious to us all."

*Serious to us all,* she thought. Mr. Lennox had never had the chance for private conversation with her father since her refusal, or else matters would indeed be grave. First, she felt guilty, ashamed of growing into a woman enough to be courted. Second, she feared her father might be displeased that she had independently declined Mr. Lennox's proposal. But soon, logic settled in; nothing recent and sudden could require such complicated thoughts. He gestured for her to sit by him, stirring the fire, snuffing candles, and sighing twice before he could summon the courage to speak. Finally, the words came out with a jarring abruptness: "Margaret! I am going to leave Helstone."

"Leave Helstone, Papa! But why?"

For a minute or two, Mr. Hale said nothing. He fumbled with papers on the table, opening his lips several times only to close them again, lacking the fortitude to utter a syllable. Margaret could not endure the suspense, which gnawed at her father as much as it tore at her heart.

"But why, dear Papa? Do tell me!"

He looked up at her suddenly, then spoke with a slow, forced calmness. "Because I must no longer be a minister in the Church of England."

Margaret had imagined nothing less than the fulfillment of her mother's desires: a preferment forcing him from beautiful Helstone to some stately, silent Close in a cathedral town. Those places were grand, but leaving Helstone forever would have been a lingering, sad pain. Yet, the shock of his actual words was far greater. What could he mean? It was worse for being so mysterious. The piteous distress on his face, almost imploring a merciful judgment from his child, sent a wave of sickness through her. Could he be implicated in anything Frederick had done? Frederick was an outlaw. Had her father, driven by a natural love for his son, connived at any transgression?

"Oh! What is it? Speak, Papa! Tell me all! Why can you no longer be a clergyman? Surely, if the Bishop were told all we know about Frederick, and the hard injustice—"

"It is nothing about Frederick; the Bishop would have nothing to do with that. It is all myself. Margaret, I will tell you about it. I will answer any question this once, but after tonight, let us never speak of it again. I can meet the consequences of my painful, miserable doubts; but it is an effort beyond me to speak of what has caused me so much suffering."

"Doubts, Papa! Doubts as to religion?" Margaret asked, more shocked than ever.

"No! Not doubts as to religion; not the slightest injury to that."

He paused. Margaret sighed, feeling as if she stood on the verge of some new horror. He began again, speaking rapidly, as if rushing to complete a daunting task:

"You could not understand it all if I told you—the anxiety I have harbored for years past, knowing whether I had any right to hold my living; my efforts to quench my smoldering doubts by the authority of the Church. Oh! Margaret, how I love the holy Church from which I am to be shut out!" He could not continue for a moment or two. Margaret could not speak; it seemed to her as terrifying and mysterious as if her father were about to turn Mahometan.

"I have been reading today of the two thousand who were ejected from their churches," continued Mr. Hale, with a faint smile, "trying to steal some of their bravery; but it is of no use—no use—I cannot help feeling it acutely."

"But, Papa, have you well considered? Oh! It seems so terrible, so shocking!" Margaret suddenly burst into tears. The one staid foundation of her home, of her cherished image of her beloved father, seemed to reel and rock. What could she say? What was to be done? The sight of her distress spurred Mr. Hale to steel himself, attempting to comfort her. He swallowed down the dry, choking sobs that had been heaving up from his heart, went to his bookcase, and retrieved a volume he had recently read, believing it to have given him strength for this course.

"Listen, dear Margaret," he said, wrapping one arm around her waist. She took his hand in hers, gripping it tightly, but she could not lift her head; nor could she attend to what he read, so great was her internal agitation.

"This is the soliloquy of one who was once a clergyman in a country parish, like me; it was written by Mr. Oldfield, minister of Carsington, in Derbyshire, a hundred and sixty years ago, or more. His trials are over. He fought the good fight." These final two sentences he whispered, as if to himself. Then he read aloud:

"When thou canst no longer continue in thy work without dishonour to God, discredit to religion, foregoing thy integrity, wounding conscience, spoiling thy peace, and hazarding the loss of thy salvation; in a word, when the conditions upon which thou must continue (if thou wilt continue) in thy employments are sinful, and unwarranted by the word of God, thou mayest, yea, thou must believe that God will turn thy very silence, suspension, deprivation, and laying aside, to His glory, and the advancement of the Gospel's interest. When God will not use thee in one kind, yet He will in another. A soul that desires to serve and honour Him shall never want opportunity to do it; nor must thou so limit the Holy One of Israel, as to think He hath but one way in which He can glorify Himself by thee. He can do it by thy silence as well as by thy preaching; the laying aside as well as thy continuance in thy work. It is not pretence of doing God the greatest service, or performing the weightiest duty, that will excuse the least sin, though that sin capacitated or gave us the opportunity for doing that duty. Thou wilt have little thanks, O my soul! if, when thou art charged with corrupting God's worship, falsifying thy vows, thou pretendest a necessity for it in order to a continuance in the ministry."

As he read, glancing at much more which he chose not to utter, he gained resolution for himself, feeling as if he too could be brave and firm in doing what he believed to be right. But as he ceased, he heard Margaret's low, convulsive sob, and his courage sank beneath the keen sense of suffering

Margaret proved a patient audience for her mother's myriad schemes to alleviate the hardships of the parish's poorest souls. Yet, beneath her polite attention, each proposal struck a fresh blow to her spirit. By the time the frost descended upon the earth, they would be long departed from Helstone. Old Simon's rheumatism would ache in the biting cold, his failing sight unassisted; no one would bring him warm broth or flannel, nor read to him in the quiet of the evenings, unless it were a stranger, and then the old man would gaze in vain for her familiar face. Mary Domville's frail boy would creep to the door, hoping to catch a glimpse of her passing through the woods, only to find emptiness. These friends would never comprehend why she had abandoned them, and there were countless others whose faces she would never again see. "Father has always poured his stipend entirely into the needs of this community," her mother explained, perhaps unaware that she was inadvertently encroaching on future funds. "Yet the winter ahead promises to be severe, and our poor neighbors require aid."

"Oh, Mamma, let us do everything within our power," Margaret urged, blind to the practical implications, seized instead by the desperate notion that this charity was being rendered for the final time. "We may not remain here long."

"Do you feel unwell, my dearest?" Mrs. Hale asked, her anxiety sharp as she misread Margaret's melancholy as physical sickness. "You appear so pale and weary. It is this soft, damp, unhealthy air."

"No, Mamma, it is not that," Margaret corrected gently, finding solace in the scent of the countryside, a stark contrast to the smog-choked streets of Harley Street. "The air is delicious, filled with the freshest fragrance. But I am truly tired; surely it is nearly time for rest."

"Not far off—it is half-past nine. You had better retire immediately, dear. Ask Dixon for some gruel. I shall check on you once you are in bed. I am afraid you have caught a chill, or perhaps breathed in the miasma from those stagnant ponds—"

"Oh, Mamma," Margaret murmured, offering a faint smile as she kissed her mother's cheek, "I am perfectly well; do not trouble yourself. I am simply exhausted."

Retiring upstairs, Margaret acquiesced to a bowl of gruel to assuage her mother's fears. Lying listlessly in bed, she waited until Mrs. Hale had entered to make final inquiries and bestow a final kiss before locking her door and retiring to her own chamber. The moment the lock clicked shut, the girl sprang from the bed, draping a dressing-gown over her frame. She paced the floor, the rhythmic creak of the wood forcing her to slow her steps, until she collapsed onto the window seat in the recessed bay. Earlier that morning, the gleaming spire of the church tower had promised a sunlit day, but sixteen hours later, she sat shrouded in a grief too heavy for tears, a cold, dull ache draining the vitality from her young heart. The vision of Mr. Henry Lennox's visit, his generous offer, felt like a phantom, severed from reality by the harsh truth: her father's admission of doubt had branded him a schismatic, an outcast, and every consequence spun from that single, blinding fact.

Gazing out at the dark, grey silhouettes of the church towers, square and rigid against the deep, transparent blue of the sky, she felt an infinite distance separating her from the divine. Into those depths she looked, searching forever for a sign, yet finding none. To her, the earth seemed more utterly desolate than if enclosed by an iron dome hiding the Almighty's glory behind it. Those endless spaces of serene stillness mocked her, shutting in the cries of Earth's suffering, allowing them to rise into an infinite splendor only to vanish, lost forever before reaching His throne.

In this somber state, her father entered unnoticed. The moonlight illuminated his daughter in her unusual position. He approached and placed a hand on her shoulder before she realized his presence.

"Margaret, I heard you were awake. I could not resist coming in to pray with you—to recite the Lord's Prayer together. It will benefit us both."

Mr. Hale and Margaret knelt by the window seat; he looked upward, she bowed her head in humble shame. God was present, close around them, attentive to her father's whispered words. Even if her father were a heretic, had not she, in moments of despair mere minutes prior, revealed herself as a skeptic far more profound? She spoke not a word, retreating to bed like a child fleeing from shame after a transgression. If the world presented perplexing puzzles, she wished only to trust and discover the single necessary step for the hour. Yet, the memory of Mr. Lennox haunted her dreams. He climbed a tree of impossible height to retrieve her bonnet, fell, and she struggled in vain to save him, held back by an invisible, powerful hand. Then, the scene shifted; she found herself back in the drawing room at Harley Street, conversing with him as of yore, yet perpetually aware of his death by that terrible fall.

A miserable, unresting night followed, an ill-preparation for the dawn. She awoke with a start, unrefreshed, sensing a reality far more terrible than her feverish visions. The sorrow returned, accompanied by a terrible discord. Where, how far apart, had her father wandered, led by doubts that appeared to her as temptations from the Evil One? She longed to ask, yet dreaded the answer.

The crisp, fine morning brought her mother a peculiar sense of well-being and happiness at breakfast. She chattered on, outlining projects for village kindness, oblivious to her husband's silence and Margaret's monosyllabic responses. Before the meal was cleared, Mr. Hale rose, leaning heavily on the table as if to steady himself.

"I shall not return until evening. I am going to Bracy Common and shall ask Farmer Dobson for provisions. I will be back for tea at seven."

He did not look at either of them, yet Margaret understood the subtext. By seven, the announcement to her mother must be made. Mr. Hale would have delayed it, but Margaret possessed a different constitution. She could not bear the mounting pressure of the day; it was better to confront the worst immediately, lest the day prove too brief to comfort her mother. Standing by the window, contemplating how to begin, she waited for the servants to depart. Her mother ascended the stairs to prepare for school, returning shortly thereafter in a brisker mood than usual.

"Mamma, please accompany me around the garden this morning. Just one turn," Margaret suggested, linking her arm through her mother's waist.

They stepped through the open window. Mrs. Hale spoke, though Margaret could not decipher the words. Her gaze caught a bee entering a deep-bellied flower. *That* would be the signal. As the insect emerged laden with its spoil, she would speak.

"Mamma!" The words burst forth, unbidden and raw. "Papa is leaving Helstone! He is abandoning the Church and relocating to Milton-Northern."

The three hard facts hung suspended in the air.

"What compels you to say this?" Mrs. Hale asked, her voice a mixture of surprise and disbelief. "Who has been spreading such nonsense?"

"Papa himself," Margaret replied, wishing to offer gentleness but lacking the words. They approached a garden bench. Mrs. Hale sat down, tears instantly springing to her eyes.

"I do not understand," she whispered. "Either you have committed a grave error, or I fail to comprehend you."

"No, Mamma, I have made no mistake," Margaret insisted. "Papa has written to the Bishop, stating that his doubts render him unable to conscientiously serve as a priest of the Church of England. Consequently, he must relinquish Helstone. He has also consulted Mr. Bell—Frederick's godfather—and it is arranged that we relocate to Milton-Northern." Mrs. Hale lifted her eyes, the shadow on her countenance confirming she, at least, believed the truth.

"I cannot believe it," Mrs. Hale murmured. "He would surely have told me before reaching this point."

A sudden realization struck Margaret: her mother ought to have been informed sooner. Whatever her faults of discontent, it was a grievous error for her father to leave her to learn of his theological crisis and impending exile from a more informed child. Sitting beside her mother, she cradled the older woman's head against her breast, pressing her soft cheeks tenderly against her face.

"Darling, darling Mamma! We feared causing you pain. Papa felt the burden acutely. You know you are not robust, and the suspense must have been dreadful."

"When did he tell you, Margaret?"

"Only yesterday," Margaret replied, detecting a flicker of jealousy in the question. "Poor Papa!" She attempted to steer her mother's thoughts toward compassion for her father's struggle. Mrs. Hale raised her head.

"What does he mean by 'doubts'?" she asked. "Does he not mean that he believes differently—that he knows better than the Church?"

Margaret shook her head, tears filling her eyes as her mother touched the raw nerve of

"The unwatched garden bough sways,
And tender blossoms flutter down;
The unloved beech gathers browns,
While maples burn themselves away..."

So sang Tennyson, a fitting prelude to the final day in Helstone. The house was a hive of activity, filled with packing cases being carted to the railway station. Even the once-pristine lawn lay trampled and untidy beneath the straw drifting from open windows. Inside, the rooms echoed strangely, bathed in a harsh, uncurtained light that rendered them already alien.

In her dressing-room, Mrs. Hale and Dixon worked in a slow, sentimental rhythm, pausing frequently to coo over forgotten treasures—relics of the children from years past. Their progress was negligible, lost to the weight of sentiment. Downstairs, Margaret stood a figure of calm resolve amidst the chaos, directing the men hired to assist the cook and Charlotte. To the servants, who wept intermittently, she appeared unnervingly composed. They speculated that a young lady raised in London could not possibly care deeply for Helstone, failing to perceive the heavy, suffocating ache within her. They did not see the constant strain required to keep her composure, the mental fortitude needed to prevent a cry of pain. If she faltered, who would lead? Her father was locked in the vestry with the clerk, buried in papers and registers, preparing his own books for transport—a task requiring a solitary focus she dared not disturb. Nor would she yield to strangers, let alone the familiar grief of the cook and Charlotte.

Finally, the packers retired to the kitchen for tea. Margaret moved stiffly from the hall, traversing the bare, echoing drawing-room and stepping out into the twilight of an early November. A film of soft, dull mist obscured the world, lending it a lilac hue as the sun dipped below the horizon. A robin sang somewhere near, perhaps the same bird her father had called his "winter pet," for whom he had crafted a wooden house beside his study window. The leaves outside were gorgeously colored, amber and gold against the low, slanting rays, waiting for the first frost to lay them low. Already, a few drifted down.

She walked along the path beneath the pear-tree wall, a route untouched since she had paced it at Henry Lennox's side. There, amidst the bed of thyme, he had begun to speak of things she must not dwell upon. Her eyes had lingered on a late-blooming rose as she answered him, distracted by the vivid beauty of carrot leaves he mentioned. It had been only a fortnight ago! And now, everything was transformed. Where was he? Likely in London, navigating the same social circles, dining with the Harley Street set or his younger friends. Perhaps even now, as she trudged through the damp, dreary garden, he was enjoying a run in the Temple Gardens, escaping the roar of the city and the hum of thousands of busy lives, freshening himself after a day of legal toil. He had often described these hasty walks, snatched between study and supper, and the thought of them had haunted her imagination.

Here, the garden was silent. The robin had fled into the vast stillness of night. Occasionally, a distant cottage door creaked shut, admitting a weary laborer to his home, sounding impossibly far away. Beyond the garden, a stealthy, crunching sound emerged from the crisp fallen leaves of the forest. It sounded close, menacing.

Margaret recognized it. Earlier that autumn, sitting in her darkened bedroom, she had watched the poachers' silent leaps over the fence, their quick tramps across the moonlit lawn, their vanishing into the black shadows. Then, their wild, adventurous freedom had fascinated her; she had wished them success, harboring no fear. But tonight, a primal terror seized her. She did not know why.

She heard Charlotte closing the windows, fastening them for the night, unaware that someone had ventured into the garden. Suddenly, a heavy crash echoed nearby—a branch, perhaps rotted or snapped by force, falling from the tree line. Panic surged through Margaret. She sprinted, swift as Camilla, to the window and tapped it with a trembling urgency that jolted Charlotte awake.

"Let me in! Let me in! It is only me, Charlotte!"

Her heart hammered until she was safely inside the drawing-room, surrounded by the familiar, hemmed-in walls. She collapsed onto a packing case. The room was cheerless and chill, devoid of fire or light save for Charlotte's long, unsnuffed candle. Charlotte stared at her with surprise, sensing something wrong before seeing it. Margaret rose, feeling the shock more than witnessing it.

"I was afraid you were shutting me out altogether, Charlotte," she said, managing a faint, strained smile. "And then you would never have heard me in the kitchen, where the doors to the lane and churchyard are locked long ago."

"Oh, miss, I would surely have missed you soon. The men would have asked you for directions. And I've put tea in Master's study; it's the most comfortable room, so to speak."

"Thank you, Charlotte. You are a kind girl. I shall be sorry to leave you. Try to write to me, if I can offer any help or advice. I shall always welcome a letter from Helstone. I will send you my address as soon as I know it."

The study was prepared, a fire blazing brightly, unlit candles arranged on the table. Margaret sat on the rug, seeking warmth as the damp evening clung to her dress and exhaustion chilled her bones. She balanced herself by clasping her hands tightly around her knees, her head drooping slightly, an posture of desolation that masked the turmoil within.

But when her father's steps echoed on the gravel outside, she sprang up. Hastily smoothing back her heavy black hair and wiping away tears she had not noticed falling, she went to open the door.

He looked far more depressed than she did. She struggled to find words, forcing herself to speak of topics that might interest him, knowing each effort might be her last.

"Have you had a very long walk today?" she asked, noting his refusal to touch any food.

"As far as Fordham Beeches. I went to see Widow Maltby. She is deeply grieved at not having wished you goodbye. She says little Susan has kept watch down the lane for days past."

"Nay, Margaret, what is the matter, dear?" His voice cracked. The image of the little child watching endlessly, continually disappointed—not from forgetfulness, but from sheer inability to leave home—was the final drop in Margaret's cup. She sobbed, as if her heart would break.

Mr. Hale was distressingly perplexed. He rose and paced the room nervously. Margaret tried to stifle her emotions, waiting until she could speak with firmness. She heard him muttering to himself.

"I cannot bear it. I cannot bear to see the sufferings of others. I think I could endure my own with patience. Oh, is there no going back?"

"No, father," she said, looking straight at him, her voice low and steady. "It is bad enough to believe you in error. It would be infinitely worse to have known you a hypocrite." She dropped her voice at the end, shuddering at the implication of such a thought regarding her father.

"Besides," she continued, "it is simply that I am tired tonight. Do not think I am suffering because of what you have done, dear papa. We cannot talk about it tonight, I believe." Tears and sobs threatened to overwhelm her again. "I had better go and bring Mother this cup of tea. She had hers very early, when I was too busy to attend to her, and I am certain she will need it now."

By railroad time, they were inexorably torn away from the beloved Helstone the next morning. They departed, casting one last glance at the long, low parsonage, half-hidden behind China-roses and pyracanthus, glowing with a homely warmth in the morning sun. Before they could settle into the carriage sent from Southampton, it was gone, returning no more. A sharp sting pierced Margaret's heart as she strained to catch the last glimpse of the old church tower above the forest trees at the bend. Her father remembered, too, and silently yielded the single view to him. She leaned back, shutting her eyes, and tears welled, hanging for an instant on her lashes before rolling slowly down her cheeks, unheeded, onto her dress.

They stopped in London all night at a quiet hotel. Poor Mrs. Hale had wept nearly the whole day, while Dixon expressed her sorrow through extreme irritability, constantly trying to avoid touching the unconscious Mr. Hale, whom she blamed for all their suffering.

As they traveled through the familiar streets, passing houses they had often visited and shops where she had once waited patiently by her aunt's side, they found themselves almost entirely alone. Though the journey had felt interminable, it was the height of the afternoon bustle in London. Long since removed from the capital, Mrs. Hale revived with the curiosity of a child, gazing at the shops and carriages.

"Oh, there's Harrison's, where I bought so many of my wedding things! Dear, how altered! They have immense plate-glass windows, larger than Crawford's in Southampton. And there, I declare... yes, it is—they have just passed Mr. Henry Lennox. Where could he be going, among all these shops?"

Margaret started forward, then quickly recoiled, smiling at herself for the

"Mist clings to the sunlight,
Smoked-out cottages
Encircle me on every side."

The following afternoon, approximately twenty miles north of Milton, the carriage turned onto a minor railway line leading toward Heston. Heston itself presented as a single, elongating strip of buildings stretching parallel to the coast, possessing a distinct character unlike the languid resorts of southern England or the polished promenades of the Continent. To use a Scottish idiom, the town possessed a "purposeful" quality. The country carts were heavier, burdened with iron rather than wood and leather; the people in the streets, though ostensibly on a pleasure trip, carried a distinctly industrious mindset. The colors of the surroundings appeared grayer, more durable, lacking the frivolous gaiety found elsewhere. Notably, there were no smock-frocks among the rural folk; the garments impeded movement and snagged easily on machinery, leading to their abandonment. In southern villages, shopmen often lounged at their doors when not occupied, soaking in the fresh air and surveying the street. In Heston, however, if a shopkeeper had a moment's leisure from a customer, they immediately resumed business within the shop—often, as Margaret suspected unnecessarily, by unrolling and re-rolling ribbons. These distinctions struck her sharply as she and her mother ventured forth the next morning in search of lodging.

Their two nights spent in hotels had exceeded Mr. Hale's financial projections, and they were relieved to secure the first clean, cheerful rooms available. For the first time in many days, Margaret felt a profound sense of rest. This repose carried a dreamlike quality, rendering it all the more perfect and luxurious. From the windows, the distant sea lapped against the sandy shore with a measured rhythm, while nearby, the cries of donkey boys drifted through the air. Unusual scenes unfolded before her like paintings, which she, in her lethargic contentment, felt no urge to scrutinize thoroughly before they faded from view. A stroll down to the beach allowed her to inhale the sea air, soft and warm despite the late November chill; the vast, misty sea-line touched the tender hues of the sky, and the white sail of a distant vessel turned silver in a pale sunbeam. It seemed as though she could lose herself in such a luxury of pensiveness, making the present absolute, refusing to dwell on the past or speculate on the future.

Yet, the future demanded confrontation, however stern or iron-clad its nature. An arrangement was made for the following evening for Margaret and her father to travel to Milton-Northern to locate a dwelling. Mr. Hale had received correspondence from both Mr. Bell and Mr. Thornton and was eager to ascertain critical details regarding his position and prospects there, matters he could only resolve through an interview with the latter gentleman. Margaret understood the necessity of moving, yet she harbored a visceral aversion to a manufacturing town and believed her mother was benefiting from the Heston air. Consequently, she would have willingly postponed the journey.

For several miles prior to reaching Milton, they observed a deep lead-colored cloud hovering over the horizon. It appeared darker due to the stark contrast with the pale gray-blue of the wintry sky; frost had already signaled its arrival in Heston. Closer to the town, the air held a faint, acrid tang of smoke—a loss of natural fragrance rather than a potent odor. They were whisked rapidly along long, straight, seemingly endless streets of uniformly built brick houses. Here and there, a massive, oblong factory with countless windows rose like a hen amidst her chicks, belching thick, "unparliamentary" smoke and serving as the source of the ominous cloud Margaret had mistaken for a sign of rain. As they traversed the broader avenues from the station to the hotel, the carriage halted frequently, blocked by heavily laden waggons. While Margaret had occasionally accompanied her aunt into cities, those heavy, lumbering vehicles there served varied purposes. Here, every wagon and truck transported cotton, whether in raw burlap sacks or woven calico bales. Pedestrians crowded the sidewalks, mostly well-dressed in terms of material, yet exhibiting a slovenly looseness that struck Margaret as distinct from the threadbare, anxious smartness of a similar class in London.

"This is New Street," Mr. Hale remarked. "I believe it is the principal artery of Milton. Mr. Bell often speaks of how the opening of this street from a lane into a major thoroughfare thirty years ago caused his property values to soar. Mr. Thornton's mill must be nearby, as he rents from Mr. Bell, but I suspect he operates from his warehouse."

"Our hotel, Father?" Margaret asked.

"I believe it is near the end of this street. Shall we dine before or after viewing the houses listed in the *Milton Times*?"

"Oh, let us complete our business first."

"As you wish. First, I shall check if there is any note or letter from Mr. Thornton, who promised to inform me of anything concerning these houses, and then we shall depart. We will retain the cab; it is safer than becoming lost and missing the afternoon train."

No letters awaited him. They proceeded to hunt for a residence. Thirty pounds per annum was the limit they could afford; in Hampshire, they might have secured a spacious home with a pleasant garden for this sum. Here, even basic accommodations consisting of two sitting-rooms and four bedrooms seemed elusive. They viewed numerous properties, rejecting each as they inspected them, until they exchanged glances of dismay.

"We must revert to the second option, I suppose. That one in Crampton, isn't it? It had three sitting-rooms. Do you recall how we laughed at the disparity between the three sitting-rooms and the three bedrooms? Yet, I have devised a plan. The ground-floor front room will serve as your study and our dining room—"though poor Papa!"—since you know Mother deserves the cheeriest sitting-room possible. The front room upstairs, despite its atrocious blue-and-pink wallpaper and heavy cornice, offers a genuinely lovely view over the plain, with a great bend of a river or canal below. You may occupy the small bedroom behind it, in the projection above the kitchen, and you and Mother can share the room behind the drawing room, while that closet in the roof will make a splendid dressing area for you."

"But what of Dixon, and the maid we intend to hire?"

"Oh, wait. I am overwhelmed by the brilliance of my managerial genius. Dixon shall have the rear sitting room. I am sure she will appreciate it; she grumbled so about the stairs at Heston. The maid will take the sloping attic above your and Mother's chambers. Will not that suffice?"

"It may do. However, the papers. What terrible taste! And the excessive coloring and heavy cornices!"

"Do not worry, Father! Surely you can persuade the landlord to re-paper one or two rooms—the drawing room and yours, since Mother will frequent them most—and your bookshelves will conceal much of that garish pattern in the dining room."

"You truly believe it is the best choice? If so, I shall proceed immediately to visit this Mr. Donkin, mentioned in the advertisement. I will return you to the hotel where you may order lunch and rest; by the time the meal is ready, I shall be back. I trust I shall succeed in obtaining new wallpapers."

Margaret shared his hope, though she remained silent. She had never encountered a taste that favored ornament, however flawed, to the extent that it surpassed the plainness and simplicity which constitute the true framework of elegance.

Her father escorted her through the hotel entrance, leaving her at the foot of the staircase before departing to the address of the landlord. As Margaret placed her hand upon the door of their prospective sitting-room, she was overtaken by a brisk-paced waiter.

"I beg your pardon, madam. The gentleman departed so swiftly that I lacked time to notify him. Mr. Thornton arrived almost immediately after you left. Understanding, from what the gentleman said, that you would return within an hour, I informed him of this. He returned about five minutes ago and requested to await Mr. Hale. He is currently in your room, madam."

"Thank you. My father returns shortly, and then you may convey the message."

Margaret entered the room, projecting a straight, fearless, and dignified demeanor habitual to her. She felt no unease; she possessed too many social habits for such discomfort. This was a visitor engaged in business with her father, who had previously demonstrated obliging qualities, and thus she intended to accord him a full measure of civility. Mr. Thornton, conversely, was considerably more surprised and discomposed. Anticipating a quiet, middle-aged clergyman, a young lady emerged with frank dignity—a woman of a strikingly different type from those he usually encountered. Her attire was austere: a close straw bonnet of superior material and shape, trimmed with white ribbon; a large Indian shawl draped about her in long, heavy folds, worn with the gravity of an empress's drapery. He failed to recognize her identity, caught by her simple, direct, unashamed gaze, which indicated that his presence was of no consequence to her beautiful countenance and elicited no flush of surprise from her pale, ivory skin. Though he had heard rumors of Mr. Hale's daughter, he had presumed her to be a child.

"Mr. Thornton, I presume!" Margaret said after a half-second's pause, during which his tongue seemed unable to formulate words. "Will you sit down? My father brought me to the threshold not a minute ago, but unfortunately, he was unaware of your presence and has departed on urgent business.

"And it's hame, hame, hame,
Hame fain wad I be."

It required the delicate, floral wallpaper to soften the blow of Milton upon Mrs. Hale, yet even that was insufficient. What was truly lacking could not be supplied. By the time Mrs. Hale arrived, the thick, yellow fogs of November had rolled in, swallowing the view of the valley plain cut off by the river's sweeping bend. To the newcomers, the world had vanished behind a curtain of gray.

Margaret and Dixon had labored for two days, unpacking boxes and arranging furniture, yet the interior remained chaotic. Outside, a dense, choking mist pressed against the glass, invading every open door in swirling, unhealthy wreaths.

"Oh, Margaret!" cried Mrs. Hale, her voice hollow with dread. "Are we to live here?"

Margaret's heart mirrored the desolation in her mother's tone. With effort, she managed a calm reply. "Oh, the fogs in London are sometimes far worse!"

"But then you knew London, and friends waited behind it," Mrs. Hale insisted, gesturing wildly at the gloom. "Here... well! We are utterly desolate. Oh, Dixon, what a place!"

"Indeed, ma'am, I'm sure it won't take long to finish you off, and then I'll know who stays! Miss Hale, that is far too heavy a burden for you to carry alone."

"Not at all, thank you, Dixon," Margaret answered coolly, masking her weariness. "The kindest thing we can do for Mamma is to prepare her room for rest. I shall return shortly with coffee."

Her father shared his wife's gloom, seeking sympathy from Margaret. "Margaret, I do believe this is an unhealthy spot. Imagine if Mother's health—or yours—should fail. I wished I'd chosen a cottage in Wales; this is simply terrible." He moved to the window, staring out at the gray void.

"There was no comfort to offer. They were settled in Milton and must endure the smoke and fog for the foreseeable future; indeed, life itself seemed obscured by a fog of circumstances. Only the day before, Mr. Hale had calculated their costs with alarm. The removal, plus their fortnight at Heston, had drained nearly all his modest savings. Here they were, trapped, and forced to remain.

That night, when Margaret realized the permanence of their situation, she felt a stupor of despair settle over her. The heavy, smoky air clung to her bedroom, a long, narrow projection at the rear of the house. Its window faced a blank wall of another similar projection, barely ten feet away, looming through the fog like an impenetrable barrier to hope. Within the room, chaos reigned. All their energy had been devoted to making Mamma's quarters comfortable. Margaret sat on a packing box, her gaze fixed on a direction card that struck her as unmistakably from Helstone—beautiful, beloved Helstone! She sank into dismal thoughts until she resolved to divert her mind. Suddenly, she recalled a letter from Edith, only half-read amidst the morning's rush.

Edith's letter painted a vibrant picture of their arrival at Corfu. It described the voyage across the Mediterranean, filled with music and dance aboard ship. A new, gaudy life awaited them. Edith detailed her villa perched high on precipitous rocks overlooking the sea, sharing it with Captain Lennox and another newlywed officer. Even in late autumn, their days were spent boating or picnicking outdoors. Edith's existence seemed like a vast vault of blue sky, utterly free from flecks or clouds. Her husband's drills and her duty copying popular tunes for the bandmaster were merely trifling, arduous chores compared to the joy surrounding them. She expressed a warm hope that if the regiment stayed another year, Margaret might join them. Edith also referenced the rainy afternoon a year prior in Harley Street, when Edith refused to wear her new gown to a dull dinner, fearing it would get wet in the carriage—a mishap that led to their first meeting with Captain Lennox.

"Yes," Margaret remembered clearly. Edith and Aunt Shaw had dined there; Margaret had joined later. Vivid memories flooded back: the opulent arrangements, the stately beauty of the furniture, the spacious house, and the tranquil ease of those guests. Everything stood in stark, jarring contrast to the present misery. That smooth sea of former life closed over without a trace, leaving no marker of where they had all been. The habitual dinners, the social calls, the dancing evenings—they all continued endlessly, though Aunt Shaw and Edith were no longer there, and Margaret, of course, was missed even less. She suspected none of that old circle thought of her, except perhaps Henry Lennox. Knowing his capacity to banish unpleasant thoughts, she believed he too would strive to forget her, as he often boasted of doing.

Yet, as she dwelt on what might have been, a bitter gratitude arose. Had she loved Henry as a lover and accepted him, only for her father's fortunes to plummet, Mr. Lennox would have received the news with impatient fury. While it was a humiliation to feel the world degrading her father through its coarse judgments, she could bear it patiently, fortified by knowledge of her father's pure intentions. However, the fact that society viewed her father as degraded would have irked Mr. Lennox deeply. Realizing these possibilities, she grew thankful for what *was*. They were at their lowest point now; they could not descend further. Edith's and Aunt Shaw's shock would have to be met with bravery when their next letters arrived. Rising slowly, Margaret began to undress, savoring the rare luxury of leisure after a frantic day. She fell asleep hoping for some inner or outer brightness, unaware of how long the darkness would persist. The season was ill-suited for health or spirits. Soon, Mamma contracted a severe cold, and Dixon, already unwell, struggled to cope. There was no girl available to assist; all were employed in the factories. Those who applied were harshly rebuked by Dixon for daring to trust themselves to work in a gentleman's household. Thus, they relied on a charwoman in near-constant employment. Margaret longed to summon Charlotte, but Charlotte was superior in skill to what they could afford, and the distance was prohibitive.

Meanwhile, Mr. Hale encountered several pupils recommended by Mr. Bell or influenced directly by Mr. Thornton. These students were largely of an age where many peers remained in school, yet prevailing local wisdom dictated that to forge a capable tradesman, a boy must be seized young and acclimated to the rhythm of the mill, office, or warehouse. Sending them to Scotch universities risked unsettling them for commercial pursuits; Oxford or Cambridge were inaccessible until eighteen. Consequently, most manufacturers placed their sons in positions at fourteen or fifteen, ruthlessly pruning any branches leading toward literature or higher mental cultivation, aiming to channel all vigor into commerce. Yet, there were wiser parents, some with enough self-awareness to recognize their own deficiencies and seek remedies. Indeed, a few, no longer youths but men in the prime of life, possessed the stern wisdom to admit their ignorance and learn belatedly what they should have known early. Mr. Thornton was perhaps the oldest of Mr. Hale's pupils and undoubtedly his favorite. Mr. Hale fell into the habit of quoting Thornton's opinions so frequently and reverently that it became a domestic jest to wonder how much actual teaching occurred within the allotted hour, given the sheer volume of conversation consumed.

Margaret subtly encouraged this lighthearted approach to her father's acquaintance with Mr. Thornton, sensing her mother viewed the new friendship with jealous scrutiny. Previously, when Mr. Hale's time was wholly devoted to his books and parishioners at Helstone, she cared little whether she saw much of him. Now, however, his eager anticipation of renewing contact with Mr. Thornton seemed to wound her, as if he were slighting her company. Mr. Hale's excessive praise carried the usual effect of over-praise on his listeners; they were somewhat rebellious against Aristides being constantly hailed as "Always Just."

After a quiet, pastoral existence in a country parsonage for over twenty years, something dazzling about Mr. Hale's new reality was the energy that effortlessly conquered immense difficulties. The machinery of Milton, the power of its men, impressed him with a sense of grandeur that he yielded to without probing the details of its operation. Margaret, conversely, ventured less among machinery and crowds. She witnessed less of power in its public effects. By chance, she found herself drawn close to a few individuals who, in matters affecting the masses, suffered acutely for the sake of the many. The eternal question haunted her: *Has everything been done to minimize the suffering of these exceptions?* Or, in the triumph of the thronging procession, had the helpless been trampled underfoot, rather than gently lifted aside from the conqueror's path, whom they lacked the power to follow?

It fell to Margaret to search for a servant to aid Dixon, who had initially promised to find exactly the right person for the rough work. Yet Dixon's concept of a "helpful girl" stemmed from her recollections of tidy, older scholars at Helstone School, who were so proud they rarely visited the parsonage on busy days, treating Mrs. Dixon with a mixture of profound respect and genuine terror. Dixon was not unaware of this awed reverence; indeed, it flattered her greatly, akin to Louis XIV being flattered by his courtiers shielding their eyes from his blinding presence. Nothing short of her loyal devotion to Mrs. Hale could have sustained her through the rough, independent spirit

"Let China's earth, enriched with coloured stains,
Pencil'd with gold, and streaked with azure veins,
The grateful flavour of the Indian leaf,
Or Mocha's sunburnt berry glad receive."

The following morning, at an hour unbecoming a gentleman of leisure, Mr. Hale ascended the stairs to the small drawing-room. His movements betrayed a restless examination of the furniture, a physical manifestation of the hesitation that plagued him. He wished to speak, yet the words remained trapped behind a veil of nervous deference until, finally, he broke the silence.

"My dear!" he exclaimed, "I have invited Mr. Thornton to tea tonight."

Mrs. Hale reclined in her easy chair, eyes closed, her face bearing a habitual mask of suffering that seemed etched into her features by recent troubles. Yet, at her husband's announcement, she sprang upright, her composure shattered by querulous indignation.

"Mr. Thornton! And tonight! What possible purpose could drive a man like that to our door?" She waved a hand dismissively. "And consider Dixon! She is occupied with my muslins and laces, and how can she manage without soft water amidst these dreadful eastern gales? I fear Milton is condemned to such storms forever."

"The wind is shifting, my love," Mr. Hale replied, peering out at the smoke curling from the east. Though he lacked knowledge of the compass points, he adjusted his understanding of the weather to suit his immediate needs.

"Don't deceive me!" Mrs. Hale shivered, tightening her shawl around her frail form. "Whether east or west, I suppose he must come."

"Oh, Mother!" Margaret interjected, stepping between them. "That proves you have never truly observed Mr. Thornton. He possesses a spirit that revels in adversity, whether enemies, tempests, or circumstance. The stormier the weather, the more likely he is to appear. Besides, I shall assist Dixon; I am becoming quite proficient at starching. And, Father, he requires no diversion beyond conversation with you." She turned to her father with evident delight. "I am eager to witness the encounter between Pythias and Damon. We met once, but our tongues tripped over each other, leaving us ill-at-ease."

"I doubt you would find him congenial, Margaret," Mr. Hale cautioned gently. "He is no dandy."

Margaret twisted her neck in a gesture of mock disdain. "I hold no special reverence for 'ladies' men,' Father. But Mr. Thornton approaches us as a friend, one who has recognized your worth—"

"The only soul in Milton capable of doing so," Mrs. Hale muttered.

"Then let us extend a warm welcome, perhaps offering coconut cakes. Dixon would be delighted to prepare them, and I shall happily iron your caps, Mother."

Throughout that morning, Margaret harbored a fervent desire to keep Mr. Thornton at bay. She had envisioned a different array of duties: composing a letter to Edith, delving into Dante, or visiting the Higgins household. Instead, she stood before the ironing board, listening to Dixon's grievances, praying that an overflow of sympathy would prevent the laundry maid from recounting her woes to Mrs. Hale. Periodically, she had to summon her resolve regarding her father's esteem for Mr. Thornton to stifle the weary irritation rising within her, a sensation that threatened to trigger the severe headaches plaguing her recently. When she finally seated herself to inform her mother of her transformation from "Peggy the laundry-maid" to "Miss Margaret Hale," she intended it as a jest. However, her mother received the remark with grave seriousness, launching into a tirade about her former days as Miss Beresford and the absurdity of having to scrub floors to accommodate a tradesman.

"Oh, Mother!" Margaret interrupted, rising hastily. "Do not chastise me for this slip of tongue. I derive no pleasure from ironing, nor do I mind the labor, but I am a lady through and through, regardless of whether I am scouring a floor or polishing silver. I am merely fatigued for a moment; soon I shall be ready to resume. As for Mr. Thornton's profession, it is a circumstance beyond his control, poor fellow. His education, alas, offers few alternatives." With a sigh, she retreated to her chamber, unable to endure the atmosphere any longer.

Simultaneously, within the confines of Mr. Thornton's residence, a starkly different tableau unfolded.
