But," she added, "she fears the Milton dissenters may not appreciate them. Has she not formed all her conceptions of Dissenters based solely on the Quakers?"

"If you ever learn or suspect that your mother desires anything, ensure you inform me without delay. I am perpetually afraid she does not communicate her wishes. Please, attend to that young woman Mrs. Thornton named. Had we a competent, efficient housekeeper, Dixon could remain constantly with her, and I assure you we would quickly integrate her into our household if mere care suffices. She has been exceedingly fatigued recently due to the heat and the scarcity of staff. A period of rest will restore her completely—will it not, Margaret?"

"I hope so," Margaret whispered, her tone so laden with sadness that her father noticed instantly. He reached out and pinched her cheek.

"Well, if you appear so pale, I shall have to apply a touch of rouge. Mind yourself, child, or you'll require the doctor next."

Yet, he remained unable to settle. He moved restlessly back and forth on the balls of his feet, peering into the bedroom to confirm whether his wife was still sleeping. Margaret's heart ached at his agitation—the futile struggle to stifle and strangulate the terrible fear lurking in the shadows of his soul.

Finally, he returned, somewhat reassured.

"She is awake now, Margaret. She smiled broadly upon seeing me stand by her side. It was precisely her old smile. She remarked that she feels refreshed and ready for tea. Where is the note for her? She wishes to see it. I will read it aloud while you prepare the tea."

The note revealed a formal invitation from Mrs. Thornton to Mr., Mrs., and Miss Hale for dinner on the twenty-first. To Margaret's surprise, following the somber possibilities she had uncovered earlier that day, an acceptance appeared inevitable. The prospect of her husband and daughter attending had already captured Mrs. Hale's imagination prior to Margaret hearing the details. It seemed an essential diversion from the monotonous confinement of illness. Clinging to the idea with a fretful persistence, Mrs. Hale insisted on going whenever Margaret expressed objection.

"Nay, Margaret," Mr. Hale said anxiously as he prepared to draft the response the following morning. "If she wishes it, I am certain we will both go willingly. She would never express a desire to do so unless she genuinely felt stronger, truly better than we believed, would she not, Margaret?"

"Eh? Margaret?" he queried, his hands moving with nervous jerks. It seemed cruel to deny him the solace he craved. Furthermore, his passionate refusal to acknowledge the existence of fear nearly inspired hope within Margaret herself.

"I do believe she is improved since last night," she admitted. "Her eyes shine brighter, and her complexion is clearer."

"God bless you," Mr. Hale said earnestly. "But is it true? Yesterday was so scorching that everyone felt unwell. It was a terribly unfortunate day for Mr. Donaldson to visit her."

Thus, he departed to his daily duties, which had expanded to include preparing lectures for the working class at a neighboring Lyceum. He had selected *Ecclesiastical Architecture* as his subject, aligning more closely with his personal tastes and knowledge than with the probable interests of his audience or the financial desperation of the institution, which was deeply indebted and eager for any gratis instruction from a learned man like Mr. Hale.

That same night, Mr. Thornton asked his wife, "Well, mother, who has accepted your invitations for the twenty-first?"

"Fanny, where are the notes?" Mrs. Thornton recited the list rapidly. "The Slicksons accept, Collingbrooks accept, Stephenses accept, Browns decline. The Hales—both father and daughter are coming; mother is too ill an invalid to travel. The Macphersons accept, along with Mr. Horsfall and Mr. Young. I considered inviting the Porters, as the Browns cannot come."

"Very well," Mr. Thornton said. "Do you realize, however, that I am genuinely afraid Mrs. Hale is far from well, judging by what Dr. Donaldson told me?"

"It is peculiar of them to accept a dinner invitation if she is so severely ill," Fanny observed.

"I did not say 'severely ill,'" her brother replied sharply. "I merely said 'far from well.' They may not comprehend the gravity of the situation." Suddenly, he recalled that, based on Dr. Donaldson's account, Margaret, at least, must be fully aware of the precise nature of her condition.

"Most likely they are quite conscious of what I said yesterday, John—of the immense social advantage it would offer Mr. Hale to be introduced to individuals like the Stephenses and the Collingbrooks."

"I am certain that motive would not sway them. No! I believe I understand the dynamic."

"John!" Fanny laughed in her petite, weak, nervous fashion. "How you profess to understand these Hales, and yet how you never concede that we can perceive anything about them! Are they truly so distinct from the ordinary people one encounters?"

She did not intend to irritate him; yet, if she had aimed to do so, she could scarcely have succeeded more thoroughly. He bristled in silence, refusing to dignify her question with a reply.

"They do not seem to me extraordinary," Mrs. Thornton remarked. "He appears to be a decent, albeit somewhat simple, gentleman—perhaps too unsophisticated for commerce, hence his decision to become a clergyman first and now a teacher. She possesses a certain refinement, heightened by her infirmity. As for the girl... she is the sole individual who perplexes me when I reflect upon her, though I seldom do. She seems to harbor a grandiose notion of putting on airs, and I cannot fathom why. I could almost imagine she considers herself superior to her company, at times. And yet, they are not wealthy; from everything I gather, they have never possessed significant means."

"And she lacks accomplishments, mother," Fanny interjected. "She cannot play."

"Proceed, Fanny. What further qualities does she lack to meet your standards?"

"Nay, John!" Mrs. Thornton said, softening. "Fanny's remark caused no offense. I myself heard Miss Hale admit her inability to play. If you would leave us alone, we might perhaps grow fond of her and recognize her virtues."

"I am certain I never could!" murmured Fanny, feeling shielded by her mother. Mr. Thornton heard the words but chose not to respond. He walked up and down the dining room, desiring that his mother order candles so he might begin reading or writing, thereby halting the conversation. Yet, he never intervened in the minor domestic regulations Mrs. Thornton adhered to, born of her lifelong habits of economy.

"Mother," he stopped and spoke with brave sincerity, "I wish you would like Miss Hale."

"Why," she asked, startled by his earnest, tender demeanor, "do you suppose you intend to marry her? A girl without a

Mrs. Hale watched Margaret with a mixture of amusement and deep, childish wonder regarding the upcoming dinner party at Mr. Thornton's. Like a child anticipating a treat, she sought to have every detail described before the event, though her curiosity was tinged with the vanity of a former beauty who had known better days. Now, confined by ill health, she found solace in imagining her daughter transformed from the modest clergyman's wife into a lady of society. She fretted over the selection of garments, oscillating between a white silk gown that might have yellowed, a pink gauze, or a green silk, feeling the "embarrassment of riches" in her uncertainty.

Margaret, usually composed, became animated by her mother's nervous inquiries. She offered to try on each dress, though she suppressed her usual playful tricks, sensing that her mother viewed them as interruptions to this serious business. The conversation shifted abruptly when Margaret mentioned her engagement to Bessy Higgins, who reacted with startling intensity. To Bessy, the prospect of Margaret dining at Marlborough Mills—a home frequented by the Mayor and Members of Parliament—was shocking. With her limited understanding of wealth, measured by yards of cloth, Bessy feared Margaret would be "looked down upon" in her print gown.

Yet, Margaret's spirit lifted at the challenge. She resolved to wear her finest white silk, a gown once worn for a cousin's wedding, confident in her ability to uphold the honor of the occasion. As they spoke, however, the shadows of the outside world crept into the room. Bessy grew feverish, her vision clouded by distressing dreams of Margaret in shining raiment, a vision that haunted her afflicted mind. She clung to the idea that these dreams foretold reality, seeking physical contact with Margaret as if to anchor herself against the chaos of her imagination.

Despite Bessy's agitation, the gravity of the situation deepened when Nicholas Higgins returned. His demeanor was sharp, his words heavy with the bitterness of the strike. He spoke of the mill owners' contracts, predicting that within weeks, the masters would capitulate to secure their remaining orders. He boasted of a march of seven hundred strikers, fueled by the promise of a five percent increase, convinced of their inevitable victory. His confidence, however, clashed violently with the grim reality revealed moments later.

In the adjacent room, the atmosphere was suffocating. John Boucher, a neighbor with a large family, stood pale and gaunt, recounting the horrors of starvation. His wife, unable to endure the sight of their children starving, had fallen into a wasting sickness, sinking away despite adequate food. Boucher, driven by desperation and a twisted logic, declared he would return to work at the old, low wages if his wife died before the strike yielded results. He spoke of hating the Union leaders if they failed, his voice trembling with a rage born of helpless love.

Nicholas Higgins, hearing this, halted in his tracks. The man's face, white with terror and exhaustion, forced Margaret to weep. Higgins, realizing the fatal flaw in his argument, turned to Boucher with a sudden humility. "So help me God!" he cried, acknowledging that if his advice led to ruin, it was not his fault, but the sin of those who left him ignorant. He pleaded for faith in the Union, insisting that patience would eventually force the masters to bend.

Silent witnesses to this tragedy, Margaret and Bessy shared a look of profound sorrow. When Boucher's wife finally succumbed, leaving her husband and children destitute, Margaret could do nothing but offer what little money she possessed. She handed cash to Bessy, instructing her to provide food for Boucher's children without revealing the source, fearing judgment. Bessy accepted the gift, her heart warming despite the crushing weight of the news. As the day wore on, Bessy grew increasingly faint, her dreams and waking terrors blending into a single, terrifying haze. Margaret, watching her friend fade, realized that the "gleam of brightness" inside the Hale household was necessary precisely because the world outside was so thoroughly engulfed in gloom. The stark contrast between the warm, hopeful interior and the brutal, hungry exterior served only to heighten the agony of the present moment, leaving Margaret to wonder how long she could bear the silence of those who suffered most.

"Old and young, boy, let 'em all eat, I have it;
Let 'em have ten tire of teeth a-piece, I care not."

Margaret returned home with a mind so burdened by the sights and sorrows she had witnessed that she struggled merely to summon the energy required for her household duties. Her mother, confined to the house and accustomed to receiving news from Margaret's briefest walks, expected a cheerful report.

"And can your factory friend come on Thursday to see you dressed?"

"She was so ill I never thought of asking her," Margaret murmured, her voice heavy with pity.

"My dear!" cried Mrs. Hale, a flicker of jealous concern crossing her features at the thought of another suffering invalid. "But it must be terribly sad to be ill in those little back streets. It is hard enough here already. What aid could you offer her, Margaret? Mr. Thornton has sent me some of his vintage port since you left. Do you think a bottle would help her?"

"No, Mother!" Margaret protested gently. "They do not appear to be destitute; they do not speak of poverty. Moreover, Bessy suffers from consumption—wine would not serve her. Perhaps I might bring her a jar of preserves made from our beloved Helstone fruits."

"But there is another family," Margaret continued, her composure breaking as she recounted the grim realities of Higgins's cottage, describing the despair and the stark conditions she had observed. "Oh, Mamma, Mamma! How am I to adorn myself in finery and attend these fashionable gatherings after witnessing such profound misery today?"

Her distress proved too much for Mrs. Hale. The sight of her daughter's agitation, coupled with the knowledge of the suffering outside, stirred a restless irritation within her. She immediately dispatched Margaret to pack a basket in the drawing-room, instructing it to be sent to the afflicted families instantly. She was nearly incensed when Margaret remarked that it mattered little if the gift arrived until morning, for Mrs. Hale knew Higgins had already secured immediate provisions and had left funds with Bessy. To suggest such indifference was, in her view, unforgivable. She hovered over the task until the basket was safely delivered, then sat down, reflecting with troubled conscience.

"After all," she mused aloud, "we may have erred. It was only on Mr. Thornton's last visit that he remarked those who prolong the struggle by aiding the strikers are not true friends. And this Boucher was a striker, was he not?"

Upon Mr. Hale's ascent from a lesson with his son, the matter was brought to his attention. Margaret, in her agitated state, did not dwell on the broader implications of charity versus principle regarding the strike itself. Mr. Hale, however, approached the dilemma with the deliberation of a judge. He recalled the stark arguments Mr. Thornton had voiced moments prior, yet found himself making a reluctant compromise. While acknowledging the validity of Thornton's stance—that a prolonged strike might necessitate hiring distant labor or lead to mechanization, rendering local aid counterproductive—he admitted that in this specific instance, refusing assistance to Boucher seemed morally untenable. He resolved to visit the man personally the following morning to ascertain his needs.

True to his word, Mr. Hale visited the next day. Though Boucher was absent, he held a lengthy and compassionate conversation with his wife. He promised to secure an Infirmary order for her and, observing the ample provisions Mrs. Hale had generously supplied—which the children, acting as masters in their father's absence, had consumed somewhat freely—returned with a far more reassuring account than Margaret had dared to anticipate. Indeed, Margaret's fearful description the previous night had primed her father's imagination to envision a catastrophe; consequently, his reaction painted a picture of comfort that exceeded the grim reality.

"I shall return soon to see the man himself," Mr. Hale declared. "I am still learning to measure these homes against the cottages of Helstone. Here, I see furnishings and foods that our laborers could never dream of acquiring, yet with their wages halted, the pawnshop remains their sole recourse. One must cultivate a new standard of judgment when living in Milton."

Meanwhile, Besser showed slight improvement, though her weakness rendered her oblivious to Margaret's earlier feverish longing to see her dressed. As Margaret prepared for the evening, she could not help but contrast this somber occasion with the joyous, frivolous preparations of the past year, when she and Edith had delighted in dressing for mere amusement. Now, the act of adorning herself offered her only solace in the knowledge that her mother would derive pleasure from viewing her. When Dixon opened the door to the drawing-room, inviting admiration, Margaret blushed.

"Miss Hale looks well, ma'am," Dixon observed approvingly. "Mrs. Shaw's coral could not have been placed more successfully. It provides the perfect accent of color. Without it, Miss Margaret, you would be far too pale."

Margaret's hair, too dense for plaits, was wound in intricate twists and massive coils, forming a crown-like circle around her head before gathering into a large spiral knot at the nape. Two substantial coral pins, resembling arrows, anchored the mass, while matching strings looped her white silk sleeves. Heavy coral beads rested upon her neck, framing the curve of her throat.

"Oh, Margaret!" her mother exclaimed, seizing the moment for maternal vanity. "How I long to accompany you to one of the old Barrington assemblies, taking you as Lady Beresford once took me!"

Margaret kissed her mother, suppressing a smile despite her low spirits. "I would rather remain at home with you, Mother."

"Nonsense, darling! Do observe the dinner carefully. I am eager to learn how they manage affairs in Milton, particularly the second course. Note what they serve instead of game."

Had Mrs. Hale witnessed the table, she might have been astonished by its sumptuousness. Yet to Margaret, with her refined London sensibilities, the array of delicacies seemed excessive, the presentation lacking the tight, elegant restraint she preferred. This abundance, however, reflected Mrs. Thornton's rigid code of hospitality: every guest must have access to every dainty, regardless of inclination. Careful in her daily habits, Mrs. Thornton prided herself on setting feasts before those who cared for them. Her son, John, shared this sentiment. Though he denied himself the trivial expense of unnecessary items and regretted sending invitations, he nonetheless welcomed the opportunity to witness the old magnificence of preparation.

Margaret and her father arrived punctually. The drawing-room was empty save for Mrs. Thornton and Fanny. The space blazed with yellow silk damask and flowered carpets, adorned with ornaments until the eye grew weary, presenting a jarring contrast to the bare, utilitarian exterior overlooking the vast mill yard, where wide gates stood open for carriages. High on the left, the mill loomed, casting a shadow that darkened the summer evening prematurely.

"My son was detained by business until the last moment," Mrs. Thornton explained to Mr. Hale. "He will be here directly. Will you take a seat?"

Standing by a window, Mr. Hale turned away. "Do you not find proximity to the mill somewhat disagreeable at times?"

Mrs. Thornton drew herself erect. "Never. I have not grown so refined as to wish to forget the source of my son's wealth and power. Furthermore, there is no other factory in Milton. A single room measures two hundred and twenty square yards."

"I meant the smoke and noise—the constant flow of workers entering and leaving might prove irritating."

"I concur, Mr. Hale!" Fanny interjected. "There is a perpetual odor of steam and oil, and the noise is utterly deafening."

"I have heard noises termed 'music' far louder," Mr. Hale countered calmly. "The engine-house is at the street end; we hear it only in summer when windows are open. As for the hum of the workers, it troubles me no more than the buzzing of a bee-hive. If I reflect upon it, I associate it solely with my son, feeling how everything flows through him, directed by his head. Currently, there are no sounds from the mill; the hands have proven ungrateful enough to strike, as you may have heard. However, the business I discussed upon your arrival concerns the steps he intends to take to teach them their proper station." Her usually stern countenance deepened into dark anger as she spoke. The expression did not fade when Mr. Thornton entered. She perceived instantly the heavy burden of care and anxiety weighing upon him, a load he could not shed, even as he greeted his guests with a facade of cheer. He extended his hand to Margaret. They had never touched hands before, yet she remained unaware. He inquired after Mrs. Hale and heard Mr. Hale's optimistic report. Glancing at Margaret to gauge her agreement with her father, he saw no dissenting shadow upon her face. In that glance, struck anew by her great beauty, he noted how becoming her elegance was to her noble figure and serene countenance. It seemed she ought to wear such attire always.

While Margaret conversed with Fanny, he observed his sister's restless movements, constantly adjusting her gown, her wandering eyes scanning the room without focus. He contrasted this with Margaret's large, soft eyes, fixed steadily on a single point, radiating a gentle influence of repose. Her lips, curved in attentive listening, framed by the sweep of her glossy raven hair down to her smooth shoulders, and her round, white arms resting motionless in a poised attitude, captivated him. Mr. Thornton sighed as he absorbed this vision, then turned his back

*"On earth is known to none
The smile that is not sister to a tear."*

Margaret and her father walked home under a canopy of stars. The air was crisp and invigorating, carrying the scent of summer evenings, and Margaret, dressed in a gown of white silk that fluttered softly against her legs, felt a lightness of spirit. Beside her, Mr. Hale moved with a gentle step, his hand occasionally resting on her shoulder.

"I believe Mr. Thornton is troubled," Margaret observed, looking ahead. "He seemed quite uneasy tonight."

"He was thoughtful, perhaps," Mr. Hale replied, his tone measured. "Yet, when addressing the others, he maintained his customary composure. That stoicism, however, often masks deeper currents within men of his nature."

"His countenance, Father, speaks of anxiety," Margaret persisted. "And I think it right that it does. If I were in his place, knowing the simmering resentment of his workers—the way they view him as a 'hard man,' devoid of feeling, rigid in his adherence to principle—I would not expect calm. I recall the words of Mr. Boucher, half-mad with grief and injustice. To imagine Mr. Thornton remaining so serene amidst such storm is difficult for me to accept."

"My dear girl, you judge him by the surface of the storm, whereas I see the rock beneath. Mr. Thornton is a man of immense pride, Margaret, a quality that shields his vulnerabilities. He is not easily swayed from his judgments, nor does he permit his emotions to dictate his actions openly. This is a trait I have always admired in him. He possesses a strength of character forged by a practical life, a necessity for constant judgment and self-restraint from youth. While he lacks the breadth of historical knowledge that comes from leisure, he is acutely aware of this deficit, and that awareness alone proves a fertile mind. You are merely seeing the shield, not the warrior within."

"But I am not entirely convinced," Margaret murmured, her brow furrowing slightly. "To me, he remains a stranger, a figure of great intellect but perhaps lacking warmth. Yet, I found myself drawn to the conversation of the gentlemen tonight, despite my confusion. And I was truly discomfited when Miss Thornton removed me from the circle, fearing I would be isolated among so many men. The company of the ladies, I confess, was tedious—though they did possess a peculiar wit. They played a game of sorts, weaving sentences around nouns that signified wealth: *housekeepers*, *under-gardeners*, *glass*, *lace*. It was a strange exercise, reminding me of our childhood games, yet it highlighted the gulf between their world and ours."

"And yet, you felt a kinship?" Mr. Hale asked gently.

"Yes," she admitted, a soft smile touching her lips. "A sense of goodwill. I know he is kind in his own way, and slowly, I begin to appreciate that kindness."

"Then you shall grow to admire him, I am sure." Mr. Hale smiled, a quiet, paternal expression. "Though, I must say, I was mistaken in my assessment of your appearance tonight. You carried yourself with the grace of a lady."

Margaret laughed, a bright, clear sound. "Did you really think so? I felt like a fraud, Father. Sitting there with my idle hands, draped in silk, while I thought of the hard labor the servants performed today. They treated me as a princess, and I felt guilty for the moment."

"You were a lady, my dear," Mr. Hale said warmly. "And they served you as such."

Their conversation dissolved as Dixon opened the front door, her face pale with terror.

"Oh, Master Hale! Oh, Miss Margaret!" Dixon gasped, clutching her apron. "Thank God you are home! Dr. Donaldson is here. The charwoman has returned, but the woman next door sent for him immediately. The servant who usually watches is gone. She... she was dying, sir! I thought she'd pass away an hour ago!"

Mr. Hale stepped forward, steadying himself, his face draining of color. He looked at Margaret, searching her eyes for any sign of dread, but found instead a mixture of shock and profound sorrow. She understood more than he did, yet she stood frozen, her expression one of awed, helpless apprehension.

"Oh! I should not have left her!" Margaret cried, supporting her father as he stumbled toward the stairs. "Wicked daughter that I am!"

Dr. Donaldson awaited them on the landing. "She is recovering now," he whispered urgently. "The opiate has worked. The spasms were severe, naturally frightening the maid, but she will rally."

"This time!" Mr. Hale breathed, his vision blurring, his limbs trembling as if age had suddenly overtaken him. "Let me go to her!"

Dr. Donaldson took hold of his arm, guiding him into the bedroom. Margaret followed closely. Lying in the bed was her mother, her face etched with an unmistakable pallor. Though she slept, the silence between her and death was thin. It was evident that the Reaper was waiting, poised to claim her again. Mr. Hale stared at her for a long, agonizing minute, unable to speak. Then, a shiver racked his frame, and he turned away from the doctor, groping blindly for the door. The candles, hastily lit in the panic, burned brightly, yet he could not find the handle. He staggered into the drawing-room, collapsing onto a chair.

"Speak to him, Miss Hale," Dr. Donaldson urged, his voice firm. "We must rouse him."

"Papa!" Margaret called out, her voice breaking with raw emotion. "Papa! Speak to me!"

Slowly, the fog in his eyes cleared. With a Herculean effort, he lifted his gaze. "Margaret," he croaked, tears streaming down his cheeks. "Did you know of this? Oh, it was cruel of you!"

"No, sir! It was not cruel!" Dr. Donaldson interjected swiftly. "Miss Hale acted under my instructions. There may have been an error in judgment, but it was not cruelty. Your wife will be altered tomorrow, I trust. The spasms, as I predicted, required sedation. She will sleep deeply, and that look of terror will fade."

"But not the illness?" Mr. Hale whispered, his voice barely audible.

Dr. Donaldson glanced at Margaret. Her bowed head, her silent plea, indicated that she wished the full truth spoken. "Not the disease," the doctor said solemnly. "Our skill, however vaunted, cannot cure the malady. We can only delay its march and alleviate the suffering. Be a man, sir—a Christian. Have faith in the immortality of the soul, which no earthly pain or sickness can touch."

The response Mr. Hale offered was a strangled sob, followed by deep, manly weeping that resonated through the silent house like a heavy drumbeat.

Margaret knelt beside him, her hands clasping his, her tears falling freely. Time seemed to stretch and contract in equal measure. Finally, Mr. Hale, the first to regain his composure, broke the silence. "What must we do? Tell us both. Margaret is my staff, my right hand."

Dr. Donaldson laid out his plan with clear, sensible authority. Fear for the night was over; peace for the morrow, and likely for many days to follow, was assured. However, there was no promise of ultimate recovery. He advised Mr. Hale to retire, suggesting that only one guard was needed for the night, leaving ample time for rest. He promised to return early in the morning. With a kind handshake, he departed.

Words were scarce between the parents; exhaustion from the terror left them with little energy for discourse, save for the necessary decisions. Mr. Hale resolved to sit up all night, but Margaret, with a gentle firmness, prevailed upon him to lie down on the drawing-room sofa. Dixon, stubborn and blunt, refused to go to bed, and as for Margaret, it was impossible for her to abandon her mother. Thus, Dixon sat, staring, blinking, drooping, and jerking awake, until eventually, she succumbed to a heavy snore.

Margaret, having discarded her gown with impatient disgust, wrapped herself in her dressing-gown. Sleep eluded her completely. Every sensation seemed amplified, every nerve ending hyper-vigilant. For over two hours, she heard her father's restless movements in the adjacent room. He drifted constantly to her mother's door, pausing to listen intently, prompting her to open it and report the stillness of the vigil, answering the broken questions his parched lips could scarcely form. Eventually, he too fell into a fitful slumber, and the house sank into a profound silence.

Sitting behind the curtain, Margaret thought. In the vast expanse of time and space, the concerns of recent days faded. Less than thirty-six hours prior, her heart had ached for Bessy Higgins and Boucher; now, those sorrows seemed like a dream from a forgotten life. Everything that had happened outside the house felt severed from her mother, rendered unreal by distance. Even Harley Street, once vibrant with the news of home, seemed distant. Helstone itself receded into a dim, gray past. The monotonous winter and spring, previously uneventful, now held a strange association with what she cherished most. She longed to grasp the tails of that departing time, praying it would return, offering back what she had

A Blow and Its Consequences.

"But work grew scarce, while bread grew dear,
And wages lessened, too;
For Irish hordes were bidders here,
Our half-paid work to do."
—Corn Law Rhymes.

Margaret was ushered into the drawing-room, where the atmosphere had settled into a grim, stagnant normalcy. The windows stood ajar against the stifling heat, the Venetian blinds casting a gray, distorted light from the pavement below. This unnatural illumination, mingled with the greenish gloom filtering through the upper panes, rendered even Margaret's complexion ghostly and wan in the mirrors. She sat in silence, waiting. No one approached. From afar, the wind occasionally seemed to carry the indistinct hum of a multitude closer, only to die away into a profound, suffocating stillness.

Finally, Fanny entered.

"Mamma will be with you shortly, Miss Hale," Fanny said, her voice trembling slightly. "She wanted me to apologize for the delay. You likely know that my brother has hired hands from Ireland, and it has enraged the Milton workers to the point of madness. They claim he has no right to seek labor elsewhere, and in their fury, they've threatened these poor, starving newcomers so severely that we dare not let them out. You'll find them huddled in the top room of the mill to keep them safe from the brutes who refuse to work for him or let them live. Mother is arranging their food, and John is trying to calm them, as some of the women are already crying to return. Ah! Here comes Mamma!"

Mrs. Thornton appeared, her face a mask of black, stern severity. To Margaret, the sight confirmed that she had arrived at a perilous moment. Yet, she had come solely at her mother's request to inquire about necessities for the progression of the mistress's illness. Mrs. Thornton's brow furrowed deeply, her lips tightening as Margaret gently described her mother's restlessness and Dr. Donaldson's recommendation for a water-bed. Mrs. Thornton offered no immediate response. Then, abruptly, she sprang up, her composure shattering.

"They're at the gates! Call John, Fanny! Call him from the mill immediately! They're battering at the gates! They'll break them down!"

Simultaneously, the gathering tramp she had ignored while listening to Margaret's words crashed against the wooden barrier outside. An angry din of voices roared behind the fence, shaking it as if invisible maddened giants used their bodies as battering rams. They pulled back only to surge forward again with renewed, united impetus, causing the strong gates to vibrate violently, like reeds before a gale.

Women clustered around the windows, transfixed by the terrifying spectacle. Mrs. Thornton, the servants, and Margaret stood frozen. Fanny had scrambled upstairs in a panic, returning moments later in hysterical sobs, collapsing onto the sofa. Mrs. Thornton scanned the room for her son, who remained in the mill. He emerged, looked up at the pale, terrified cluster of faces, and offered a smile of grim courage before locking the factory door. He then signaled a servant to unlock it, allowing Fanny—who had bolted the door in her flight—to pass.

As Mrs. Thornton stepped forward to enter the factory, the sound of her son's familiar, commanding voice seemed to strike the infuriated crowd like a blow of blood. Until now, they had been voiceless, gasping for breath as they struggled to breach the gates. Hearing him speak, however, they erupted into a fierce, unearthly groan that turned Mrs. Thornton's face ashen with fear.

John Thornton entered, flushed but his eyes gleaming with a proud, defiant light. To Margaret, who had always feared she lacked courage, this moment transformed everything. She felt not fear, but an intense, painful sympathy for the crisis unfolding.

"I'm sorry, Miss Hale," John said, stepping forward frankly. "You have visited us at this unfortunate hour, and I fear you are now involved in whatever risk we must bear. Mother! Had you not better retire to the back rooms? I suspect they may have broken through from Pinner's Lane into the stable yard. If not, you will be safer there. Go, Jane!"

Jane obeyed, followed closely by the others.

"I stay here!" Mrs. Thornton declared firmly. "Where you stand, I remain." Indeed, retreat offered no safety; the crowd had surrounded the outbuildings at the rear, unleashing a dreadful, threatening roar from behind. The servants fled to the garrets, their cries echoing in the panic. John smiled with scorn at their flight. He glanced at Margaret, who stood solitary by the window nearest the factory. Her eyes glinted, her cheeks flushed with a deep, fiery color. Sensing his gaze, she turned to him with a question that had been forming in her mind:

"Where are the poor imported workpeople? Are they in the factory?"

"Yes," he replied. "I left them cowering in a small room at the top of the back stairs, ordering them to flee down there if the mill doors were attacked. But it is not them they seek; it is me."

"When can the soldiers arrive?" his mother asked, her voice low but unsteady.

John consulted his watch with the same steady composure he displayed in all matters. He calculated briefly.

"If Williams departed the moment I told him and avoids the throng, it will be twenty minutes yet."

"Twenty minutes!" Mrs. Thornton gasped, terror finally cracking her voice.

"Close the windows instantly, Mother! These gates cannot withstand another shock. Close that window, Miss Hale."

Margaret shut her window and moved to support Mrs. Thornton's trembling hand. For several minutes, the unseen street fell silent. Mrs. Thornton stared wildly at her son's countenance, searching for an interpretation of the sudden quiet. His face was carved from rigid lines of contemptuous defiance; neither hope nor fear could be read there.

Fanny sat up. "Are they gone?" she whispered.

"Gone!" John replied. "Listen!"

They heard it: the single, straining breath, the slow creak of yielding wood, the wrench of iron, and the mighty crash of the ponderous gates falling. Fanny staggered, took a step toward her mother, and collapsed into a fainting fit. With a strength born of sheer will, Mrs. Thornton lifted her and carried her away.

"Thank God!" John murmured as he watched her exit. "Had you not better go upstairs, Miss Hale?"

Margaret's lips formed a "No," but he could not hear her. Beneath the walls, the tramp of innumerable footsteps thundered, accompanied by a fierce, low growl of angry voices that held a ferocious, satisfied murmur.

"Never mind!" John said, attempting to reassure her. "I am sorry you were trapped in this alarm, but it cannot last long. A few more minutes, and the soldiers will be here."

"Oh, God!" Margaret cried suddenly. "There is Boucher! I know that face, though he is livid with rage. Look! Look!"

"Who is Boucher?" John asked coolly, moving to the window to identify the man.

As soon as they spotted John, the crowd let out a yell that was not human—it was the demoniac hunger of a wild beast denied its prey. John drew back momentarily, dismayed by the intensity of hatred radiating from them.

"Let them yell!" he shouted. "In five minutes more... I only hope my poor Irishmen are not terrified out of their wits by such fiendlike noises. Hold your courage for five minutes, Miss Hale."

"Don't be afraid for me," she said hastily. "But what will happen in five minutes? Can you do nothing to soothese poor creatures? It is awful to witness."

"The soldiers will be here directly, and that will bring them to reason."

"To reason!" Margaret repeated quickly. "What sort of reason?"

"The only reason that deals with men who turn themselves into wild beasts. By heaven! They are rushing toward the mill door!"

"Mr. Thornton," Margaret said, her whole body shaking with passion, "go down this instant, if you are not a coward. Go down and face them like a man. Save these poor strangers whom you have brought here. Speak to your workmen as if they were human beings. Speak kindly. Do not let the soldiers come in and butcher these poor, mad creatures. I see one there who is insane. If you possess any nobility, go out and speak to them, man to man!"

He turned to look at her as she spoke. A dark cloud passed over his face, and he set his teeth tight.

"I will go. Perhaps I may ask you to accompany me downstairs and bar the door behind me; my mother and sister require that protection."

"Oh! Mr. Thornton! I do not know—I may be wrong—only—"

But he was already gone. Downstairs in the hall, he unbolted the front door. All she could do was follow quickly, fasten it behind him, and climb the stairs again with a sick, dizzy heart. Once more, she positioned herself by the farthest window. Below, on the steps, he stood visible to the thousand angry eyes of the crowd, yet she could see or hear nothing but the savage satisfaction of their rolling, angry murmur. She flung the window wide open. Many in the crowd were merely boys—cruel and thoughtless, their cruelty stemming from their lack of thought. Others were men, gaunt as wolves,

"Which when his mother saw, she in her mind
Was troubled sore, he wist well what to ween."

Scarcely had Margaret departed before Mr. Thornton burst into the dining-room, his countenance aflame with agitation. "I could not arrive sooner; the superintendent detained me!" He swept the room with a searching gaze, his eyes finally locking onto his mother with an intensity that bordered on fierceness. "Where is she?"

His mother, quietly rearranging the displaced chairs, offered no immediate answer. "Where is Miss Hale?" he repeated, his tone sharpening.

"Gone home," she replied, her voice curt.

"Gone home!" The exclamation rang out, filled with disbelief.

"Yes," she continued, dismissing the gravity of the moment. "She was considerably improved. I do not believe it was any serious injury at all; some simply faint at the slightest shock."

"I am distressed that she has returned home," Mr. Thornton muttered, pacing the floor with restless energy. "She was scarcely fit for travel."

"She insisted on it," Mrs. Thornton stated firmly. "And Mr. Lowe confirmed her condition. I fetched him personally."

"Thank you, Mother." He paused, extending his hand in a gesture of gratitude, only to withdraw it as she failed to register the motion. "What disposition have you made of the Irish workers?"

"Dispatched them to the Dragon Inn for a substantial meal, though they are pitiable wretches," she answered. "Fortunately, I encountered Father Grady and engaged him to address them, preventing a mass departure." Her attention shifted back to the crisis. "How did Miss Hale manage to reach home? I am certain she could not have walked."

"A carriage was procured," Mrs. Thornton noted, her composure returning. "Every arrangement was handled with propriety, including the payment. Let us discuss matters less disruptive."

"I confess I am unsure where I would have stood without her intervention."

Mrs. Thornton's expression hardened. "Have you become so inept that you require a young lady for protection?"

A flush of indignation colored Mr. Thornton's cheeks. "Few women would have accepted the blows intended for me with such fortitude—" he added, emphasizing the sincerity behind her actions, "and certainly not with the genuine goodwill displayed."

"A girl under the influence of affection performs remarkable feats," Mrs. Thornton observed, her tone dismissive.

"Mother!" He stepped forward, halting abruptly as he struggled to contain a surge of emotion. He breathed heavily, his frame taut, his eyes burning with a mixture of triumph, rage, pride, and bewildered uncertainty. The raw power of these conflicting feelings unsettled his mother. Unable to decipher the storm within him, she moved to the sideboard, retrieving a duster from a drawer. Noticing a stray drop of eau de Cologne on the polished sofa arm, she instinctively reached for it, turning her back to her son long past necessity. When she finally spoke, her voice sounded strained and artificial.

"You have addressed the matter of the rioters, I presume? Do you anticipate further violence? Where were the constables when needed most?"

"On the contrary," he replied, regaining his composure. "I witnessed several officers struggling effectively near the gate, and more arrived precisely as the yard cleared. I could have detained several individuals if I had maintained my focus. However, identification will pose no difficulty; numerous witnesses remain."

"But will they not return tonight?"

"I intend to arrange a robust guard for the premises. I have agreed to meet Captain Hanbury at the station in half an hour."

"You ought to have some tea first."

"Tea? Prudence dictates it. It is half-past six, and I may be absent for some duration. Do not wait up for me, Mother."

"Do you expect me to retire before ensuring your safety?"

"Well, perhaps not." He hesitated. "However, if time permits, I shall visit Crampton after settling affairs with the police and Hamper and Clarkson." Their gazes met, holding an unspoken weight for a minute before she questioned him.

"Why the detour to Crampton?"

"To inquire after Miss Hale."

"I shall dispatch Williams. He must provide the water-bed she requested and ascertain her well-being."

"I must attend to it myself."

"Not solely to inquire on her health?"

"No, not merely that. I wish to express my gratitude for standing between me and the mob."

"What compelled you to descend at all? You placed yourself directly in harm's way!"

He cast a sharp glance at her, sensing she remained ignorant of the private exchange in the drawing-room, and countered with another query. "Will you be frightened to remain alone until I secure the police? Or shall I send Williams immediately, ensuring they arrive before tea concludes? Time is of the essence; I must depart within fifteen minutes."

Mrs. Thornton withdrew from the room. Her servants, accustomed to her sharp directives, found her instructions today confused and hesitant. Mr. Thornton lingered in the dining-room, ostensibly planning his duties at the police office, yet his thoughts were entirely consumed by Margaret. The memory of her arms encircling his neck, the soft, clinging pressure that sent a flush through his skin, rendered everything else dim and vague.

The tea service was unnervingly quiet, broken only by Fanny's incessant recounting of her alarm, her subsequent relief, and her physical tremors. "There, that suffices," Mr. Thornton interrupted, rising. "The reality itself was ample." As he prepared to leave, his mother laid a hand upon his arm.

"You will return here before proceeding to the Hales'," she urged in a hushed, anxious tone.

"I comprehend what lies ahead," Fanny murmured to herself.

"Why?" Mrs. Thornton pressed gently. "Will it prove too late to disturb them?"

"John, return to me for this single evening," she pleaded, a rare vulnerability breaking through her pride. "It will be late for Mrs. Hale. Yet, that is not the sole concern. Tomorrow, you will..." She stopped, her eyes glistening. "Come back tonight, John!"

Though rarely one to plead, she had never failed in her entreaties. "I shall return directly after concluding my business. You will ensure they are inquired after? Specifically her?"

Mrs. Thornton was typically taciturn and a poor listener when her son was absent, yet upon his return, her senses were acutely attuned to every detail of his endeavors to protect himself and his chosen allies from future outrage. He perceived his objective clearly: punishment and suffering were the inevitable consequences for those who participated in the riot. All that was required to safeguard property and assert the proprietor's authority was a decisive, sword-like execution of justice.

"Mother! Are you aware of what I must convey to Miss Hale tomorrow?" The question struck her unexpectedly amidst a lull where she had momentarily forgotten Margaret.

She lifted her gaze. "Yes, John. You can hardly avoid it."

"Give me alternative instruction? I fail to grasp your meaning."

"I imply that, having allowed her emotions to overwhelm you, you are bound by honor—"

"Honor!" He scoffed. "I fear honor plays no role here. 'Her emotions overwhelmed me!' Which emotions do you refer to?"

"Nay, John, there is no cause for anger. Did she not rush to cling to you to shield you from peril?"

"She did!" he admitted. "Yet, Mother," he halted in his stride, facing her squarely. "I dare not harbor hope. I have never been faint-hearted before, but I cannot conceive that a creature of her sort would care for me."

"Don't indulge in folly, John. A creature of that description! She might be the daughter of a duke, judging by your tone." She smiled slowly, tears gathering in her eyes. "And what further proof of her affection could you desire? I suspect she has wrestled with her aristocratic prejudices, but I find her more commendable for seeing clearly at last. It is a considerable concession on my part to admit this," she whispered, her voice trembling. "For after tonight, I stand second."

"Dearest Mother!" (Love remains inherently selfish, and instantly he reverted to his own hopes and fears, casting a chilling shadow over Mrs. Thornton's heart.) "But I know she does not care for me. I shall place myself at her feet—I must. Were it but a sliver of chance in a thousand, or a million, I would do it."

"Don't tremble!" she commanded, suppressing her own mortification regarding his lack of attention to her fleeting maternal warmth and the sting of jealous neglect. "As far as love extends, she may prove worthy of you. It required immense effort to subdue her pride. Do not fear, John," she said, kissing him as she bade him goodnight. She exited the room with slow, majestic dignity. Once inside her chamber, however, she locked the door and sank into a chair to weep tears she had never before shed.

***

Margaret entered the sitting room, where her parents sat in hushed conference, appearing very pale and wan. She approached them cautiously before finding the strength to speak. "Mrs. Thornton intends to send the water-bed, Mamma."

"My dear, how exhausted you appear! Is the heat oppressive, Margaret?"

"It is very hot, and the streets are rugged due to the strike."

Margaret's complexion flared briefly with vitality before fading instantly. "A message arrived from Bessy Higgins requesting your company," Mrs. Hale said. "Yet, I am

A stanza from William Fowler hung in the silence of the room, a melancholy testament to a heart both captive and defiant. "Your beauty was the first that won the place / And scal'd the walls of my undaunted heart..." The words seemed to echo the turmoil within Margaret Hale as she dragged herself upright the following morning. Though unrefreshed, she felt a strange, heavy restfulness settle over her. The household had passed through the storm with remarkable grace; her mother had awakened only once, and the air outside, though stiflingly hot, carried the faint promise of a breeze. Somewhere in the unseen copse or thick green wood, a murmur danced—a rushing, falling noise that promised a cool retreat, a distinct gladness that mirrored the peace she sought.

Seated at her work in Mrs. Hale's room, Margaret plotted a path to normalcy. Soon she would assist her mother with dressing, and later visit Bessy Higgins. Above all, she intended to banish the memory of the Thorntons, refusing to conjure images of them until they stood physically before her again. Yet, paradoxically, the sheer effort to suppress the thought served only to sharpen it. From time to time, a hot flush swept across her pale cheeks, rising like a sunbeam breaking through watery clouds, staining her complexion with a sudden, undeniable color.

Soft footsteps approached, and Dixon slid the door open, creeping silently across the floorboards. "Mr. Thornton, Miss Margaret. He is in the drawing-room."

Margaret dropped her embroidery instantly, the needle clinking against the fabric. "Did he ask for me? Has Papa returned?"

"He asked for you, miss; Master is out."

"Very well, I will come," Margaret said, her voice steady, though her heart hammered a frantic rhythm against her ribs. She lingered by the shaded window, reluctant to leave the sanctuary of the room, but duty compelled her forward.

In the drawing-room, John Thornton stood near a window, ostensibly gazing out at the street, yet inwardly paralyzed by fear. His own heart beat a thunderous drumbeat at the prospect of her arrival. He could not shake the lingering sensation of her arms wrapped around his neck, an embrace he had initially resented as clumsy but which now haunted him with a terrifying tenderness. The memory of her clinging defense seemed to dissolve his iron resolve, melting his composure like wax before a flame. He dreaded the approach, terrified that if he moved to meet her, he would reach out with arms wide open, begging her to return to that safe haven she had created the previous day. A strong man, he trembled at the mere anticipation of the words he needed to speak, fearing how she might receive them. Perhaps she would wilt, flush, and flutter into his embrace, finding her natural home once more. One moment he burned with impatience at the possibility of this reunion; the next, a cold dread seized him—a vision of passionate rejection that withered his future with a blight so deadly he could scarcely bear to entertain it.

Startled by a shift in the air, he turned. She had entered so softly, her progress masked by the hum of the street, that he had heard nothing but her gentle tread. She stood by the table, refusing to sit. Her eyelids cast long shadows over her eyes, her lips slightly parted to reveal the pale line beneath. Her breathing was slow and deep, expanding her chin and the noble lines of her nose, the only movement on a face otherwise still. Her skin, usually warm and rosy, appeared wan today, the loss of health accentuated by the heavy curtain of dark hair framing her temples, hiding the bruise of the blow she had suffered. Even with her eyes lowered, her head was tilted back in its familiar, proud posture. She resembled a prisoner falsely accused of a crime she despised, too indignant to defend herself.

Thornton stepped forward hastily, then composed himself, walking with quiet firmness to the door—which she had left ajar—to close it. Returning to stand opposite her, he allowed himself a moment to take in the total impression of her presence before daring to disturb it, perhaps to repel it, with whatever words lay on his tongue.

"Miss Hale, I was very ungrateful yesterday—"

"You had nothing to be grateful for," she interrupted, raising her gaze to meet his directly. "You mean, I suppose, that you believe you ought to thank me for what I did." Despite her anger, a thick blush spread across her face, burning into her eyes, yet her gaze remained grave and steady. "It was only a natural instinct; any woman would have acted precisely the same. We all feel the sanctity of our sex as a high privilege when confronted with danger. I ought, rather," she hastened to add, "to apologize to you for those thoughtless words which sent you down into peril."

"It was not your words; it was the truth they conveyed, however pungently expressed," he countered, stalling just short of the edge where his hot passion threatened to carry him away. He weighed each word carefully, his will triumphing over impulse. "But you shall not drive me off upon that excuse and so escape the expression of my deep gratitude, my—" He paused, halting in his career.

"I do not attempt to escape from anything," she declared calmly. "I simply state that you owe me no gratitude; and I may add that any expression of it would be painful to me, for I do not feel I deserve it. Still, if it will relieve you from even a fancied obligation, speak on."

"I do not wish to be relieved from any obligation," he said, goaded by her serene demeanor. "Fancied or real, I question neither. I choose to believe that I owe my very life to you—ay, smile if you will, and call it an exaggeration. I believe it, because it adds a value to that existence to know—" He broke off, lowering his voice to a tender, intense whisper that made her shiver and tremble before him, "to think circumstances so wrought that whenever I exult in existence henceforward, I may say to myself, 'All this gladness in life, all honest pride in my labor, all this keen sense of being, I owe to her!' It doubles the joy, it makes the pride glow, it sharpens the sense of existence until I can barely distinguish whether it is pain or pleasure to realize I owe it to one person—nay, you must, you shall hear," he stepped forward with stern determination, "to one whom I love as I do not believe any man has ever loved a woman before." He gripped her hand tightly, panting as he awaited her response.

With sudden, icy fury, he drew his hand away. "How!" he exclaimed. "Offends you! I am indeed most unfortunate."

"Yes!" she replied, regaining her dignity. "I do feel offended; and, I think, justly. You seem to imagine that my conduct yesterday was a personal act between us, and that you may come to thank me for it. Instead, you fail to perceive, as a gentleman would—yes! a gentleman, as we discussed before—that any woman, worthy of the name, would step forward to shield, with her revered helplessness, a man from the violence of a mob."

"And the gentleman thus rescued is forbidden the relief of thanks!" he broke in contemptuously. "I am a man. I claim the right of expressing my feelings."

"And I yield to that right," she answered, her voice rising with indignation, "simply stating that you gave me pain by insisting upon it." Tears, held back for a long struggle, rose violently to her eyes, choking her voice. "But you seem to have imagined that I was not merely guided by womanly instinct, but"—she struggled for words—"but that I was prompted by some particular feeling for *you*—you! Why, there was not a man—not a single desperate soul in that entire crowd—for whom I did not feel greater sympathy, for whom I could not have done what little I could more heartily."

"You may speak on, Miss Hale. I am aware of these misplaced sympathies of yours. I now believe it was only your innate sense of oppression—that yes, I, though a master, may be oppressed—that made you act so nobly. I know you despise me; allow me to say, it is because you do not understand me."

"I do not care to understand," she replied, gripping the table to steady herself. To her mind, he was cruel, and she was weak with indignation.

"No, I see you do not. You are unfair and unjust."

Margaret compressed her lips. She would offer no answer to such accusations. Yet, despite his savage words, he could have thrown himself at her feet and kissed the hem of her garment. She remained silent and unmoving. Hot tears of wounded pride streamed down her face. He waited, longing for some taunt, some spark of rebuttal, but she remained a statue of silence. Finally, he took up his hat.

"One word more. You look as if you thought it tainted you to be loved by me. You cannot avoid it. Nay, I, if I would, cannot cleanse you from it. But I would not if I could. I have never loved any woman before: my life has been too busy, my thoughts too absorbed with other matters. Now I love, and will love. But do not be afraid of too much expression on my part."

"I am not afraid," she replied, lifting herself upright. "No one has ever dared to be impertinent to me, and no one

The air in the room hung heavy, thick with the aftermath of Mr. Thornton's visit. Margaret sat frozen, the echoes of his voice still vibrating in her ears like a trapped bird. A strange, involuntary comparison had taken root in her mind between Mr. Lennox and Mr. Thornton. With Lennox, the line between friendship and love had been dangerously blurred, only to snap back with immediate regret on both sides. But with Thornton, there had been no gentle slope. From the start, their paths had been drawn in opposition; his opinions clashed with hers, dismissed not with kindness, but with a rock-like disdain that made her protests feel futile. Yet, now, he had returned in a wild, passionate storm, declaring his love.

At first, Margaret had feared the confession was forced, a desperate reaction to her humiliation. But within moments, a terrifying clarity settled over her. He did not love her merely because he pities her; he loved her. The conviction burned brightly, undeniable and absolute. She shrank back, shivering as if confronted by some ancient, terrible power. To hide from it was useless; his "strong idea" haunted her thoughts, wandering relentlessly through her mind. How dared he claim endurance when she had shaken him off with contempt? Regret gnawed at her as she realized she had failed to speak with sufficient strength, wishing desperately for the words that now crowded her mind but remained unuttered. The interview lingered like a nightmare horror, refusing to fade even as she attempted to wake from it, cowering in the corner of her mind with ghastly eyes, waiting to see if she dared breathe a word of it to anyone.

Driven away from this threatening vision, Margaret sought action. She needed to shake off the paralysis of the past hour. Opening the window to let in the stifling air, then stepping out into the profound silence of the house, she found herself unable to remain alone. The stillness of the midday, broken only by the labored breathing of an invalid, pressed upon her. An impulse seized her: *Go and see Bessy Higgins.* The thought of the girl's suffering flashed into her mind, offering a distraction from her internal turmoil.

Bessy lay flat on the settle, moved close to the fire despite the sultry heat, as if recovering from a violent paroxysm of pain. Margaret, noting the girl's lack of breath, raised her and rearranged the pillows with quiet efficiency. When Bessy finally spoke, her voice was weak and wistful. "I thought I should na' ha' seen yo' again." Margaret confessed she had not been able to come sooner due to her mother's illness, a small white lie that brought color to her cheeks.

Bessy explained that sending Mary for Margaret had pushed her limits, the earlier riotous scenes having torn her apart. She craved Margaret's voice, seeking some words of peace to help her drift into the silence of God, much like a baby lulled by a mother's song. When Margaret offered to read, Bessy eagerly accepted, promising that while the sense might seem distant at first, the comforting texts would draw close.

As Margaret began to read, Bessy's body twitched, restless and convulsed. Suddenly, she burst out, "Don't go on reading. It's no use. I'm blaspheming all the time in my mind, wi' thinking angrily on what canna be helped." She shifted to the subject of the riot at Marlborough Mills, specifically Thornton's factory.

Margaret, coloring deeply, asked if her father had been present. "Not he," Bessy replied, her spirit dimmed. "He'd ha' given his right hand if it had never come to pass. It's that that's fretting me. He's fairly knocked down in his mind by it." She described the committee's strategy: unity, patience, and strict adherence to the law, avoiding any provocation that might justify violence. They believed they were right in their demands and trusted the public to see justice in their suffering. However, the plan had unraveled through the actions of Boucher, a foolish man who had gone contrary to orders.

"He ruined th' strike, just as bad as if he meant to be a Judas," Bessy whispered, her voice trembling. "Eh! but father giv'd it him last night! He said he'd tell the police where to find the ringleader, give him up to the mill-owners... For," she dropped her voice, "they do say that Boucher threw a stone at Thornton's sister—that welly killed her."

"That's not true!" Margaret exclaimed, her face flushing red then draining white. "It was not Boucher that threw the stone."

"You'd be there then?" Bessy asked, her eyes languid.

"Yes. Never mind." Margaret insisted firmly. "But what did he answer to your father?"

"He did na' speak words," Bessy recounted, describing Boucher's spent passion, his quick breath, and the thought that he might be sobbing. "But when father said he'd give him up to police, he gave a great cry, and struck father on th' face wi' his closed fist, and he off like lightning."

Her father, stunned by the blow, had sat down, putting his hand before his eyes before rushing out. Bessy had thrown herself onto the settle, clinging to him. "Father, father!" she had cried. "Thou'll never go peach on that poor clemmed man." Her father, pale and bloody-faced, had shaken her off, vowing that while he despised Boucher, he could not betray him now that the man had struck him. He intended to fight him later, in proper condition, but for now, he would not denounce him.

"I know not if I slept or waked," Bessy murmured, growing drowsy again. "Read me not a sermon chapter, but a story chapter; they've pictures in them..." She drifted off, muttering pleas for the New Heavens and New Earth, seeking an escape from the weariness of her reality.

Margaret covered the girl with a blanket and left, carrying an uneasy conscience. Back home, she found her mother, Mrs. Hale, in the drawing-room. Her mother was in one of her better days, praising the water-bed with a fervor that bordered on obsession. She claimed the beds of former times possessed a quality lost to modern manufacturing, a sentiment Mr. Hale gently disputed, attributing the difference to the vitality of youth. His wife dismissed his theory, insisting it was the specific craftsmanship of Sir John Beresford's beds that mattered.

Margaret laughed, admitting she rarely thought about her bed, falling asleep instantly wherever she lay. This prompted her mother to recount stories of her husband's childhood, contrasting the beauty of little Frederick with Margaret's own infancy. "I said, 'Dear, what an ugly little thing!' And she said, 'It's not every child that's like Master Fred,'" Mrs. Hale recalled, her voice filled with affection. "Everybody loved him. He was born with the gift of winning hearts. It makes me think very badly of Captain Reid when I know that he disliked my own dear boy."

Then, the mood shifted. Mrs. Hale's eyes grew misty, her voice choking. "Your poor father, Margaret. He has left the room. He can't bear to hear Fred spoken of." Margaret sat by her mother's sofa, holding her hand, as tears flowed freely. Finally, Mrs. Hale sat upright, her tone solemn and urgent. "If I can get better, it must be through seeing my son Frederick once more. It will waken up all the poor springs of health left in me."

"And, Margaret," she continued, her voice quavering with the contemplation of impending death, "if I am to die... I must see my child first. I charge you, Margaret, as you yourself hope for comfort in your last illness, bring him to me that I may bless him. Only for five minutes... Let me see him before I die!"

Margaret felt the weight of the entreaty. Logic and caution held no sway against the passionate plea of a dying woman. The sight of her mother's large, pleading eyes fixed upon her compelled her to vow compliance. "Mamma, I will write to-night, and tell Frederick what you say. I am as sure that he will come directly to us, as I am sure of my life."

"But the post goes out at five!" Mrs. Hale urged, trembling with feverish eagerness. "Write by it, won't you? Don't lose a single post; for just by that very post I may miss him."

"Papa is out," Margaret reminded her gently.

"Papa is out! and what then?" Mrs. Hale snapped, her eyes flashing. "Do you mean that he would deny me this last wish? Why, I should not be ill, be dying—if he had not taken me away from Helstone, to this unhealthy, smoky, sunless place!"

"Oh, mamma!" Margaret sighed.

"Yes; it is so, indeed," her mother insisted. "He knows it himself... The longing to see Frederick stands between me and God. I cannot pray till I have this one thing." She fell back, exhausted, convinced that her son would arrive within twenty-two days.

"You are not writing!" Mrs. Hale demanded suddenly, sitting up again. "Bring me some pens and paper; I will try and write myself." She trembled, greedily watching Margaret prepare to write.

"Only wait till papa comes in," Margaret pleaded, trying to delay the inevitable. "Let us ask him how best to do it

When Mr. Thornton departed from the house that morning, the road beneath his feet seemed to sway under the weight of a fury that threatened to blind him. His mind spun with a dizziness akin to vertigo, as if Margaret Hale, rather than appearing as the tender, graceful lady she was, had been a rough fish-wife striking him with heavy fists. He suffered a genuine, physical agony—a pounding headache and a pulse that hammered irregular beats against his ribs. The clamor of the streets, the glare of the sun, the endless rumble of traffic became intolerable to his nerves. He cursed himself for a fool, yet the source of his torment remained a foggy mystery, disproportionate in its aftermath to whatever cause had sparked it. A strange, irrational longing seized him: to sit on a doorstep beside a weeping child, letting his own passions rage outward in tearful storm against some imagined wrong.

"I hate Margaret!" he whispered to the empty air, shaping the words with a ferocity that betrayed nothing of his truth. Yet, as soon as the syllables formed, a jagged lightning bolt of love tore through his dark, thunderous resolve, cleaving it in twain. This was his solace, his torture: the knowledge that though she might scorn him, despise him, or treat him with the cold indifference of a queen, he remained immutable. She could not alter him. He loved her, and he would love her, defying both her and this miserable, throbbing body.

He paused, steeling this resolution until it burned bright and clear. An omnibus clattered past, bound for the country. The driver, mistaking his stillness for a desire for a seat, halted near the curb. Apologizing would have taken too much effort; so, he stepped aboard. The vehicle carried him swiftly away from the town, past rows of uniform housing, past manicured villas with their neat gardens, until the landscape softened into true country hedgerows and finally a small rural settlement. Here, the passengers alighted, and Mr. Thornton followed, unable to remain where the machine had deposited him.

He strode into the open fields, forcing a brisk pace to jolt the stagnant water of his mind. Now, the memories flooded back with cruel clarity. How ridiculous he must have appeared—the man who had done precisely what he had sworn never to do, meeting every disaster he had predicted for himself. Was he truly bewitched by those luminous eyes, that soft, sighing mouth pressed so intimately against his shoulder mere hours prior? He could not banish the recollection of her embrace, however brief, however fleeting the promise of "never again." He saw her only in flashes, unable to grasp her wholly. One moment she was brave, the next timid; today tender, tomorrow haughty and regal. He mentally reviewed every encounter, trying to find the version of her that fit his logic, but she eluded categorization. Even this morning, she had looked magnificent, her eyes blazing with indignation at the notion that sharing his danger yesterday implied any affection whatsoever.

If Mr. Thornton considered himself a fool in the morning, his afternoon offered no enlightenment. His sixpenny ride yielded only a sharper conviction: that no one existed like Margaret Hale, that she did not love him and never would, yet that neither she nor the entire world should stand between him and his devotion. Returning to the market square, he boarded the bus to return to Milton.

By late afternoon, the vehicle set him down near his warehouse. The familiar surroundings triggered the familiar rhythms of duty. He knew the mountain of tasks awaiting him: the commotion of the previous day had doubled his workload. He must meet with his fellow magistrates, finalize the precarious arrangements for his newly arrived Irish workers, and ensure they remained isolated from the disgruntled locals of Milton. Finally, he must return home to face his mother.

Inside the dining room, Mrs. Thornton had spent the entire day in a state of suspended animation, waiting for the news of her son's union with Miss Hale. She had braced herself against every creak of the floorboards, every footfall, catching up dropped needles with a trembling, dim-sighted hand. Time and again, the door had opened to admit some trivial visitor, causing her rigid countenance to slacken into a mask of despondency, alien to her usual sternness. She violently pulled her gaze away from the bleak future of widowhood, forcing her thoughts back into the safe channels of household management. She began counting out fresh linens for the prospective bride, sorting piles of tablecloths and napkins.

Confusion arose as she distinguished what belonged to her—from the era of G.H.T.—from what her son had purchased and marked with his own initials. Some of George and Hannah's old stock, exquisite Dutch damasks, held a beauty unmatched by modern fabrics. Mrs. Thornton stared at them, lost in a reverie of her youth, her pride. Then, knitting her brows, she pinched her lips together and carefully unpicked the faded "G.H." from a hem. She searched frantically for Turkey-red thread to embroider the new monogram, but the spool was bare; she lacked the heart to order more just yet. She stared into the void, a theater of visions playing out where her son was the sole protagonist—her pride, her property.

Still, he did not arrive. Of course, he was with Miss Hale. That new love was already eroding her position as the first in his heart. A terrible, physical sting of jealous pain shot through her, indistinguishable from grief, compelling her to slump into a chair. Moments later, she rose again, as upright and iron-hard as before. A grim smile touched her lips, the first sign of it all day, preparing for the triumphant knock on the door. She told herself that Miss Hale was merely John's wife, a vessel for the glory of his choice. All the comforts of a large household, the honor and obedience of a wife, the "purple and fine linen"—these would flow naturally like jewels onto a king's robe, requiring no individual consideration. If Miss Hale were a native of Milton, Mrs. Thornton would have embraced her instantly. She possessed spirit, flavor, and a certain pungent wit. True, she was prejudiced and ill-educated, flaws easily excused by her Southern upbringing. A silent, mortifying comparison with Fanny played out in her mind, leading her to speak harshly to her daughter, abusing her thoroughly before retreating to read *Henry's Commentaries*, an exercise she used to punish herself for lingering on the linens.

His step! She heard it, even as her eye traced the line of text and her mind mechanically recited the sentence. While her intellect processed the words, her heightened senses interpreted the rhythm of his approach. He moved to the hatstand, then to the threshold of the room. Why did he hesitate? Let her know the truth.

Yet, her gaze remained lowered over the book. He approached the table, standing silent until she finished the paragraph that seemed to hold her spellbound. With an effort, she lifted her head. "Well, John?"

She understood the subtext of those two words. But he had armored his heart. A bitter jest rose to his lips, the acid of his soul, but he stifled it; his mother deserved better. He circled behind her, blocking her view of his expression, and bent forward to kiss the stone-gray, frozen face. Murmuring softly, he whispered, "No one loves me, Mother. No one cares for me, save you."

He turned away, leaning his forehead against the mantelpiece, fighting back tears that blurred his vision. She rose, swaying slightly—for the first time in her life, the formidable woman trembled. Placing her hands on his broad shoulders, she looked deeply into his face, demanding he look at her.

"Mother's love is a gift from God, John. It holds fast forever. A girl's love is like a breath of smoke—it shifts with every wind. And she would not have you, my own boy, would she?" She bit her lip hard, exposing her teeth in a snarl that stretched across her mouth. He shook his head slowly.

"I am not fit for her, Mother. I knew I was not."

She ground out words between clenched jaws, unintelligible to his ear, yet her eyes revealed a curse, raw and intent as any spoken aloud. Yet, strangely, her heart leaped with a lightness, knowing he was hers again, bound to her.

"Mother!" he urged, hurriedly, "I cannot utter a single word against her. Spare me! My heart is broken and weak—I love her, I love her more than ever!"

"And I hate her," Mrs. Thornton hissed in a low, fierce tone. "I struggled not to hate her when she stood between us, telling myself she would bring you happiness, and I would give my very blood to ensure it. But now, I hate her for your misery. Yes, John, do not try to hide your aching heart from me. I am the mother who bore you, and your sorrow is my agony. If you do not hate her, then I shall."

"Then, Mother, you drive me to love her more. She is treated unfairly by you, and I must restore the balance. But why speak of love or hatred? She does not care for me, and that is sufficient, more than enough. Let us never mention the subject again. It is the only kindness you can show me. Let us never name her."

"With all my

"For never any thing can be amiss
When simpleness and duty tender it."

Mr. Thornton approached the business of the following day with unerring clarity. A slight demand for finished goods presented itself, and he seized upon it, driving hard bargains with the precision of a clockmaker. His performance at the meeting of the magistrates was equally sharp; he offered his strongest sense and his unique ability to perceive consequences instantly, leading to rapid decisions. While older men, those of greater wealth and established standing, looked to him for wisdom, he remained indifferent to their deference, regarding it no more than the soft west wind that barely disturbed the smoke rising from Milton's great chimneys. Unaware of the silent respect paid to him, he focused solely on the swift accomplishment of his duties. To him, the whispers circulating among the womenfolk of the town—that if he had not been present, matters would have gone much worse—were merely background noise. He swept aside his business with relentless efficiency. It appeared that the deep mortification of the previous day, followed by hours of stunned inaction, had finally burned away the mists from his intellect. He felt his power and reveled in it, capable of defying his own heart. Had he known it, he could have sung the song of the miller living by the river Dee: "I care for nobody—Nobody cares for me."

Before him sat the evidence against Boucher and other ringleaders of the riot; the charges against three others, accused of conspiracy, failed. Yet, he sternly charged the police to remain vigilant, insisting that the swift arm of the law stand ready to strike the moment proof of guilt emerged. Leaving the hot, reeking room of the borough court, he stepped out into the fresher, yet still sultry, streets. Suddenly, he seemed to give way; a profound languor overtook him, and his thoughts wandered inevitably to *her*. They recalled not the scene of his recent repulsion, but the looks and actions of the day before. He walked mechanically through the crowded thoroughfares, weaving in and out among the populace without truly seeing them, nearly sick with a longing for that brief, halcyon hour when she had clung to him and her heart beat against his.

"Why, Mr. Thornton! you're cutting me very coolly, I must say. And how is Mrs. Thornton? Brave weather this! We doctors don't like it, I can tell you!"

"I beg your pardon, Dr. Donaldson. I really didn't see you. My mother's quite well, thank you. It is a fine day, and good for the harvest, I hope. If the wheat is well got in, we shall have a brisk trade next year, whatever you doctors have."

"Ay, ay. Each man for himself. Your bad weather, and your bad times, are my good ones. When trade is bad, there's more undermining of health, and preparation for death, going on among you Milton men than you're aware of."

"Not with me, Doctor. I'm made of iron. The news of the very worst bad debt I ever had, never made my pulse vary. This strike, which affects me more than any one else in Milton,—more than Hamper,—never comes near my appetite. You must go elsewhere for a patient, Doctor."

"By the way, you've recommended me a good patient, poor lady! Not to go on talking in this heartless way, I seriously believe that Mrs. Hale—that lady in Crampton, you know—hasn't many weeks to live. I never had any hope of cure, as I think I told you; but I've been seeing her to-day, and I think very badly of her."

Mr. Thornton fell silent. For an instant, the vaunted steadiness of his pulse faltered.

"Can I do anything, Doctor?" he asked, his voice altered. "You know—you would see, that money is not very plentiful, are there any comforts or dainties she ought to have?"

"No," replied the Doctor, shaking his head. "She craves for fruit,—she has a constant fever on her; but jargonelle pears will do as well as anything, and there are quantities of them in the market."

"You will tell me if there is anything I can do, I'm sure," replied Mr. Thornton. "I rely upon you."
