"Oh! never fear! I'll not spare your purse,—I know it's deep enough. I wish you'd give me a carte-blanche for all my patients, and all their wants."

Yet Mr. Thornton possessed no general benevolence, nor universal philanthropy; few would have credited him with strong affections. However, he proceeded directly to the first fruit-shop in Milton. With meticulous care, he selected a bunch of purple grapes bearing the most delicate bloom, the richest-colored peaches, and vines with the freshest leaves. These treasures were packed into a basket, and the shopman awaited the answer to his inquiry, "Where shall we send them to, sir?"

There was no reply. "To Marlborough Mills, I suppose, sir?"

"No!" Mr. Thornton said abruptly. "Give the basket to me,—I'll take it."

The burden required both his hands to carry, forcing him to navigate the busiest section of the town where ladies shopped. Many a young acquaintance turned to stare, finding it strange to see him occupied like a porter or an errand-boy.

*I will not be daunted from doing as I choose by the thought of her,* he thought. *I like to take this fruit to the poor mother, and it is simply right that I should. She shall never scorn me out of doing what I please. A pretty joke, indeed, if, for fear of a haughty girl, I failed in doing a kindness to a man I liked! I do it for Mr. Hale; I do it in defiance of her.*

Moving at an unusual pace, he reached Crampton quickly. Ascending two steps at a time, he entered the drawing-room before Dixon could announce him. His face was flushed, his eyes shining with a kindly earnestness. Mrs. Hale lay on the sofa, heated with fever. Mr. Hale was reading aloud, and Margaret worked on a low stool beside her mother. Her heart fluttered violently, though whether it was due to his presence or the gravity of the situation, he took no notice of her—not even of Mr. Hale himself. He advanced straight to Mrs. Hale with the basket, speaking in a subdued, gentle tone that was profoundly touching when uttered by a robust man addressing a feeble invalid.

"I met Dr. Donaldson, ma'am, and as he said fruit would be good for you, I have taken the liberty—the great liberty—of bringing you some that seemed to me fine."

Mrs. Hale was excessively pleased, trembling with eager delight. Mr. Hale, with fewer words, expressed a deeper gratitude.

"Fetch a plate, Margaret—a basket—anything." Margaret rose by the table, half afraid to move or make a sound that might alert Mr. Thornton to her presence. She feared an awkward collision of consciousness, assuming that from her position on the low seat, now standing behind her father, he had overlooked her in his haste. Yet, as if he did not feel the weight of her awareness, his eyes never rested upon her.

"I must go," he said. "I cannot stay. If you will forgive this liberty,—my rough ways,—too abrupt, I fear—but I will be more gentle next time. You will allow me the pleasure of bringing you some fruit again, if I should see any that is tempting. Good afternoon, Mr. Hale. Good-bye, ma'am."

And he was gone. Not one word, not one look directed toward Margaret. She was convinced he had not seen her. She moved silently to fetch a plate, lifting the fruit out tenderly with the tips of her delicate fingers. It was kind of him to bring it; and after everything that had happened yesterday!

"Oh! it is so delicious!" said Mrs. Hale, her voice weak. "How kind of him to think of me! Margaret love, only taste these grapes! Was it not good of him?"

"Yes!" said Margaret, quietly.

"Margaret!" Mrs. Hale said, rather querulously, "you won't like anything Mr. Thornton does. I never saw anybody so prejudiced."

Mr. Hale had been peeling a peach for his wife; cutting off a small piece for himself, he remarked, "If I had any prejudices, the gift of such delicious fruit as this would melt them away. I have not tasted such fruit—no! not even in Hampshire—since I was a boy; and, to boys, I fancy, all fruit is good. I remember eating sloes and crabs with a relish. Do you remember the matted-up currant bushes, Margaret, at the corner of the west-wall at the garden at home?"

Did she not? Did she not remember every weather-stain on the old stone wall; the gray and yellow lichens marking it like a map; the little crane's-bill growing in the crevices? Shaken by the tumultuous events of the last two days, her entire existence currently strained upon her fortitude. Somehow, these careless words of her father's, touching on the remembrance of sunny times past, caused her to start up. Dropping her sewing on the floor, she hurried out of the room into her own little chamber. Hardly had she yielded to the first choking sob when she became aware of Dixon standing at her drawers, evidently searching for something.

"Bless me, miss! How you startled me! Missus is not worse,

"That afternoon she walked swiftly to the Higgins's house. Mary was looking out for her, with a half-distrustful face. Margaret smiled into her eyes to reassure her. They passed quickly through the house-place, upstairs, and into the quiet presence of the dead. Then Margaret was glad that she had come. The face, so often weary with pain, so restless with troublous thoughts, had now the faint soft smile of eternal rest upon it. The slow tears gathered into Margaret's eyes, but a deep calm entered into her soul. And that was death! It looked more peaceful than life. All beautiful scriptures came into her mind. "They rest from their labours." "The weary are at rest." "He giveth his beloved sleep."

Slowly, slowly Margaret turned away from the bed. Mary was humbly sobbing in the background. They went downstairs without a word.

Resting his hand upon the house-table, Nicholas Higgins stood in the midst of the floor; his great eyes startled open by the news he had heard, as he came along the court, from many busy tongues. His eyes were dry and fierce; studying the reality of her death; bringing himself to understand that her place should know her no more. For she had been sickly, dying so long, that he had persuaded himself she would not die; that she would "pull through."

Margaret felt as if she had no business to be there, familiarly acquainting herself with the surroundings of death, which he, the father, had only just learnt. There had been a pause of an instant on the steep crooked stair, when she first saw him; but now she tried to steal past his abstracted gaze, and to leave him in the solemn circle of his household misery.

Mary sat down on the first chair she came to, and throwing her apron over her head, began to cry.

The noise appeared to rouse him. He took sudden hold of Margaret's arm, and held her till he could gather words to speak. His throat seemed dry; they came up thick, and choked, and hoarse:

"Were yo' with her? Did yo' see her die?"

"No!" replied Margaret, standing still with the utmost patience, now she found herself perceived. It was some time before he spoke again, but he kept his hold on her arm.

"All men must die," said he at last, with a strange sort of gravity, which first suggested to Margaret the idea that he had been drinking—not enough to intoxicate himself, but enough to make his thoughts bewildered. "But she were younger than me." Still he pondered over the event, not looking at Margaret, though he grasped her tight. Suddenly, he looked up at her with a wild searching inquiry in his glance. "Yo're sure and certain she's dead—not in a dwam, a faint? she's been so before, often."

"She is dead," replied Margaret. She felt no fear in speaking to him, though he hurt her arm with his gripe, and wild gleams came across the stupidity of his eyes.

"She is dead!" she said.

He looked at her still with that searching look, which seemed to fade out of his eyes as he gazed. Then he suddenly let go his hold of Margaret, and, throwing his body half across the table, he shook it and every piece of furniture in the room, with his violent sobs. Mary came trembling towards him.

"Get thee gone!—get thee gone!" he cried, striking wildly and blindly at her. "What do I care for thee?" Margaret took her hand and held it softly in hers. He tore his hair, he beat his head against the hard wood, then he lay exhausted and stupid. Still his daughter and Margaret did not move. Mary trembled from head to foot.

At last—it might have been a quarter of an hour, it might have been an hour—he lifted himself up. His eyes were swollen and bloodshot, and he seemed to have forgotten that any one was by; he scowled at the watchers when he saw them. He shook himself heavily, gave them one more sullen look, spoke never a word, but made for the door.

"Oh, father, father!" said Mary, throwing herself upon his arm,—"not to-night! Any night but to-night. Oh, help me! he's going out to drink again! Father, I'll not leave yo'. Yo' may strike, but I'll not leave yo'. She told me last of all to keep yo' fro' drink!"

But Margaret stood in the doorway, silent yet commanding. He looked up at her defyingly.

"It's my own house. Stand out o' the way, wench, or I'll make yo'!" He had shaken off Mary with violence; he looked ready to strike Margaret. But she never moved a feature—never took her deep, serious eyes off him. He stared back on her with gloomy fierceness. If she had stirred hand or foot, he would have thrust her aside with even more violence than he had used to his own daughter, whose face was bleeding from her fall against a chair.

"What are yo' looking at me in that way for?" asked he at last, daunted and awed by her severe calm. "If yo' think for to keep me from going what gait I choose, because she loved yo'—and in my own house, too, where I never asked yo' to come, yo're mista'en. It's very hard upon a man that he can't go to the only comfort left."

Margaret felt that he acknowledged her power. What could she do next? He had seated himself on a chair, close to the door; half-conquered half-resenting; intending to go out as soon as she left her position, but unwilling to use the violence he had threatened not five minutes before. Margaret laid her hand on his arm.

"Come with me," she said. "Come and see her!"

The voice in which she spoke was very low and solemn; but there was no fear or doubt expressed in it, either of him or of his compliance. He sullenly rose up. He stood uncertain, with dogged irresolution upon his face. She waited him there; quietly and patiently waited for his time to move. He had a strange pleasure in making her wait; but at last he moved towards the stairs.

She and he stood by the corpse.

"Her last words to Mary were, 'Keep my father fro' drink.'"

"It canna hurt her now," muttered he. "Nought can hurt her now." Then, raising his voice to a wailing cry, he went on: "We may quarrel and fall out—we may make peace and be friends—we may clem to skin and bone—and nought o' all our griefs will ever touch her more. Hoo's had her portion on 'em. What wi' hard work first, and sickness at last, hoo' led the life of a dog. And to die without knowing one good piece o' rejoicing in all her days! Nay, wench, whatever hoo said, hoo can know nought about it now, and I mun ha' a sup o' drink just to steady me again sorrow."

"No," said Margaret, softening with his softened manner. "You shall not. If her life has been what you say, at any rate she did not fear death as some do. Oh, you should have heard her speak of the life to come—the life hidden with God, that she is now gone to."

He shook his head, glancing sideways up at Margaret as he did so. His pale haggard face struck her painfully.

"You are sorely tried. Where have you been all day—not at work?"

"Not at work, sure enough," said he, with a short, grim laugh. "Not at what you call work. I were at the Committee, till I were sickened out wi' trying to make fools hear reason. I were fetched to Boucher's wife afore seven this morning. She's bed-fast, but she were raving and raging to know where her dunner-headed brute of a chap was, as if I'd to keep him—as if he were fit to be ruled by me. The d—d fool, who has put his foot in all our plans! And I've walked my feet sore wi' going about for to see men who wouldn't be seen, now the law is raised against us. And I were sore-hearted, too, which is worse than sore-footed; and if I did see a friend who ossed to treat me, I never knew hoo lay a-dying here. Bess, lass, thou'd believe me, thou wouldst—wouldstn't thou?" turning to the poor dumb form with wild appeal.

"I am sure," said Margaret, "I am sure you did not know; it was quite sudden. But, now, you see, it would be different; you do know; you do see her lying there; you hear what she said with her last breath. You will not go?"

No answer. In fact, where was he to look for comfort?

"Come home with me," said she at last, with a bold venture, half trembling at her own proposal as she made it. "At least you shall have some comfortable food, which I'm sure you need."

"Yo'r father's a parson?" asked he, with a sudden turn in his ideas.

"He was," said Margaret, shortly.

"I'll go and take a dish o' tea with him, since yo've asked me. I've many a thing I often wished to say to a

"Some wishes crossed my mind and dimly cheered it,
And one or two poor melancholy pleasures,
Each in the pale unwarming light of hope,
Silvering its flimsy wing, flew silent by—
Moths in the moonbeam!"

Morning broke with a letter for Margaret from Edith. The writing was as affectionate and scattered as its author, yet the warmth of it soothed Margaret's own tender heart, and her upbringing had taught her to overlook the lack of sequence. The message read: "Oh, Margaret, it is worth a journey from England to see my boy! He is a superb little fellow, especially in his caps, and most especially in the one you sent him, you good, dainty-fingered, persevering little lady! Having made all the mothers here envious, I want to show him to somebody new... perhaps, that's all the reason; perhaps it is not—nay, possibly, there is just a little cousinly love mixed with it; but I do want you so much to come here, Margaret! I'm sure it would be the very best thing for Aunt Hale's health; everybody here is young and well, and our skies are always blue, and our sun always shines..."

Edith rambled on about the band playing deliciously, the baby's constant smiles, and her husband Cosmo, whom she initially dismissed as stout and grumpy before retracting the insult with a laugh, declaring him a darling despite his "busy" nature. She urged Margaret to visit immediately, promising three months of sunshine and grapes to cure Aunt Hale of the "smoke of Milton," and joking that if Margaret didn't pack instantly, she might be descended from King Herod.

Margaret read the letter twice. First, she felt a profound longing for Edith's world—the freedom from care, the cheerful home, the endless sunlight. For the first time, she wished she could trade places, even if only for a single day, to feel young again amidst that brightness. Yet, beneath this envy lay a heavy realization: she was not yet twenty, but the crushing pressure of recent days had aged her spirit. Then, reading again, she laughed at the letter's resemblance to Edith herself, finding amusement in the chaos.

Her laughter ceased abruptly when Mrs. Hale entered the drawing-room, supported by Dixon's arm. Margaret rushed forward to arrange the pillows, her movements urgent as she settled her frail mother onto the sofa. "What were you laughing at, Margaret?" her mother asked, recovering her breath.

"A letter from Edith," Margaret replied softly, reading it aloud. For a moment, her mother engaged with the content, guessing names for Edith's son and speculating on the reasons behind them. Then Mr. Thornton entered, bringing a basket of peaches. He could not, or perhaps would not, deny himself the fleeting pleasure of seeing Margaret, though his motive was merely the gratification of the moment. His entrance was marked by a stiff, cold bow; thereafter, his gaze avoided hers entirely. He offered the fruit, exchanged polite, gentle words, and departed with a grave farewell that felt like an offense.

Margaret sat silent and pale. "Do you know, Margaret, I really begin to like Mr. Thornton," Mrs. Hale observed.

"Do you?" Margaret forced out, her voice icy.

"Yes! I think he is really getting quite polished in his manners." Margaret's tone softened. "He is very kind and attentive—there is no doubt of that."

"I wonder Mrs. Thornton never calls. She must know I am ill, because of the water-bed."

"I dare say, she hears how you are from her son."

"Still, I should like to see her. You have so few friends here, Margaret." A tender craving rose in Margaret's throat—the desperate wish to bespeak kindness toward a daughter who might soon be left motherless. But she remained mute. After a pause, her mother asked if Margaret could invite Mrs. Thornton to visit. "Only once—I don't want to be troublesome."

"I will do anything, if you wish it, mamma—but if—but when Frederick comes—"

"Oh, to be sure! We must keep our doors shut. I hardly know whether I dare wish him to come or not. Sometimes I think I would rather not. Sometimes I have such frightful dreams about him."

"Oh, mamma! We'll take good care. I will put my arm in the bolt sooner than he should come to the slightest harm. Trust the care of him to me, mamma. I will watch over him like a lioness over her young."

As they discussed arrangements for Frederick's expected arrival and potential househelp, Mrs. Hale chided Margaret for using "factory slang," specifically the word "slack." Margaret defended her usage, arguing that living in a factory town required its own language, and that she could surprise her mother with terms like "knobstick." Her mother dismissed it as vulgar, prompting Margaret to promise restraint.

Suddenly, Mrs. Hale turned the conversation back to the air in Milton, asserting that the smoke was indeed making her ill. Margaret sprang up, terrified that her father, who had just entered, might confirm her mother's suspicion. Anxious to protect his feelings from the faint shadow of injury he might have perceived, she began speaking rapidly of other topics, unaware that Mr. Thornton was following close behind.

"Mamma is accusing me of having picked up a great deal of vulgarity since we came to Milton," Margaret said, addressing both her mother and Mr. Thornton. The "vulgarity" concerned only local dialect, arising from their previous discussion, but Mr. Thornton's brow darkened. Margaret realized instantly how easily her words could be misinterpreted by him. Driven by a sweet, instinctive desire to avoid unnecessary pain, she pressed forward, turning directly to him.

"Now, Mr. Thornton, though 'knobstick' has not a very pretty sound, is it not expressive? Could I do without it, in speaking of the thing it represents? If using local words is vulgar, I was very vulgar in the Forest,—was I not, mamma?"

It was unusual for Margaret to dominate a conversation, but her anxiety to shield Mr. Thornton from annoyance caused her to speak with unexpected force. As she finished, she flushed crimson under the weight of conscious shame, particularly as Mr. Thornton seemed to misunderstand her intent, passing her by with a cold, ceremonial reserve to address Mrs. Hale.

His departure haunted her. The sight of him reignited her wish to see his mother and commend herself to her care. Sitting in burning silence, vexed and ashamed of her clumsiness, Margaret heard her mother's slow entreaty for Mrs. Thornton's visit repeated. Mr. Thornton promised that Mrs. Thornton would come, conversed briefly, and then left. Immediately, Margaret's movements and voice seemed released from invisible chains.

He never looked at her; yet his careful avoidance of her eyes betokened that he knew precisely where, by chance, his gaze might have rested. If she spoke, he gave no sign of attention, yet his subsequent speech to anyone else was subtly modified by what she had said. Sometimes he answered an unspoken remark, directing it to another person as though suggested by her. This was not the bad manners of ignorance, nor simple rudeness; it was a wilful, deep-seated offense, born of bitterness but tempered by repentance. No deep plan or cunning strategy served him here.

Margaret pondered him more deeply than ever before. There was no tinge of love in her thoughts, only a sharp regret for the wound she had inflicted, coupled with a gentle, patient striving to restore their former position of antagonistic friendship. She believed a friend's place was what he had held in her regard, as well as in the minds of the entire family. Her behavior toward him was humbled, almost a mute apology for the strong words spoken in reaction to the riots.

Yet he resented those words bitterly. They rang in his ears, and he prided himself on the sense of justice that compelled him to offer kindness to her parents. He exulted in the power he displayed by forcing himself to face her whenever he could devise an action to please her father or mother. He thought he disliked seeing someone who had mortified him so keenly, but he was mistaken. It was a stinging pleasure to be in the same room with her, acutely aware of her presence. Though he was no great analyst of his own motives, he was wrong to dismiss the complexity of his feelings.

"The saddest birds a season find to sing."

"Never to fold the robe o'er secret pain,
Never, weighed down by memory's clouds again,
To bow thy head! Thou art gone home!"

The following morning, Mrs. Thornton visited Mrs. Hale. The condition of the latter had deteriorated sharply overnight; a sudden, terrifying step toward death had been taken in the space of twelve hours, leaving her face gaunt and gray with a pallor that startled her own family. Mrs. Thornton, who had not seen her friend for weeks, was struck with a sudden, overwhelming tenderness. She had arrived because her son had begged it of her as a personal favor, yet within her, the proud and bitter currents of her nature clashed fiercely against the Hales' family, of whom Margaret was a member. She harbored deep doubts regarding the reality of Mrs. Hale's illness, suspecting it might be merely a fleeting whim designed to disrupt the lady's regular employment. To her son, she vented her frustrations, declaring she wished they had never approached the house, lamenting that useless tongues like Latin and Greek had ever been invented. Her son endured her tirade with quiet patience, only to return gently but firmly to his original request: that she visit Mrs. Hale immediately, as it would suit the invalid best. Reluctantly, Mrs. Thornton submitted, finding a strange, twisted pride in obeying her son's wishes, all the while magnifying his "extraordinary goodness" in maintaining ties with the Hales.

In her mind, his virtue bordered on weakness, a sentiment shared by her own disdain for Mr. and Mrs. Hale, and her intense dislike for Margaret. These thoughts consumed her until she entered the darkened chamber where Mrs. Hale lay, a mother figure whose future seemed extinguished. The room offered no variation in light or shadow; the patient possessed little power of action, moving only with faint, whispered efforts alternating with studious silence. Yet, this monotonous existence seemed unbearable to the visitor. As Mrs. Thornton, robust and vigorous with life, stepped inside, Mrs. Hale remained still, though clearly conscious of her guest's presence. For a moment or two, the sick woman did not open her eyes. Heavy moisture gathered on her lashes before she finally raised them, reaching out a trembling hand beneath the covers to grasp Mrs. Thornton's large, firm fingers.

"My dear friend," Mrs. Hale whispered, barely audible, "you have a daughter. My sister is in Italy. My child will be without a mother—in a strange land—if I die. Will you?"

Her gaze, filmy and wandering, fixed itself on Mrs. Thornton's face with an intensity of wistfulness. For a minute, Mrs. Thornton's features remained rigid, stern, and unmoved; save for the slow gathering of tears in the dying woman's eyes, it appeared as if a dark cloud crossed the visitor's cold visage. It was not a thought of her son or her living daughter, Fanny, that finally softened her heart, but a sudden remembrance triggered by the arrangement of the room: a little daughter, lost in infancy long years prior. Like a sudden sunbeam, this memory melted the icy crust behind which a real, tender woman lay dormant.

"You wish me to be a friend to Miss Hale?" Mrs. Thornton asked, her voice measured, refusing to soften despite the turmoil within.

Mrs. Hale, her eyes locked on her friend's face, pressed the hand lying beside hers on the coverlet. She could not speak further. Mrs. Thornton sighed. "I will be a true friend, if circumstances require it. Not a tender friend—that I cannot be," she added hastily, cutting off her intended remark about the recipient, "for it is not my nature to show affection even where I feel it, nor do I volunteer advice in general. Still, at your request—and if it brings you any comfort—I will promise." A pause followed. Mrs. Thornton was too conscientious to pledge what she did not intend to fulfill, and performing acts of kindness toward Margaret, whom she currently despised, seemed almost impossible.

"I promise," she said, with grave severity, a tone that somehow inspired the dying woman with faith more stable than flickering life itself. "I promise that in any difficulty in which Miss Hale—"

"Call her Margaret!" gasped Mrs. Hale.

"In which she comes to me for help, I will assist her with every power I possess, as if she were my own daughter. I also promise that if ever I see her doing what I deem wrong—"

"But Margaret never does wrong—not wilfully wrong," pleaded Mrs. Hale.

Mrs. Thornton continued, ignoring the plea: "If ever I see her doing what I believe to be wrong—such wrong not touching me or mine, in which case I might be accused of bias—I will tell her of it, faithfully and plainly, as I should wish for my own daughter."

A long silence ensued. Mrs. Hale felt that this promise excluded many things, yet it was significant. It contained reservations she could not decipher, but she was too weak, dizzy, and exhausted to press further. Mrs. Thornton reviewed the probable scenarios in which she had bound herself to act, deriving a fierce pleasure from the prospect of delivering unwelcome truths to Margaret in the shape of duty. Mrs. Hale began to speak again.

"I thank you. I pray God to bless you. I shall never see you again in this world. But my last words are: I thank you for your promise of kindness to my child."

"Not kindness!" Mrs. Thornton testified, ungraciously truthful to the end. Having soothed her conscience with these words, she was not sorry they went unheard. She pressed Mrs. Hale's soft, languid hand, then rose and departed from the house unseen by a single soul.

While Mrs. Thornton conducted this somber interview, Margaret and Dixon were huddled together, plotting how to keep Frederick's impending arrival a profound secret from everyone outside the house. A letter from him was expected daily, and he would undoubtedly follow swiftly. Martha must be dispatched on a holiday; Dixon must maintain a stern guard at the front door, admitting only the rare visitors who frequented Mr. Hale's downstairs room, Mrs. Hale's critical illness providing ample justification. If Mary Higgins assisted Dixon in the kitchen, she was to hear and see as little of Frederick as possible, referred to as Mr. Dickinson if necessary. Her sluggish, incurious nature served as the ultimate safeguard.

They resolved that Martha should depart that very afternoon. Margaret wished she had left the previous day, fearing it might seem odd to grant a servant a holiday amidst her mistress's dire need for attendance. Throughout the afternoon, poor Margaret acted as a Roman daughter, draining her scanty reserves of strength to support her father. Mr. Hale oscillated between hope and despair between his wife's attacks, buoying himself up during brief respites and believing in ultimate recovery. Consequently, when the paroxysms returned, each more severe than the last, they were fresh agonies and crushing disappointments. Sitting in the drawing-room, unable to bear the solitude of his study or occupy himself, he buried his head in his arms, folded upon the table. Margaret's heart ached witnessing his state; yet, since he remained silent, she hesitated to offer unsolicited comfort. Martha was gone, and Dixon sat vigil with Mrs. Hale while she slept. The house grew still and quiet, darkness descending without the movement to fetch candles. Margaret sat by the window, gazing at the streetlamps and the road, seeing nothing, attuned only to her father's heavy sighs. She dreaded going down for lights, fearing that her withdrawal might release him from tacit restraint into violent emotion without her being nearby to soothe him. Just as she considered attending to the kitchen fire, a muffled doorbell rang with a violent pull, causing the wires to jingle throughout the house. Startled, she rose, passed her father—who showed no reaction to the veiled, dull sound—and returned to kiss him tenderly. He remained unmoved, oblivious to her embrace.

Descending softly through the dark, she found Dixon attempting to engage the chain before opening the door. Margaret, her mind pre-occupied, felt no fear. A man's tall figure stood between her and the luminous street. He looked away, but turned quickly at the sound of the latch.

"Is this Mr. Hale's?" he asked, in a clear, full, delicate voice.

Margaret trembled all over, unable to answer initially. In a moment, she sighed, "Frederick!" stretching out both hands to catch his and draw him inside.

"Oh, Margaret!" he said, holding her off by the shoulders after their kiss, as if in the darkness he could see her face and read a quicker answer to his question than words could give. "My mother—is she alive?"

"Yes, she is alive, dear, dear brother! She—as ill as she can be—she is; but alive! She is alive!"

"Thank God!"

"Papa is utterly prostrate with this great grief."

"You expect me, don't you?"

"No, we have had no letter."

"Then I have come before it. But my mother knows I am coming."

"Oh! we all knew you would come. But wait a little! Step in here. Give me your hand. What is this? Oh! your carpet-bag. Dixon has shut the shutters; but this is papa's study, and I can take you to a chair to rest yourself for a few minutes; while I go and tell him."

She groped toward the taper and lucifer matches. Suddenly,

"Show not that manner, and these features all,
The serpent's cunning, and the sinner's fall?"

The chill, shivering morning of October descended upon Milton not with the gentle, silvery mist of the countryside, which clears before the sun to reveal its vibrant hues, but with the heavy, oppressive fog described by Milton, where sunlight struggles to pierce the gloom, revealing only long, dusky streets. Inside the Hale household, the air was thick with a silence broken only by the occasional sob. Margaret moved through the rooms with a languid, weary step, her eyes constantly blurred by unshed tears. Yet, she found no time to surrender to grief; the weight of responsibility pressed heavily upon her shoulders. With her father and brother consumed by mourning, the practical necessities of the funeral—the arrangements, the guests, the logistics—devolved entirely onto her.

As the fire roared brightly and the tea-kettle sang its cheerful song, signaling readiness for breakfast, Margaret paused to inspect the room one final time. She desired the space to appear as joyful and orderly as possible, a stark, painful contrast to the devastation swirling within her soul. As she knelt beside the sofa, burying her face in the cushions to stifle a cry, a gentle hand touched her shoulder. It was Dixon.

"Come, Miss Hale—come, my dear!" Dixon urged, her voice unusually tender for the stern servant. "You must not give way. If you do collapse, who shall guide us? There is so much to arrange: the funeral details, the invitations, the location. Master Frederick is beside himself with weeping, and his father, poor soul, wanders about as if lost. It is a terrible burden, I know, but death claims us all. You have been spared this loss until now."

Perhaps Dixon was right. Yet, to Margaret, this specific bereavement felt unique, a wound that defied comparison with any other tragedy in the world. Finding no solace in the old woman's words, she was nonetheless moved by Dixon's unexpected gentleness. Driven by a need to repay this kindness, she rose, wiped her cheeks, and offered a fragile smile. "Breakfast is ready," she announced softly, moving to summon Mr. Hale and Frederick.

Her father entered as if in a trance, his movements slow and disconnected, as though his mind resided somewhere beyond the physical realm. Frederick, however, strode in with a forced vitality, seizing Margaret's hand and gazing into her eyes before bursting into fresh tears. Throughout the meal, Margaret struggled to keep the conversation light, filling the air with trivialities to prevent the lingering shadows of their shared memories from surfacing—the strained silence, the desperate listening for a sign from the sickroom, the last meal together.

Afterward, Margaret resolved to discuss the funeral arrangements with her father. He nodded to her suggestions, though they often contradicted one another, offering no real guidance. As she prepared to retreat to consult Dixon, Mr. Hale gently gestured for her to return.

"Ask Mr. Bell," he whispered, his voice hollow.

"Mr. Bell?" she queried, startled. "Of Oxford?"

"Yes," he murmured, sinking back into listlessness. "He was once my groom's-man."

Margaret understood the connection immediately. "I will write today," she decided.

For the remainder of the morning, she labored in a whirlwind of melancholic tasks, longing for rest but finding none. Towards evening, Dixon approached her.

"I have acted, miss. I was truly afraid for Master Frederick; grief threatened to strike him down. He spent the entire day with the late mistress, speaking to her as if she were breathing. When I entered, he was silent, a confused maze. I thought it vital to rouse him. I told him it is unsafe for him to remain here. Furthermore, on Tuesday, while out, I encountered a man from Southampton—the first local visitor I have seen since arriving. It was young Leonards, the draper's son, a notorious scamp who drove his father to madness before fleeing to sea. I never cared for him. He served aboard the *Orion* with Frederick, though I am unsure if he participated in the mutiny."

"Did he recognize you?" Margaret asked, leaning forward eagerly.

"That is the trouble," Dixon replied. "Had he not called out my name, I am certain he would not have known me. Being a stranger here, I might have feigned kinship, but I am not so foolish. He addressed me as 'Miss Dixon,' and I reminded him politely that I am still unmarried, though I have had chances if I had not been so particular. He was civil enough, asking after my father whom I had helped to employ George, despite having thrown him out. To spite me, seeing how civil we were, he began to inquire about Master Frederick. He spoke of Frederick's scrapes, suggesting he would be hanged for mutiny, mentioning the hundred-pound reward, and calling him a disgrace to his family. He did this merely to annoy me, for I had previously helped secure employment for his father."

"But you did not reveal our whereabouts? About Frederick?"

"Not a word," Dixon assured her. "However, before he departed, he turned back and said, 'If you can assist in trapping Lieutenant Hale, Miss Dixon, we shall go partners in the reward. I know you would like to be my partner, would you not?' Then he boarded the bus, leaving me with a leering, wicked smile."

Margaret felt a sharp pang of discomfort. "Have you told Frederick?"

"No," Dixon admitted. "I was uneasy knowing Leonards was in town, but the funeral preparations consumed my thoughts. Seeing Master Frederick so rigid and glum, I realized that the threat of Leonards might finally snap him out of his stupor. He must leave before Mr. Bell arrives."

"Oh, I am not afraid of Mr. Bell," Margaret said, her voice tightening. "I am terrified of this Leonards. I must inform Frederick. What does he look like?"

"A disreputable figure," Dixon described. "Red whiskers that would shame anyone, dressed in coarse fustian like a laborer, despite claiming a confidential position."

It became evident that Frederick must depart. He had already claimed his rightful place within the family, vowing to support his father and sister, yet now the specter of Leonards loomed. As Margaret sat by the drawing-room fire, watching her father pace restlessly under the pressure of this new fear, Frederick entered. The frantic violence of his grief had subsided, replaced by a dim, somber resolve.

"How pale you look, Margaret," he said quietly. "You have thought for everyone, and no one has thought for you. Lie down on this sofa; there is nothing left for you to do."

"That is the hardest part," Margaret whispered sadly. Yet, she obeyed, lying down as Frederick covered her feet with a shawl and sat beside her. In the subdued tones of their conversation, Margaret recounted Dixon's encounter. Frederick's lips tightened in dismay.

"I would dearly like to confront that young fellow," he muttered. "A worse sailor never sailed, nor a worse man. Consider the circumstances: when every decent sailor was indignant at our captain, he sought favor. Pah! And here he is. If he knew I was within twenty miles, he would ferret me out to settle old scores. I would rather anyone possessed the hundred pounds they claim I am worth than that rascal. What a pity Dixon could not be convinced to provide for her old age!"

"Hush, Frederick!" Margaret pleaded.

Just then, Mr. Hale approached, his face aglow with tremors and eagerness. Having overheard their discussion, he seized Frederick's hand.

"My boy, you must go. It is perilous, but necessary. You have done all you could; you have been a comfort to her."

"Papa, must he go?" Margaret begged, fighting against the conviction rising within her.

"I declare, I have a mind to face the music and stand my trial. If I could only gather my evidence! I cannot bear to be in the power of such a blackguard as Leonards. In other circumstances, I might have enjoyed this stolen visit—it possesses all the charm the Frenchwoman attributed to forbidden pleasures."

"One of the earliest memories I have," Margaret interjected gently, "is yours being in great disgrace for stealing apples. We had trees laden with fruit, yet someone told you stolen fruit tastes sweetest. You took it literally and went a-robbing. Your feelings have not changed much since then."

"Yes, you must go," Mr. Hale insisted, his focus singular, struggling to follow his children's zigzagging thoughts.

Margaret and Frederick exchanged a glance. A fleeting, profound sympathy passed between them, a bond that would vanish if he departed. So much was understood without words. Frederick broke the spell first.

"Do you know, Margaret, I was nearly frightened out of my wits this afternoon? I was in my bedroom, hearing a ring at the front door. Thinking the caller had long gone, I intended to emerge, but opening my door, I saw Dixon descending the stairs. She frowned and kicked me back into hiding. I kept the door ajar and heard a man receive a message in Father's study, then depart. Who could it have been? Perhaps some shopmen?"

"Likely," Margaret said indifferently. "There was a small man who came for orders around two o'clock."

"But this was not a small man—a powerful giant

"What! To remain denounce-worthy—dragged, perhaps, in chains?"

For the entirety of the following day, the three sat together in silence. Mr. Hale spoke only when his children pressed him, forcing him, as it were, into the present moment. Frederick's grief, having spent its initial fury, now settled into a heavy, silent shame; he was acutely aware of how utterly he had been worn down by emotion, yet beneath this surface pride lay a sorrow for his lost mother that was profound and enduring, destined to haunt his days without utterance. Margaret, though less volatile in her first shock, now suffered a deeper, more lingering pain. Tears frequently clouded her eyes, and a mournful tenderness clung to her speech, intensified whenever her gaze met Frederick's and her thoughts turned to his imminent departure. Yet, despite her personal anguish, she found relief in his leaving, driven by a fierce, protective love for her father. The terror that seized Mr. Hale, fearing his son's capture, had grown since Mrs. Hale's death, feeding exclusively on his obsessive dread. He started at every unfamiliar noise and refused comfort unless Frederick sat hidden from the sight of anyone entering the room.

Towards evening, Mr. Hale broke the silence. "Will you accompany Frederick to the station, Margaret? I must know he has departed safely. Promise me you will confirm he has cleared Milton, at the least?"

"Certainly," Margaret replied softly. "I shall like it, Father, if you will not be lonely without me."

"No, no!" he protested, his voice tight with anxiety. "Unless you tell me you have seen him off, I shall constantly imagine someone knows of him and has stopped him. Go to the Outwood station. It is equally near, yet sparsely populated. Hire a cab there; there is less likelihood of him being spotted. What hour does your train depart, Fred?"

"Ten minutes past six. Nearly dark. Pray, what shall you do, Margaret?"

"Oh, I can manage," she assured him, her voice gaining a steeliness born of necessity. "I am growing braver, harder. The road home is well-lit, even if darkness falls. Last week, I walked out much later."

Margaret breathed a sigh of relief when the final farewell between the dead mother and the living father concluded. She hurried Frederick into a cab, eager to curtail a scene that she knew would tear her father apart further, who was already terribly shaken. Due to a common error in the Railway Guide regarding arrival times at minor stations, coupled with the cab's delay, they arrived at Outwood with nearly twenty minutes to spare. The booking office was closed, precluding the purchase of tickets. Instead, they descended the steps to the ground level, traversing a broad path of cinders that cut diagonally across a field bordering the carriage road. There, they paced back and forth, wasting away the fleeting moments allotted to them.

Margaret's hand rested within Frederick's arm, and he clasped it affectionately. "Margaret! I intend to consult Mr. Lennox concerning the possibility of clearing my name, thereby securing liberty to return to England whenever I please. This endeavor is undertaken chiefly for your sake, lest anything should befall my father. His state is dreadful; he looks profoundly altered. I beseech you to persuade him to entertain the Cadiz scheme. Consider: what could you endure if he were taken away? You possess no friends nearby; our circle of relations is curiously barren."

Margaret struggled to stifle tears at the tender, piercing anxiety with which Frederick laid before her a spectre she feared was inevitable, given the toll the last few months had exacted from Mr. Hale. Yet, she rallied her spirits. "Two years have witnessed such strange, unforeseen transformations in my life that I feel it futile to calculate precisely what I ought to do should some future catastrophe occur. I strive to attend solely to the present."

She paused. They stood motionless beside the stile leading into the road, bathed in the dying light of the setting sun. Frederick held her hand, his gaze fixed on hers with wistful, searching intensity, reading there a depth of care and trouble that her words failed to convey. Continuing, she said, "We shall correspond frequently, and I promise—for I perceive it will soothe your mind—that I will disclose every tribulation I encounter. Father is..." She hesitated, giving a barely perceptible start. Frederick felt the sudden twitch of her hand and instantly turned his face toward the road. Along the path, a horseman rode slowly, passing directly by the stile where they stood. Margaret bowed, and received a stiff, curt return.

"Who is that?" Frederick demanded, before the rider vanished from earshot.

"A gentleman named Mr. Thornton," Margaret replied, her cheeks slightly flushed, her spirit somewhat drooping. "You have encountered him before."

"Merely his back. An unprepossessing figure. What a scowl!"

"Some grievous matter has vexed him," Margaret offered, her tone apologetic. "You would scarcely deem him unprepossessing had you beheld him with Mother."

"I suppose it is high time I procure my ticket. Had I foreseen the darkness, we need not have returned the cab, Margaret."

"Oh, do not fret about that," she interjected gently. "I may hire a cab here, or return via the railway, where shops, people, and lamps line the entire route from Milton. Think not of me; safeguard yourself. I am ill with the thought that Leonards may share your carriage. Examine the interior thoroughly before boarding."

They retraced their steps to the station. Margaret insisted on entering the glare of the gas lamps within to secure her ticket. Several idle young men loitered near the station master. Margaret believed she had glimpsed the countenance of one of them previously and returned a proud, haughty glance of offended dignity against his impudent stare of undisguised admiration. Rushing to her brother, who stood outside, she seized his arm. "Have you secured your luggage? Let us wander upon the platform," she said, her voice fluttering slightly at the prospect of imminent solitude, her newfound courage leaking away faster than she wished to admit.

A footstep followed them across the flagstones, halting simultaneously with theirs. Both froze, gazing down the tracks and listening for the whistle of the approaching train. Neither spoke; their hearts swelled with a tumultuous fullness. In another moment, the train would arrive; in a minute, Frederick would be gone. Margaret almost repented the haste with which she had urged him toward London; it appeared to court additional risks of discovery. Had he sailed for Spain via Liverpool, he might have escaped within hours.

Frederick turned, facing the bright flare of the gas lamp, which burned with vivid anticipation of the train. A man dressed as a railway porter stepped forward—a disreputable figure who appeared drunk into a state of brutishness, though his faculties remained sharp.

"By your leave, miss!" he barked, shoving Margaret roughly aside and seizing Frederick by the collar. "Your name is Hale, I presume?"

Before Margaret could comprehend how it occurred, her vision swam as everything danced before her eyes, Frederick executed a swift, practiced maneuver, tripping the man. With a heavy thud, the intruder crashed onto the platform, falling from the three-foot elevation down to the soft earth beside the railroad. He lay there, stunned.

"Run, run!" Margaret gasped. "The train is here! Was it Leonards? Oh, run! I will carry your bag!"

Seizing his arm, she pushed him forward with all her feeble strength. A carriage door swung open; he leaped inside. As he leaned out, shouting, "God bless you, Margaret!" the train surged past her, leaving her standing alone and shattered. A profound sickness and faintness overwhelmed her, making her grateful to retreat into the ladies' waiting-room and collapse into a seat for a brief respite. Initially, she could only gasp for air. The urgency, the sickening alarm, the hairbreadth escape—it all crashed over her. If the train had not arrived at that precise second, the man would have risen and summoned aid to arrest him. She wondered if he had regained his footing, whether she had seen him stir, if he had sustained serious injury. Venturing out, the platform was brightly lit yet utterly deserted. She moved to the edge and peered over cautiously. No one was there. Relief washed over her, for otherwise, terrible nightmares would surely haunt her sleep. Even so, trembling and affrighted, she felt incapable of walking home along the lonely, dark road, illuminated only by the distant blaze of the station. She resolved to wait for the down train and ride it home. But what if Leonards recognized her as Frederick's companion? She scanned the area before risking entry into the booking-office to purchase her ticket. Only railway officials lingered, speaking loudly amongst themselves.

"So Leonards has been drinking again!" declared one, appearing to hold authority. "He'll require all his boasted influence to retain his position this time."

"Where is he?" asked another. Margaret, her back turned, counted her change with trembling fingers, terrified to rotate until she heard the response.

"I know not. He entered but five minutes prior, weaving a lengthy tale of some fall he'd suffered, swearing furiously. He sought to borrow funds to reach London by the next up-train. He made all manner of tipsy promises, yet I had other matters to attend to. I told him to attend to his

The silence within the Hale household felt unnatural, a heavy, suffocating void left in the wake of such terrifying commotion. Mr. Hale, having ensured every necessity for his daughter's return, sat once more in his customary chair, sinking into one of those somber, wakeful reveries that often plagued him. Dixon had enlisted Mary Higgins to manage the kitchen, directing her with a low, fierce whisper; her rebukes carried an energy born of reverence, for speaking loudly would have seemed sacrilegious while a corpse lay in the house. Margaret resolved not to speak of the final, crowning terror to her father. To discuss it would serve no purpose; it had ended, albeit tragically. Now, her greatest fear was not the death itself, but that Leonard would somehow secure funds to follow Frederick to London and hunt him down. Yet, the odds were immense against any such scheme succeeding. Margaret determined not to torment herself with thoughts of what she could not prevent. Frederick would be as vigilant as she could make him, and within a day or two, he would be safely out of England.

"I suppose we shall hear from Mr. Bell tomorrow," Margaret said softly.

"Yes," replied her father, his voice flat. "I suppose so."

"If he can come, he will be here by evening, I should think."

"If he cannot, I shall ask Mr. Thornton to accompany me to the funeral. I cannot go alone. I think I would break down utterly."

"Don't ask Mr. Thornton, Papa. Let me go with you," Margaret implored, her tone sudden and earnest.

"You? My dear, ladies do not generally attend funerals."

"No!" she interrupted, her eyes flashing. "Because they cannot control themselves. Women of our class do not go because we lack the power to govern our emotions, yet we are ashamed to display them. Poor women go, and they do not care if they are seen overcome with grief. But I promise you, Papa, if you allow me, I will cause you no trouble. Do not send a stranger and leave me out. If Mr. Bell cannot come, I *must* go. I will not press this wish against your will if he does arrive."

Mr. Bell could not come. A severe attack of gout kept him confined. Yet, his letter was profoundly affectionate, brimming with genuine sorrow for his absence. He expressed hope to visit them soon, though his Milton estate required immediate attention, and his agent insisted his presence was mandatory. Or perhaps, he had avoided Milton as long as possible, and now the sole solace for this unavoidable journey was the faint hope that seeing his old friend might offer him some comfort.

Margaret found it nearly impossible to dissuade her father from inviting Mr. Thornton. An indescribable repulsion rose within her at the prospect. On the eve of the funeral, a stately note arrived from Mrs. Thornton to Miss Hale. Written at her son's request, it declared that their carriage would attend the ceremony, provided it did not offend the family. Margaret tossed the note onto her father's lap.

"Oh, let us avoid these formalities," she pleaded. "Let us go alone, Papa. You and I. They do not care for us, or else he would have offered to walk himself, rather than propose sending an empty carriage."

"I thought you were so extremely averse to his company, Margaret," Mr. Hale said, surprised.

"And so I am," she admitted, her voice trembling. "I do not want him to come at all; I should especially dislike the humiliation of asking him. But this... it feels like a mockery of mourning, and I did not expect such courtesy from him." Suddenly, she burst into tears. Having been so subdued in her grief, so considerate of others, so gentle and patient, her father could not comprehend her sudden agitation. She seemed restless, unable to sit still, and despite the tenderness he lavished upon her, her crying only grew louder.

That night proved disastrous. Margaret slept fitfully, unprepared for the fresh anxiety brought by a letter from Frederick. Mr. Lennox was out of town, returning only by Tuesday, perhaps Monday. Concluding it prudent to wait, Frederick decided to remain in London for a couple of extra days. He had considered coming down to Milton again; the temptation had been overwhelming. However, the lingering presence of Mr. Bell in his father's house, coupled with the alarming incident at the railway station moments prior, had hardened his resolve to stay put. Margaret could assure herself that he took every precaution against being tracked by Leonard. Relief washed over her as she realized her father was absent in his mother's room; if he had been present, she would have been forced to read the letter aloud. It would have triggered a state of nervous alarm in him that she would have struggled to quell.

There was more than the mere fact of Frederick's detention that troubled her. The letter contained allusions to the recognition at Milton and the possibility of a pursuit, chilling her blood to the bone. How would this affect her father? Regret gnawed at her. Many times she reproached herself for urging the plan to consult Mr. Lennox. At the time, it had seemed to involve so little delay, adding so little to the already slim chances of detection. Yet, every subsequent event had proven it undeniably undesirable. Margaret battled fiercely against this regret, against the sting of self-reproach for saying what then seemed wise but now appeared foolhardy. But her father, his mind and body too depressed to rise above the darkness, would succumb to these seeds of morbid reflection. Margaret summoned every ounce of strength to aid him.

Her father seemed to have forgotten they awaited news from Frederick. He was consumed by a single, crushing thought: the last visible token of his wife's presence was about to be removed from him, hidden forever from his sight. He trembled pitifully as the undertaker arranged the black crepe drapery around him. He looked wistfully at Margaret, and when finally released, he tottered toward her, his voice a broken murmur. "Pray for me, Margaret. I have no strength left. I cannot pray. I give her up because I must. I try to bear it, truly I do. I know it is God's will. But I cannot understand why she died. Pray for me, Margaret, that I may find faith to pray. It is a great strait, my child."

Sitting beside him in the carriage, Margaret supported him almost entirely in her arms. She repeated every noble verse of holy comfort, every text of faithful resignation she could recall. Her voice never faltered, and strangely, she drew strength from the act of offering it. Her father's lips moved along with hers, echoing the familiar texts as her words suggested them. It was terrible to witness the patient, struggling effort to achieve a resignation he lacked the inner strength to accept as part of himself.

Margaret's fortitude nearly shattered when Dixon, with a subtle gesture, pointed out Nicholas Higgins and his daughter standing apart, watching the ceremony with deep, silent attention. Nicholas wore his usual fustian, save for a scrap of black cloth sewn around his hat—a mark of mourning he had never worn for his daughter, Bessy. But Mr. Hale saw nothing. He continued to repeat the funeral service mechanically in his mind, sighing twice or thrice as the clergyman finished, before placing a hand on Margaret's arm. He silently entreated to be led away, acting as if blind, trusting her as his faithful guide.

Dixon sobbed aloud, covering her face with a handkerchief, so lost in her own grief that she failed to notice the crowd dispersing until someone spoke to her nearby. It was Mr. Thornton. He had stood there the entire time, bowed head, hidden behind a cluster of mourners, unrecognized.

"I beg your pardon," he said, his voice steady. "Can you tell me how Mr. Hale is? And Miss Hale?"

"Of course, sir," Dixon replied, wiping her eyes. "They are as one might expect. Master is terribly broke down. Miss Hale bears up better than likely."

Mr. Thornton wished, with a cruel selfishness, to hear that she suffered the natural sorrow of loss. First, there was enough pride in him to derive a twisted pleasure from the notion that his great love might come to comfort her—a sensation akin to a mother's joy when a drooping infant nestles close, dependent and safe. This delicious vision of what might have been, in which he had indulged only days ago despite her repulsions, was miserably shattered by the recollection of what he had seen near Outwood Station. "Miserably disturbed" was not strong enough. He was haunted by the image of the handsome young man, standing with her in an attitude of such familiar, easy confidence. The memory shot through him like an agony, forcing him to clench his hands tightly to subdue the pain.

At that late hour, so far from home! It took a monumental moral effort to revive his trust—once so perfect—in Margaret's purity and innocence. As soon as that effort ceased, the trust collapsed, falling dead and powerless. Wild, frantic fantasies chased one another through his mind. Here was a piece of miserable, gnawing confirmation. "She bore up better than likely." She must have some hope to look to, something so bright it could lighten the dark hours for a daughter newly bereaved. Yes! He knew how she would love. He had not loved her without gaining that instinctive knowledge of her capabilities. Her soul would walk in glorious sunlight if any man, worthy by his power to love, could win it back. Even in mourning, she would rest upon a peaceful

"Truth will fail thee never, never!
Though thy bark be tempest-driven,
Though each plank be rent and riven,
Truth will bear thee on for ever!"

For Margaret, the burden of existing "better than likely" was a crushing weight. Sometimes, amidst the cheerful facades she maintained with her father, a sudden, jagged thought pierced through her: she was alone. She had lost her mother, and now, perhaps, she was losing everything else. The silence regarding Frederick was a physical ache. The Sunday post had failed to deliver news from London, and by Tuesday, the absence of a letter left her in a state of paralyzed dread. Frederick's future was a blank, and her father's misery manifested in a restlessness that shattered his recent habits. Where he used to sit quietly for hours, he now paced the floor, traversed the rooms, and flapped the bedroom doors open and shut with a frantic, aimless energy. Margaret, exhausted from trying to soothe him with books she could not read, breathed a sigh of relief when Mr. Thornton arrived. His presence acted as an anchor, forcing her father's wandering mind back to solid ground.

Mr. Thornton entered the drawing-room with a directness that bypassed formalities. He stepped immediately to Mr. Hale, grasping his hands and holding them tight, a silent, profound communication of sorrow passing between them that needed no words. His gaze, heavy with empathy, softened as he turned to Margaret. He had intended to maintain the cool reserve of his previous visits, yet the sight of her shattered composure broke his resolve. She stood apart, her stately beauty dimmed by sleepless nights and weeping. Her face bore the marks of gentle, enduring sadness, yet beneath it lay a raw, present suffering. As he approached, his voice dropped to a tender whisper, causing tears to spill instantly. She turned away, hiding her face, and sat down, picking up her work as if it were a shield. To Mr. Thornton, watching her, the harsh realities of Outwood Lane vanished; his heart beat fast, consumed entirely by the girl before him. He spoke to Mr. Hale with a warmth and authority that made the older man feel secure, a feeling Margaret observed with grateful relief.

Suddenly, the door opened, and Dixon stood there, his face pale and flustered. "Miss Hale, you are wanted." The alarm in Dixon's voice sent a chill through Margaret's stomach. Something had happened to Frederick. She prayed silently that her father and Mr. Thornton were too engrossed in conversation to notice her departure.

"What is it, Dixon?" she asked, her voice barely audible.

"Come this way, miss," Dixon urged, ushering her into what had become her chamber following Mrs. Hale's death. "It's nothing, miss," he choked out, struggling for composure. "Only a police-inspector. He wishes to speak with you. I... I told him it might be trivial."

"Do you know why?" Margaret whispered, stepping past him.

"No, miss. He merely asked if you resided here. Martha admitted him, showing him into Master Thornton's study. I offered to explain, but he insisted on seeing you."

As Margaret reached for the handle of the study door, she paused. "Take care, Papa does not come down. Mr. Thornton is with him."

Inside, the air felt thick and oppressive. The inspector, a man of stern demeanor, was momentarily daunted by her entrance. Yet, as she faced him, her countenance transformed. Indignation, carefully suppressed, lent her features a superb, disdainful air. There was no flicker of surprise, no trace of curiosity. She awaited his words with a stillness that bordered on the supernatural. She offered no questions, only a silent, iron vigilance.

"I beg your pardon, ma'am," the inspector began, his tone professional yet grave. "My duty compels me to ask a few plain questions. A man has died at the Infirmary following a fall at Outwood Station, occurring Thursday evening, the twenty-sixth, between five and six o'clock. Initially, the fall appeared minor, but physicians attribute the fatality to an underlying internal condition and the deceased's habitual consumption of alcohol."

Margaret's large, dark eyes widened slightly, locking onto his face. Her lips tightened into a severe, unyielding line. She did not blink, nor did she tremble. When he hesitated, waiting for her reaction, she spoke, her voice flat and devoid of emotion, as if reciting a script rather than answering a charge. "Well—go on!"

"There is reason to believe an inquest will be necessary," the inspector continued, his voice steady. "Evidence suggests the fall resulted from a blow, a push, or a scuffle. It appears the deceased was provoked by the insolent remarks of a half-intoxicated individual toward a young lady accompanying a gentleman. An eyewitness on the platform observed the altercation, though dismissed it as inconsequential given the slight nature of the initial impact."

"And the witness identified the lady?" Margaret asked, her tone challenging.

"There is strong probability," the inspector replied, leaning forward slightly. "The gentleman and lady were seen near the platform at the relevant hour. The lady was described as remarkably handsome. A local grocer's assistant, present at the scene, identified her as Miss Hale of Crampton. Furthermore, the aggressor, Leonard, fled the scene in a fury, seeking solace in a gin-palace. Witnesses there recall him cursing himself for failing to utilize the electric telegraph—a grievance he attributed to a 'Cornish trick' that had cost him a hundred pounds. Overcome by pain and drink, he collapsed in the road, where he was discovered by the police. He remained delirious, unable to coherently recount the events, save for ramblings of ships and captains, ending with a curse against the Cornish deception."

The inspector paused, studying her composed face. "Thus, madam, I seek confirmation of your denial regarding your presence at the station, accompanying the gentleman who delivered the fatal blow?"

A sharp, searing pain lanced through Margaret's brain. *If only I knew Frederick was safe.* For a fleeting second, the torture of truth twisted her features, a glimpse of agony visible to a keen observer. But the inspector, practical and shallow, missed the depth of her distress. He noted only the mechanical, unvaried quality of her response, as if she were trapped behind a wall of stone, unable to shift her words.

"I was not there," she said, slowly, heavily. Her eyes remained fixed on him, glassy and dreamlike, refusing to close or soften.

The inspector's suspicions sharpened. This was not the natural cadence of a truthful denial; it sounded like a rehearsed falsehood, a statement forced out of her despite the obvious contradiction. He closed his notebook deliberately, looking up at her. She stood like an ancient statue, unmoving, her color unchanged, her pride erect.

"I trust you will not deem me impertinent," he said, "if I inform you that I may need to summon you to appear at the inquest. If my sole witness persists in identifying you, you will be required to provide an alibi." He looked at her sharply, expecting a flicker of guilt, a flush of shame. Instead, she remained perfectly still. He was taken aback by her sheer composure. It must be a mistake of identity.

"It is highly improbable I shall require further action," he concluded politely. "I hope you will forgive me for fulfilling my duties, however tedious they may appear."

As the inspector moved toward the door, Margaret bowed her head, her lips dry and stiff, incapable of forming a farewell. Suddenly, a passionate impulse seized her. She rose, walked to the study door, and unlocked it, allowing him to pass. She followed him to the front door of the house, throwing it wide open for his exit. She watched him leave with that same hollow, fixed gaze until he crossed the threshold. Only then did she turn back, locking the study door from within.

She retreated to the study, pausing, then tottering forward. She stopped again, swaying violently for a heartbeat, and collapsed to the floor, sinking into a dead swoon.

"There is naught so finely spun
But it cometh to the sun."

Mr. Thornton sat on and on. He perceived that his company afforded genuine solace to Mr. Hale, and he was moved by the half-formed, persistent plea that he stay a little longer—the soft, earnest "Do not go yet" which his friend uttered repeatedly. He wondered why Margaret had not returned, yet he lingered not with any hope of seeing her. In the presence of a man who so acutely felt the vanity of earthly things, Mr. Thornton was reasonable and self-commanded. He listened with deep interest to everything his father-in-law said regarding death, the heavy slumber, and the dulled brain.

It was remarkable how Mr. Thornton's presence unlocked secrets within Mr. Hale that remained barred even from Margaret. Perhaps because she would sympathize so keenly, displaying emotions so vivid, Hale feared the reaction upon himself; perhaps because his speculative nature generated endless doubts at such a solemn time, pleading to be resolved into certainty, knowing she would shrink from expressing them—or from hearing him deemed capable of conceiving them. Whatever the cause, Mr. Thornton became a confessor to whom he could unburden the frozen thoughts and terrors of his mind better than anyone else. Mr. Thornton spoke little, yet every sentence he uttered fortified Mr. Hale's reliance and affection. Did Hale pause in expressing some remembered agony? Two or three words from Mr. Thornton completed the sentence, revealing how deeply its meaning resonated. Was it a doubt, a fear, a wandering uncertainty seeking rest but finding none, its eyes blinded by tears? Instead of shock, Mr. Thornton appeared to have traversed that very stage of thought himself, able to suggest precisely where the ray of light lay to illuminate the dark places. Though a man of action, busy in the world's great struggle, there bound Mr. Thornton to God a deeper religion in his heart, persisting through all his mistakes and strong wilfulness, than Mr. Hale had ever imagined. They never spoke of such matters again, as fate decreed, but this single conversation rendered them peculiar souls to one another, knitting them together in a bond that loose, indiscriminate discourse on sacred things could never forge.

Throughout this time, Margaret lay as still and white as death upon the study floor. She had succumbed under her burden. It had been heavy in weight and long carried; she had been meek and patient until, all at once, her faith faltered, and she groped in vain for aid. A pitiful contraction of suffering marred her beautiful brow, though no other sign of consciousness remained. Her mouth, which had recently projected sullenly in defiance, was now relaxed and livid.

*"As if from her lips moves a spirit gentle and full of love,
Who goes saying to the soul: 'Sigh'!"*

The first sign of returning life was a quivering around the lips—a silent, mute attempt at speech—but the eyes remained closed, and the quivering sank back into stillness. Then, leaning feebly on her arms for a moment to steady herself, Margaret gathered her strength and rose. Her comb had fallen from her hair. With an intuitive desire to erase the traces of weakness and restore order, she sought for it, occasionally sitting down to regain strength during the search. Her head drooped forward, her hands meekly laid one upon the other. She endeavored to recall the force of her temptation by trying to remember the details that had thrown her into such terror, but she could not. She grasped only two facts: that Frederick had been in danger of being pursued and detected in London, not merely for manslaughter, but as the unpardonable leader of the mutiny, and that she had lied to save him. Yet there was comfort: her lie had saved him, at least by gaining time. If the inspector returned tomorrow, after she received the letter she longed for assuring her of her brother's safety, she would brave shame and stand in bitter penance—"the lofty Margaret"—acknowledging before a crowded justice hall, if need be, that she had been "like a dog, and done this thing." But if he returned before she heard from Frederick, if he came back as he had half-threatened, within a few hours! She would tell the lie again. Though she did not know how the words would emerge, after such a terrible pause for reflection and self-reproach, without betraying her falsehood, she knew it would gain time for Frederick.

She was roused by Dixon's entrance into the room; she had just been letting Mr. Thornton out.

He had hardly taken ten steps into the street before a passing omnibus stopped close by, and a man alighted, approaching him and touching his hat. It was the police inspector.

Mr. Thornton had secured him his first position in the police and had heard from time to time of his protégé's progress, but they had seldom met, and initially, Mr. Thornton did not recognize him.

"My name is Watson—George Watson, sir, whom you employed..."

"Aye, yes! Why, you are getting on famously, I hear."

"Yes, sir. I ought to thank you, sir. But there is a little matter of business for which I made bold to approach you. I believe you were the magistrate who attended to take down the deposition of a poor man who died in the Infirmary last night."

"Yes," replied Mr. Thornton. "I went and heard some sort of a rambling statement, which the clerk said was of no great use. I'm afraid he was but a drunken fellow, though there is no doubt he came to his death by violence at last. One of my mother's servants was engaged to him, I believe, and she is in great distress today. What about him?"

"Why, sir, his death is oddly connected with someone in the house I saw you leaving just now; it was Mr. Hales', I believe."

"Yes!" said Mr. Thornton, turning sharply and looking into the inspector's face with sudden interest. "What about it?"

"Well, sir, it appears to me that I have acquired a fairly distinct chain of evidence incriminating a gentleman who walked with Miss Hale that night at Outwood Station as the man who struck or pushed Leonard off the platform, thereby causing his death. But the young lady denies that she was there at the time."

"Miss Hale denies she was there!" repeated Mr. Thornton in an altered voice. "Tell me, what evening was it? What time?"

"About six o'clock, on the evening of Thursday, the twenty-sixth."

They walked on, side by side, in silence for a minute or two. The inspector broke the quiet first.

"You see, sir, there is likely to be a coroner's inquest; and I have a young man who is pretty positive—at least, he was at first; since he has heard of the young lady's denial, he says he should not like to swear; but still, he is pretty positive that he saw Miss Hale at the station, walking about with a gentleman, not five minutes before the time when one of the porters saw a scuffle, which he attributed to some of Leonard's impudence—but which led to the fall that caused his death. And seeing you emerge from the very house, sir, I thought I might make bold to ask if—you see, it is always awkward dealing with cases of disputed identity, and one does not like to doubt the word of a respectable young woman unless one has strong proof to the contrary."

"And she denied having been at the station that evening!" repeated Mr. Thornton, in a low, brooding tone.

"Yes, sir, twice over, as distinct as could be. I told her I should call again, but seeing you just as I was on my way back from questioning the young man who claimed it was her, I thought I would ask your advice, both as the magistrate who saw Leonard on his death-bed, and as the gentleman who got me my berth in the force."

"You were quite right," said Mr. Thornton. "Take no steps until you have seen me again."

"The young lady will expect me to call, from what I said."

"I only wish to delay you an hour. It is now three. Come to my warehouse at four."

"Very well, sir!"

And they parted. Mr. Thornton hurried to his warehouse, sternly forbidding his clerks to allow anyone to interrupt him, and went to his private room, locking the door. Then he subjected himself to the torture of thinking it all over, realizing every detail. How could he have lulled himself into the unsuspicious calm in which her tearful image had mirrored itself not two hours prior, until he had weakly pitied her and yearned toward her, forgetting the savage, distrustful jealousy with which the sight of her—and unknown to him—at such an hour, in such a place, had inspired him! How could one so pure stoop from her decorous and noble bearing! But was it decorous? Was it? He hated himself for the idea that forced itself upon him, just for an instant—no more—and yet, while it was present, thrilled him with its old potency of attraction toward her image. And then this falsehood—how terrible must be some dread of shame to be revealed? For, after all, the provocation given by a man like Leonard, when excited by drink, might, in all probability, be more than enough to justify anyone who came forward to state the circumstances openly and without reserve! How creeping and deadly that fear which could bow down the truthful Margaret to falsehood! He could almost pity her.

"The steps of the bearers, heavy and slow,
The sobs of the mourners, deep and low."

On the appointed day, Miss Hale and her father set forth to visit Nicholas Higgins and his family. The recent tragedy cast a pall over them both, a somber shadow revealed in their unusual attire and the deliberate nature of their outing, which marked the first time in weeks they had ventured out together without compulsion. Yet, as they approached Higgins's dwelling, a silent, profound sympathy drew them close.

Inside, the atmosphere was heavy with grief. Nicholas Higgins sat in his usual corner by the fireside, though the pipe that usually accompanied him was absent. Leaning heavily on his hand, his arm draped across his knee, he remained seated despite their arrival, though his eyes betrayed a warm, if sorrowful, welcome.

"Sit ye down, sit ye down. Fire's welly out," he muttered, poking the embers vigorously, seemingly attempting to distract attention from his own desolation. He appeared disheveled, a dark, unshaven beard framing a pallid face, clad in a jacket that cried out for repair.

"We thought we'd find you just after dinner-time," Margaret said gently.

"We have shared our sorrows since last seeing you," Mr. Hale replied.

"Ay, ay. Sorrows are more plentiful than dinners these days; I reckon my dinner hour stretches across the whole day. You're likely to find me whenever you seek."

"Are you out of work?" Margaret asked.

"Ay," he answered briefly. After a pause, he looked up, his expression grim. "I'm not lacking for brass. Dunno ye think it. Bess, poor lass, had a little stock under her pillow, ready to slip into my hand at the last moment, and Mary is cutting fustian. But I am out of work nonetheless."

"We owe Mary a debt," Mr. Hale interjected before Margaret could intervene.

"If she accepts it, I'll turn her out of doors!" Higgins snapped, his voice rising. "I'll stay within these four walls, and she must abide outside. That's all."

"But we wish to express our gratitude for her kind service," Mr. Hale persisted.

"I never thanked your daughter for her acts of love towards my poor wench. I never found the words. I must begin now if you start making ado about what little Mary could serve you."

"Is it because of the strike that you are unemployed?" Margaret asked softly.

"The strike is ended. 'Tis over for this time. I am out of work because I never asked for it. And I never asked because good words are scarce, and bad words are plentiful."

His mood was curiously sullen, offering answers that resembled riddles. Yet, Margaret sensed a desire within him for explanation.

"And what are good words?" she pressed.

"Asking for work. I reckon those are nearly the best words men can utter. 'Give me work' implies, 'And I'll do it like a man.' Those are good words."

"And bad words?"

"Ay. Bad words are refusing you work when you ask for it."

"Ay. Bad words are saying, 'Aha, my fine chap! You've been true to your orders, and I'll be true to mine. You did the best you could for those who wanted help; that's your way of being true to your kind: and I'll be true to mine. You've been a poor fool, knowing no better than to be a true, faithful fool. So go and be damned to you. There's no work for you here.' Those are bad words. I am no fool; and if I were, folks ought to have taught me how to be wise in their fashion. I could perhaps have learned, if anyone had tried to teach me."

"It would not be worthwhile," suggested Mr. Hale, "to ask your former master if he would take you back? It might be a poor chance, but it would be a chance."

Higgins lifted his gaze, fixing a sharp, searching look upon the questioner, before letting out a low, bitter titter.

"My master! If it's no offense, I'll ask you a question or two in return."
