"You are quite welcome," Mr. Hale said.

"I reckon you earn your bread somehow. Folks seldom live in Milton merely for pleasure, if they can live elsewhere."

"You are quite right. I possess some independent property, but my intention in settling in Milton was to become a private tutor."

"To teach folk. Well! I reckon they pay you for teaching them, do they not?"

"Yes," Mr. Hale smiled. "I teach in order to receive payment."

"And those who pay you... do they dictate to you? Do they tell you what to do, or what not to do with the money given in fair exchange for your labor?"

"No; certainly not!"

"They do not say, 'You may have a brother, or a friend as dear as a brother, who requires this coin for a purpose both you and he deem right; but you must promise not to give it to him.' You may perceive a worthy use for your funds, yet if we disagree, they simply cease dealing with you?' They do not say that, do they?"

"No; certainly not!"

"Would you endure it if they did?"

"It would require some very hard pressure to induce me to submit to such dictation."

"There is no pressure on all the broad earth that would compel me," declared Nicholas Higgins, his voice gaining strength. "Now you have it. You've hit the bull's-eye. Hampers—that's where I labored—requires their men to pledge themselves they shall not contribute a single penny to aid the Union, nor assist in keeping turn-outs from collapsing. They may make pledges, yes, and break them," he continued with scorn, "but they merely create liars and hypocrites. To me, it is a lesser sin to harden men's hearts so that they refuse kindness to those in need, or to obstruct a righteous and just cause, however it opposes the strong hand. But I shall never forsake myself for all the work the King could offer. I am a member of the Union, and I believe it is the sole institution capable of doing any good for the workmen. I have been a turn-out, and I know the sting of dismissal. Therefore, if I secure a shilling, sixpence shall go to them if they ask for it. Consequently, I do not see where I am to obtain a shilling."

"Is that rule regarding non-contribution to the Union enforced at all the mills?" Margaret inquired.

"I cannot say. It is a new regulation at ours, and I reckon they will discover they cannot sustain it. But it is in force now. By-and-by they will find out: tyrants make liars."

A brief silence fell. Margaret hesitated, weighing whether to speak her mind, fearing she might irritate a man already so gloomy and despondent. Finally, the words escaped her, delivered in a soft tone, her manner reluctant, showing her unwillingness to inflict pain. It did not anger Higgins, but perplexed him.

"Do you recall poor Boucher saying the Union was a tyrant? I think he called it the worst tyrant of all. And I remember agreeing with him at the time."

For a long while, he remained silent. Resting his head on his hands, he stared into the fire, shielding his expression from view.

"I shall not deny that the Union finds it necessary to force a man into his own good. I shall speak truth. A man leads a dreary existence who remains outside the Union. But once inside, his interests are safeguarded better than he could manage for himself, or anyone else could. It is the only path by which working men secure their rights, through collective unity. The greater the number of members, the higher the chance for individual justice. The Government tends to fools and madmen; thus, if any man inclines to harm himself or his neighbor, the Union places a check upon him, whether he likes it or not. That is all we do in the Union. We cannot imprison folk, but we can render a man's life so burdensome that he is compelled to enter, becoming wise and helpful in spite of himself. Boucher was a fool throughout, and never a worse fool than at the end."

"He harmed you?" Margaret asked.

"Aye, that did he. We held public opinion on our side until he and his ilk began rioting and breaking laws. Then it was all over with the strike."

"Then would it not have been far better to leave him alone, rather than forcing him to join the Union? He did you no good; you drove him mad."

"Margaret," her father warned in a low, cautionary tone, sensing the storm gathering on Higgins's face.

"I like her," Higgins said suddenly, his demeanor shifting. "She speaks plainly what is in her mind. She does not comprehend the Union for all that. 'Tis a great power; 'tis our only power. I read a bit of poetry about a plough going over a daisy, which brought tears to my eyes before I had other cause to weep. But the chap never stopped driving the plough, I warrant, for all his pity for the daisy. He possessed too much mother-wit for that. The Union is the plough, preparing the land for harvest-time. As for Boucher—'twould be setting him up too high to liken him to a daisy; he resembles a weed lounging over the ground. He must simply be made up to be put

"A spade! a rake! a hoe!
A pickaxe or a bill!
A hook to reap, or a scythe to mow,
A flail or what ye will—
And here's a ready hand
To ply the needful tool,
And skilled enough, by lessons rough,
In Labour's rugged school."

The following day, Higgins's door remained firmly locked when the sisters sought to pay their respects to the widow Boucher. An officious neighbor, however, revealed that Higgins was indeed at home, though he had visited Mrs. Boucher only briefly before departing on his daily rounds. Their reception proved deeply unsatisfying. Mrs. Boucher viewed herself as a grievously wronged woman, a sentiment born of genuine tragedy yet twisted by a corrosive selfishness. While her "animal" affection for her children existed, it was entirely overshadowed by her preoccupation with her own suffering. She regarded her offspring merely as burdens, her grief consumed by a relentless, querulous focus on her ruined state rather than on them.

Margaret attempted to bridge the gap between mother and children, finding that the latter were far truer mourners than their distracted parent. Through their eager, stammering recollections, they spoke of a kindness and tenderness lost when Daddy fell. Margaret's heart bled watching the mother drag them upstairs to gaze upon their dead, disfigured father. She saw how the coarseness of physical horror was being mingled with the sanctity of natural grief, a mixture that could only deepen their trauma. She tried to redirect their thoughts toward practical duties—how they might care for their mother, how they might honor their father's memory through service. These suggestions resonated more effectively than Mr. Hale's lofty appeals for spiritual reflection. The children, sensing immediate tasks within reach, began to attempt tidying the slatternly room, though Mr. Hale's standards remained too abstract for the indolent widow.

Mrs. Boucher's mind was a fortress of resentment, impervious to reason. She could not conceive of her husband's internal misery leading to the final, terrible step; instead, she saw only the consequences affecting herself. Though she secretly blamed him for his despair, she was inveterate in accusing everyone else—the masters, specifically Mr. Thornton, whose mill had been attacked; the Union, represented by Higgins; the hungry, noisy children—all conspirators in her ruin. She denied any justification for his suicide, yet blamed the world entirely for driving him to it.

Margaret listened to this unreasonableness with growing dismay, leaving her father dispirited when they departed.
"It is the town life," Margaret observed, trying to soothe him. "Their nerves are quickened by the haste and bustle, the constant pressure. Even children, confined in these pent-up houses, suffer from a lack of fresh air and open space. In the country, life is lived outdoors, under the sky."
"But people must live in towns," Mr. Hale countered gently. "And in the country, stagnation sets in, fostering fatalistic habits of mind."
"Yes," Margaret admitted. "Each mode of life breeds its own trials. The townsman struggles to remain patient amidst the chaos, while the country-bred man finds it difficult to summon activity and adapt to sudden emergencies. Both struggle to envision a future: the city-dweller because the present is so overwhelming and immediate, and the rural dweller because the slow pace tempts them to revel in mere animal existence, neglecting the pleasures of ambition and foresight."
"And so, the necessity for engagement in the city, and the dull contentment of the country, produce similar effects of narrow-mindedness," Mr. Hale concluded. "But this poor Mrs. Boucher! How little we can do for her."
"And yet, we dare not abandon her, though our efforts may seem futile," Margaret replied, her voice trembling slightly. "Oh, Papa, it is a hard world to live in!"
"So it is, my child," he said softly. "We feel it acutely now, perhaps, but we have known happiness even in sorrow. What a pleasure Frederick's visit was!"
"Yes, it was," Margaret said, her brightness faltering. "It was so charming, so snatched, so forbidden." She stopped abruptly. The memory of Frederick haunted her, tainted now by her own cowardice. Of all vices, she despised the lack of bravery, the meanness of heart that led to untruth. And she had lied. The shadow of Mr. Thornton's knowledge fell over her. Would he mind detection less if it were anyone else? She imagined telling her Aunt Shaw, Edith, her father, Captain Lennox, and Frederick. With Frederick, the thought was most painful; their mutual regard was still in its first flush. Yet, even falling from his esteem paled before the crushing shame she felt at the prospect of facing Mr. Thornton again.
Still, she longed to see him, to resolve the uncertainty gnawing at her soul. Her cheeks burned as she recalled her earlier defiance regarding trade practices, her implication that commerce inevitably involved deceit. She remembered Mr. Thornton's look of calm disdain, his succinct dismantling of her arguments. He had shown her that in the vast machinery of commerce, dishonesty, however clever, ultimately injured the actor. He had corrected her misuse of "chivalric" with the higher word "Christian," drawing the argument onto himself while she sat silent, harboring a faint, misplaced contempt.
Now, that contempt vanished. No more talk of chivalry. Henceforth, she must feel humiliated in his sight. Her heart leaped at every ring of the bell, only to sink into sickness at each disappointment. Her father clearly expected him, surprised by his absence. They had discussed matters the previous evening that required further time, and it had been understood they would meet again, the first opportunity Mr. Thornton allowed. Mr. Hale, who had paused his teaching due to his wife's illness, grew restless throughout the evening, plagued by the thought that the messenger delivering the book last night might have forgotten a note, or that a message lay waiting today.

"I will go and inquire, Papa," Margaret offered, rising as the bell rang again. "Stay!" she cried, sitting instantly and bending over her work. A footstep sounded on the stairs. It was not a visitor, but Dixon.
Lifting her head, Margaret sighed, a faint gladness warming her.
"It's Higgins, sir," Dixon announced. "He wants to see you, or Miss Hale. Perhaps Miss Hale first, then you, sir; he's in a strange mood."
"He had better come up," Mr. Hale directed. "Then he can choose his audience."
"Oh, very well," Dixon murmured, reluctant to approach the man. "I'd rather not hear what he has to say, sir. If you saw his shoes, you'd think the kitchen floor was cleaner."
"He can wipe them," Mr. Hale said. Dixon hurried away to summon Higgins. When the man arrived, looking at his feet with hesitation, Dixon's irritation softened. Sitting on the bottom stair, Higgins removed his muddy boots and climbed the steps silently.
"Servant, sir!" he muttered, smoothing his hair. "Please forgive me for being in stockings. I've been tramping all day, and the streets aren't exactly clean."
Margaret noted his unusual quietude, the subdued air suggesting great difficulty in speaking. Mr. Hale, ever sympathetic to shyness, stepped in.
"We shall have tea directly, Mr. Higgins. You're tired, surely, after such a wet, relaxing day?"
Margaret hastened the tea, taking the preparation into her own hands despite Dixon's protests. Yet, Martha, like those who eventually touched Margaret's spirit, found it an honor to serve her wishes. Dixon's irritability melted away under Margaret's gentle forbearance.
"Why, Master, and you must always bring the lower classes upstairs since we moved to Milton?" Dixon grumbled. "At Helstone, they never rose above the kitchen. I've told a few they might consider it an honor just to be there."
Finding it easier to speak to one than two, Higgins slipped out after Margaret left to assure himself the door was closed. Standing near Mr. Hale, he lowered his voice.
"Master, you wouldn't guess easily what I've been tramping for today. Remember how I spoke yesterday? I've been seeking work. I told myself I'd keep a civil tongue, let them say what they would. I'd bite my tongue sooner than speak in haste. For *that man's* sake—you understand." He jerked his thumb backward.
"No, I don't," Mr. Hale said, bewildered. "Who?"
"That chap lying there," Higgins whispered. "The one who drowned himself, poor chap! I didn't think he had it in him to lie still and let the water creep over him. Boucher, you know."
"Yes," Mr. Hale said, regaining composure. "Go on. You said you wouldn't speak in haste..."
"For his sake. Yet not for his sake; wherever he is, he'll know no more cold or clammy weather. But for the wife's sake, and the little children."
"God bless you!" Mr. Hale started up, then calmed himself. "What do you mean? Tell me out."
"I've told you," Higgins said, surprised by the agitation. "I wouldn't ask work for myself. But they're a charge on me. I reckon, I would have guided Boucher to a better end. But I set him off the road, so I must answer

"Then proudly, proudly up she rose,
Tho' the tear was in her eye,
Whate'er ye say, think what ye may,
Ye'll get na word frae me!"

To Mr. Thornton, Margaret's transgression was not merely a breach of truth; it was a betrayal of trust that pointed directly to another lover. The memory of that glance, exchanged with a man whose identity he refused to name, burned in his mind with a ferocity that no amount of rationalization could extinguish. He saw the scene replay in his imagination: the dusky twilight, the desolate distance from home, the intimacy that bordered on endearment. While his better judgment whispered that such a meeting might be innocent, the shadow of her deliberate lie crushed that hope. That lie, so unlike her usual candor, proved to him that she possessed a consciousness of guilt regarding some secret attachment. It was a damning evidence that she, despite his fierce love for her, was spiritually bound to another. He chafed under the comparison: she, soft and bewitching with a man who truly held her heart, versus him, rough, stern, and unyielding. He mocked himself for valuing her practical heroism during the riot above the reality of her tender devotion elsewhere.

Consequently, Mr. Thornton found himself consumed by an irritability unknown to him in years. Every interaction felt like a chore, every question demanding a curt, abrupt answer. Though he strove to mask this turmoil with a facade of quiet deliberation, his internal storm manifested in restless pacing throughout the evenings, a habit that grated heavily on his mother's nerves.

"I pray you, sit down," Mrs. Thornton urged, cutting short his endless strides. "We must speak of Betsy."

Mr. Thornton obeyed instantly, perching on a chair by the wall. "Speak."

"Betsy informs me that she must depart," Mrs. Thornton said, her voice tinged with frustration. "Her lover's passing has shattered her spirits; she claims she cannot give her heart to the work anymore."

"And?"

"She adds, however, that she knows the ways of the house intimately. Furthermore, she mentions something concerning your friend, Miss Hale."

"My friend Mr. Hale is not a friend of mine," Mr. Thornton snapped, his composure cracking slightly. "Miss Hale is simply a neighbor."

"If she had been your friend, her words would surely annoy you," Mrs. Thornton pressed gently. "Pray, listen."

Betsy recounted that on the night Leonard died, Miss Hale was seen at the station with a young man whom she believed had pushed or struck Leonard.

"That is false," Mr. Thornton interrupted, his voice rising. "Leonard did not die by violence."

"How do you know?"

"The surgeon of the Infirmary examined the body. He attributed the death to a chronic internal disease aggravated by excessive drinking. The fall, yes, but no blow."

"But Betsy insists there was a push," Mrs. Thornton argued. "And she further states that Woolmer, a grocer's assistant, swore he saw Miss Hale walking with a young man at that precise hour."

"What is this to us?" Mr. Thornton stood abruptly, turning his back to the room, his face a mask of grim stone. "Miss Hale is free to please herself."

"It matters little to you now, John," Mrs. Thornton pleaded, seizing upon the opportunity. "Yet, I made a solemn promise to Mrs. Hale. I vowed I would not let her daughter go astray without advising and remonstrating with her. I intend to fulfill that vow."

"There is no harm in what she did," Mr. Thornton retorted, his shoulders stiff. "Do not compare her to Fanny. Miss Hale possesses weighty reasons for her actions, reasons that justify her conduct in ways I cannot fathom. I believe she guards herself."

"A fine compliment, John!" Mrs. Thornton exclaimed, stepping closer. "You admit he is her lover, do you not?"

"Yes, mother," he said, his voice hollow. "Whatever his name, he is her lover. But..." He turned back, writhing as if in physical pain, leaning his forehead against his hand. "...she may need help. Womanly counsel. There are difficulties, perhaps temptations, that I do not fully grasp. I fear there are. Go to her, Mother. Gain her confidence. Tell her what is best. Some dread tortures her soul."

"For God's sake, John!" Mrs. Thornton cried, shocked by the intensity of his plea. "What do you mean? What accusation have you leveled against her?"

"Not an accusation," he insisted, his eyes blazing. "I *could* not speak against her. But her character... it requires a shield. I have cause to believe she is in straits arising from an attachment that, in itself, is innocent. But the circumstances surrounding it are dire. Never let anyone imply anything worse than that she needs gentle counsel. You promised to be that counselor!"

"No!" Mrs. Thornton declared firmly, her resolve hardening. "I promised counsel and advice, as I would give to my own daughter. I shall speak to her as I would to Fanny if she had gone gallivanting in the dusk. I shall judge her based on facts, not on some mysterious 'strait' you refuse to define. I shall fulfill my promise to Mrs. Hale."

"She will never bear it!" he protested passionately.

"She will have to bear it if I speak in her dead mother's name."

"Well!" He broke away, pacing frantically. "Leave me! I cannot endure this thought. It is better you speak to her than that she remain unheard. Oh, that look of love! And that cursed lie! It revealed a terrible shame she sought to hide from the light in which I believed she lived! Oh, Margaret! Could you not have loved me? I am uncouth, I am hard, but I would never have led you into falsehood."

As Mr. Thornton retreated to his chamber, Mrs. Thornton sat alone, her thoughts turning bitter. The more she reflected on his desperate plea for mercy, the more she felt a savage pleasure in judging Margaret harshly. She delighted in the prospect of stripping away the "glamour" that Margaret wielded over others, proving herself untainted by the beauty that had ensnared so many. With a snort of scorn, she dismissed the image of Margaret's jet-black hair and luminous eyes, resolving to spend the night crafting a speech of stern, just reproach.

"Is Miss Hale within?"

Mrs. Thornton knew the answer before Martha could respond. Margaret was already seated in the little hall, pen in hand, writing letters to Edith detailing her mother's final days. It was a softening exercise, and she brushed aside unbidden tears as the announcement of the visitor reached her ears.

Mrs. Thornton entered, and though Margaret received her with gentle, ladylike decorum, the visitor felt daunted. The atmosphere shifted; it became impossible to launch into the prepared tirade. Margaret's voice was low and rich, her manner gracious, masking a deep gratitude for the courtesy of the call. She found topics of conversation, praising Martha and discussing a Greek melody Edith had mentioned. Mrs. Thornton felt disarmed, her sharp tongue finding no purchase among rose-leaves. Yet, beneath the surface, a suspicion gnawed at her: that this sweetness was a calculated strategy to appease Mr. Thornton, perhaps signaling that the other attachment had failed and that Miss Hale intended to reclaim her rejected lover. Driven by maternal instinct and a fear of losing a man she respected, Mrs. Thornton forced herself to summon her resolve.

Standing up, she cleared her throat. "Miss Hale, I have a duty to perform. Your mother requested that I not allow you to act wrongly—or inadvertently—without remonstrating. I am here to offer advice, whether you accept it or not."

Margaret rose, flushing crimson, her eyes wide. She assumed Mrs. Thornton had come to discuss the lie she had told Mr. Thornton, expecting an explanation for the danger she had placed herself in. Though her heart sank at the thought that Mr. Thornton had not chosen to confront her personally, she bowed her head in humble submission, ready to bear any blame.

"At first, I could scarcely believe the account given by my servant regarding your walk at the Outwood Station," Mrs. Thornton continued, her voice gaining strength. "But my son confirms it. It was indiscreet, to say the least. Many a young woman has lost her reputation for similar lapses."

Margaret's eyes flamed with sudden anger. This was a new, insulting angle. If the subject were the lie, she would own it immediately. But to have her *character* attacked by a stranger? By Mrs. Thornton? It was intolerable. She said nothing. Mrs. Thornton, sensing the battle-spirit igniting in Margaret's gaze, felt her own combative nature stir.

"For your mother's sake, I have felt it right to warn you against such improprieties," Mrs. Thornton pressed, undeterred by the silence. "They must degrade you in the estimation of the world, even if they do not lead to immediate harm."

"For my mother's sake," Margaret replied, her voice trembling with tears, "I will bear much. But I cannot bear everything. She never intended for me to be exposed to insult, I am certain."

"Insult, Miss Hale?"

"Yes, Madam," Margaret said, her voice steadying. "It is insult. What do you know of me that warrants such

"Nay, I have done; you get no more of me;
And I am glad, yea glad with all my heart,
That thus so clearly I myself am free."

Margaret withdrew to her chamber, leaving Mrs. Thornton behind. Within the confines of her small room, she paced back and forth, a physical manifestation of her inner turmoil, the familiar rhythm of her agitation. Yet, acutely aware that the thin walls of this slightly built dwelling carried every footstep from room to room, she eventually sat down, waiting in silence until she was certain Mrs. Thornton had departed the house.

Forced into introspection, she began to reconstruct the conversation, parsing every utterance, every implication, as if examining evidence under a microscope. When the replay ended, she rose, her voice heavy with a melancholy resignation. "In any case, her words do not strike me; they slide off. For I am innocent of the motives she imputes. Yet, it remains a bitter truth that any woman can so readily believe such calumny of another. It is a heavy, sad burden. Where I have erred, she offers no accusation; she knows nothing. And he... he never revealed anything. I should have suspected his silence!"

She raised her chin, feigning a pride in whatever subtle decency Mr. Thornton might have displayed. But then, a fresh, stinging thought seized her, pressing her hands together so tightly her knuckles whitened. "He, too, must attribute some affection to poor Frederick." A flush warmed her cheeks as the word lingered. "I see it now. It is not simply that he knows of my deceit; he believes another woman holds sway over me; and that *I*..." A sigh escaped her lips. "Oh, deary me! What am I to do? What does this madness portend? Why do I care so deeply for his judgment, beyond the simple loss of his trust in my veracity? I cannot fathom it. Yet, misery consumes me. How tragically short this last year has felt! I have leapt from childhood to a premature old age. I have known no youth, no true womanhood; the dreams of marriage have closed forever before me. I anticipate the cares and sorrows of an elderly widow with a tear-stained spirit. I am wearied by the constant demand for fortitude. I could endure hardship for Papa; it is a natural, holy duty. Perhaps I could summon the energy to resent Mrs. Thornton's unjust, impertinent suspicions. But it is devastating to realize how utterly misunderstood I am. What has altered me today? I know not. I simply cannot stop it. Sometimes I must yield. 'No,' she declared, springing upright, steeling herself. "'I shall not—I *will not* dwell on myself or my station. I shall not dissect my own feelings. It serves no purpose now.' She paused, wiping her eyes with impatient gestures, knowing the tears would return regardless of her resolve. 'Perhaps many a woman makes a far sadder error and pays the price only when it is too late. How proudly, how sharply I spoke to him that day! Yet, I was ignorant. This realization crept upon me gradually, and I know not whence it sprang. Now, I shall hold fast. It will be arduous to treat him as before, carrying this crushing conscience, but I shall remain composed, quiet, and sparing in my words. To be sure, he avoids me; he keeps his distance, seemingly intent on evading our paths. That would be worse than all. And yet, no wonder he shuns me, believing what he must.'"

Stepping outside, Margaret moved swiftly toward the countryside, attempting to drown the churning reflections within her mind by the sheer velocity of her steps.

Upon her return, standing on the threshold, her father approached. "A good girl," he remarked warmly. "You've been to Mrs. Boucher's. I was just intending to pay a call there before dinner, if I had time."

"No, Father," Margaret replied, her face flushing with embarrassment. "I never considered it. However, I shall go immediately after dinner; I will visit while you are resting."

True to her word, Margaret proceeded. Mrs. Boucher lay gravely ill, truly dying, not merely ailing. The kind, sensible neighbor who had visited recently appeared to have assumed total charge of the household. Several children had been sent to neighbors; Mary Higgins had fetched the three youngest at dinner, and subsequently, Nicholas had ventured out to seek the doctor. The physician had not yet arrived. There was nothing to do but wait. Margaret, however, hoped to consult the doctor's opinion and reasoned that visiting the Higginses in the interim offered the best chance of learning whether Nicholas had successfully petitioned Mr. Thornton.

Inside the Higgins home, she found Nicholas busily fashioning a penny-spin on the dresser, entertaining three tiny children who clung to him with fearless adoration. He, along with the children, smiled at the long, smooth rotation of the coin. Margaret observed that this look of absorbed joy in his occupation was a promising sign. When the penny finally halted, "little Johnnie" broke into tears.

"Come here," Margaret said, lifting the child from the dresser and cradling him. She held her watch to his ear, simultaneously asking Nicholas if he had encountered Mr. Thornton.

Nicholas's expression shifted instantly, darkening with a sudden gravity. "Ay!" he confirmed. "I've seen and heard too much concerning him."

"He refused you, then?" Margaret asked, her voice filled with sorrow.

"To be sure. I knew he'd do it all along. It's no good expecting mercy from the hands of those masters. You're a stranger and a foreigner, unlikely to understand their ways; but I knew it."

"I regret having asked you. Was he angry? Did he not address you as Hamper did?"

"He wasn't overly civil!" Nicholas admitted, resuming the spinning of the penny, this time for his own amusement as well as the children's. "Do not fret. I'm exactly where I was. I'll resume tramping tomorrow. I returned his courtesy in kind. I told him I didn't possess the high regard for him that would compel me to return voluntarily; but you advised me to go, and I am beholden to you."

"You informed him you were sent by me?"

"I don't recall calling you by your name. I don't think I did. I mentioned a woman who knew no better had advised me to investigate if there was a soft spot in his heart."

"And he—?" Margaret pressed.

"Said I was to tell you to mind your own business. That's the longest spin yet, my lads. Those are civilized words compared to what he used toward me. But never mind. We stand where we stood. I'll break stones on the road before I let these little ones freeze."

Margaret placed the squirming Johnnie back onto the dresser. "I apologize for asking you to approach Mr. Thornton. I am disappointed in him."

A faint noise echoed behind her. Both she and Nicholas turned simultaneously to reveal Mr. Thornton standing there, his countenance marked by displeased surprise. Acting on an instant impulse, Margaret stepped past him, offering no words save for a deep, low bow designed to conceal the sudden pallor rising in her cheeks. He bowed equally low in return, then closed the door after her. As she hastened toward Mrs. Boucher's, she heard the sharp clang of the latch, a sound that seemed to complete the measure of her humiliation. He, too, was annoyed to discover her presence.

Yet, beneath his stern exterior, Mr. Thornton harbored a tender sentiment—a "soft place," as Nicholas Higgins had termed it. He possessed a profound pride in guarding it, keeping it sacred and secure, and remaining fiercely jealous of any circumstance threatening its intrusion. While he dreaded exposing this vulnerability, he was equally driven by a desire for all men to recognize his justice. He felt a gnawing guilt for having accorded such a scornful hearing to anyone who had waited, with humble patience, five hours to speak to him. Whether the man had spoken saucily or not mattered little to Mr. Thornton; he actually admired Higgins's boldness and was conscious of his own irritable temper at the time, likely rendering them even-handed. It was the five hours of waiting that struck him most deeply. He had not five hours to spare himself, yet he had sacrificed an hour or two of his rigorous intellectual and physical labor to gather evidence regarding the truth of Higgins's story, the nature of his character, and the tenor of his life. Though he strove to suppress it, he was convinced that everything Higgins had said rang true. Then, conviction descended like a spell, touching the latent tenderness of his heart. The man's patience, the simple generosity of his motive (for he now understood the quarrel between Boucher and Higgins), caused him to forget entirely the cold reasoning of justice and leap over it via a higher instinct. He intended to offer Higgins employment, and he was more irritated by finding Margaret there than by hearing her final words. For then he realized she was the woman who had urged Higgins to approach him, and he dreaded admitting any thought of her as a motive for an action undertaken solely because it was right.

"So that was the lady you spoke of as a woman?" he asked indignantly to Higgins. "You might have told me who she was."

"And then, perhaps, you would have addressed her more kindly than you did; you'd have acquired a mother capable of keeping your tongue in check when discours

"I have no wrong, where I can claim no right,
Naught ta'en me fro, where I have nothing had,
Yet of my woe I cannot so be quite;
Namely, since that another may be glad
With that, that thus in sorrow makes me sad."

Margaret Hale had not anticipated deriving much personal joy from Mr. Bell's visit; her anticipation was rooted solely in concern for her father. Yet, when her godfather arrived, the natural ease of their friendship unfolded instantly. He expressed that she possessed no merit in being what she was, a girl wholly aligned with his own nature; it was an inherited capacity within her to step into his affections and claim them as her own. In return, she offered him generous praise for maintaining a youthful spirit beneath his academic robes.

"Fresh and young in warmth and kindness, I mean," Mr. Bell remarked with a twinkle. "I must admit, however, that I find your opinions the most antiquated and dusty I have encountered in a long time."

"Hear this daughter of yours, Hale!" Mr. Bell exclaimed. "Her residence in Milton has utterly corrupted her. She is a democrat, a red republican, a member of the Peace Society, a socialist—"

"Papa," Margaret interjected gently, "it is simply because I advocate for the advancement of commerce. Mr. Bell would have preferred trade remain stagnant, trading wild-beast skins for acorns."

"No, no," Mr. Bell replied warmly. "I would rather dig the earth and cultivate potatoes. I would take a wild-beast skin, shave it, and weave it into broadcloth. Do not exaggerate, missy. But I am weary of this ceaseless bustle. Everyone rushing over one another, driven by a frantic desire to accumulate wealth."

"It is not everyone who can sit comfortably within university walls and watch their fortunes grow without exerting themselves," Mr. Hale observed. "There are surely many here who would be grateful if their properties increased as yours have, without any trouble on their part."

"I do not believe they would," Mr. Bell countered. "It is the struggle itself they crave. As for sitting still, learning from the past, or forging the future through faithful, prophetic labor—Pooh! I suspect there isn't a single soul in Milton who knows how to sit still. It is a profound art."

"The people of Milton, I suspect, assume Oxford men do not know how to move. It would be beneficial if they mixed a little more."

"It might benefit the Miltoners greatly. Many things could improve their lot, even if those changes would be disagreeable to others."

"Are you not a Milton man yourself?" Margaret asked, surprised. "I would have thought you would be proud of your town."

"To be honest, I see little cause for pride. If you would only come to Oxford, Margaret, I will show you a place worthy of glory."

"Well!" Mr. Hale smiled. "Mr. Thornton is coming to tea tonight, and he is as proud of Milton as you are of Oxford. The two of you must try to broaden each other's minds."

"I have no desire to become more 'liberal-minded,' thank you," Mr. Bell declared.

"Is Mr. Thornton coming to tea, Papa?" Margaret whispered softly.

"Either to tea or shortly thereafter. He was uncertain. He instructed us not to wait."

Meanwhile, Mr. Thornton had resolved against asking his mother how far she had progressed in her efforts to discuss the impropriety of Margaret's conduct with him. He was certain that if he learned the details, her account would only serve to annoy and chagrin him, inevitably colored by her perspective. He shrank from hearing Margaret's name uttered, even as he blamed her, envied her, and renounced her in his mind. Yet, he loved her intensely, despite himself. He dreamed of her approaching him, arms outstretched, with a lightness and gaiety that simultaneously repelled and allured him. This vision of Margaret, stripped of her true character as if by an evil spirit, was so deeply etched in his imagination that upon waking, he struggled to distinguish the idealized *Una* from the deceptive *Duessa*. His distaste for the latter seemed to mar the former. Nevertheless, his pride forbade him from acknowledging his vulnerability by avoiding her. He sought neither opportunity to be near her nor refuge from her presence. To prove his self-control, he lingered over every business matter that afternoon, forcing his movements into unnatural slowness and deliberation. Consequently, it was past eight o'clock before he reached Mr. Hale's.

In the study, business arrangements were conducted with Mr. Bell. The latter remained seated by the fire, talking wearily long after business was concluded, making it evident they might just as well have retired upstairs. Mr. Thornton refused to suggest moving, chafing at Mr. Bell's prolixity and considering him a tedious companion. Conversely, Mr. Bell secretly viewed Mr. Thornton as brusque, curt, and lacking in both intelligence and manner. Finally, a faint noise from above indicated it was time to ascend. They found Margaret with a letter open before her, eagerly discussing its contents with her father. Upon the gentlemen's entrance, she hastily closed it. However, Mr. Thornton's keen senses caught a fragment of Mr. Hale's words to Mr. Bell.

"A letter from Henry Lennox. It fills Margaret with hope."

Mr. Bell nodded. Margaret flushed crimson as Mr. Thornton fixed his gaze upon her. He felt an overwhelming urge to rise and depart the room instantly, never to return.

"We were reflecting," Mr. Hale said, "that you and Mr. Thornton have followed Margaret's counsel, attempting to convert one another, given your lengthy absence in the study."

"And you presumed we had reduced each other to mere opinions, like the Kilkenny cats' tails. Whose opinion do you imagine possesses the most obstinate vitality?"

Mr. Thornton had no concept of their discussion and disdained to ask. Mr. Hale politely clarified.

"Mr. Thornton, we accused Mr. Bell this morning of a certain Oxonian medieval bigotry regarding his native town. We, I believe with Margaret, suggested it would do him good to associate with the Milton manufacturers."

"My apologies. I thought it would do the Milton manufacturers good to associate more with Oxford men. Was that not the sentiment, Margaret?"

"I believe I thought it would do both sides good to see more of each other. I did not realize it was either of ours."

"So, Mr. Thornton, we ought to have been improving one another downstairs, rather than discussing vanished families of the Smiths and Harrisons. However, I am willing to fulfill my part now. I wonder when you Miltonians intend to settle down. Your entire lives seem devoted to gathering materials for existence."

"By living, do you mean enjoyment?"

"Yes, enjoyment. I do not specify the nature, for I trust we both consider mere pleasure a very poor substitute for enjoyment."

"I would prefer the nature of the enjoyment be defined."

"Well! Enjoyment or leisure—the enjoyment derived from the power and influence money affords. You are all striving for money. For what purpose?"

Mr. Thornton fell silent. Then he murmured, "I truly do not know. But money is not what I strive for."

"What, then?"

"It is a domestic question. I shall have to submit to such a catechism, and I am not certain I am prepared."

"No!" Mr. Hale interrupted. "Let us not make this catechism personal. Neither of you represents humanity; you are each too individual for that."

"I am unsure if I should consider that a compliment. I would like to be the representative of Oxford, with its beauty, learning, and proud history. What do you say, Margaret? Should I feel flattered?"

"I know Oxford poorly. But there is a distinction between representing a city and being the representative man of its inhabitants."

"True enough, Miss Margaret. Now I recall, you were opposed to me this morning, adopting a distinctly Miltonian and manufacturing stance."

Margaret perceived the swift, surprised glance Mr. Thornton directed at her and felt annoyance at the interpretation he might draw from Mr. Bell's remark. Mr. Bell continued:

"Oh! I wish I could show you our High Street, our Radcliffe Square. I am omitting our colleges, just as I grant Mr. Thornton leave to omit his factories when describing the charms of Milton. I have a right to critique my birthplace. Remember, I am a Milton man."

Mr. Thornton felt annoyed, more than warranted, by Mr. Bell's words. He was not in a humor for jest. Under different circumstances, he could have relished Mr. Bell's half-teasing condemnation of a town whose life clashed with every habit he had cultivated. But now, he was sufficiently irritated to attempt defending what was never seriously intended to be attacked.

"I do not set up Milton as a model town."

"Not in architecture?" Mr. Bell asked slyly.

"No! We have been too occupied to attend to mere outward appearances."

"Do not say *mere* outward appearances," Mr. Hale gently corrected. "They impress us all, from childhood onward—every day of our lives."

"Wait a moment," Mr. Thornton said. "Remember we are of a different stock from the Greeks, to whom beauty was everything. To them, Mr. Bell might speak of a life of leisure and serene enjoyment, much of which entered through their outward senses. I do not mean to despise them, nor to ape them. But I possess Teut

The journey was ending, yet the season offered no corresponding warmth. Winter was slowly yielding to lengthening days, but the February sun brought no brightness, no spark of hope to the heavy, grey atmosphere. Mrs. Thornton had completely withdrawn from the household, her presence a memory rather than a reality. Mr. Thornton visited occasionally, but his stops were brief, confined strictly to the study where he conferred with her father. To Mr. Hale, these rare encounters seemed to hold a heightened value, a precious currency of trust. From what Margaret could discern, Mr. Thornton's silence stemmed not from wounded pride or lingering resentment regarding the strike, but from the sheer complexity of his business affairs, which demanded a focus far greater than he had given last winter. Yet, amidst his serious discourse, Margaret noticed a thread of consistency: whenever his name arose, or when he spoke of her, it was always with a calm, friendly neutrality. Neither avoidance nor active pursuit marked his words; they simply existed, unburdened by the storm clouds of the past.

Margaret herself lacked the resilience to lift her father's spirits. The long period of anxiety and turmoil that had preceded this dreary, peaceful stillness had stretched her mind thin, robbing it of its elasticity. She sought solace in teaching the younger Boucher children, working diligently at "goodness" with a fierce, almost painful intensity. Her heart, however, remained numb, dead to the fruits of her labor. Though she performed her duties punctually, a shadow of bleakness clung to her existence, rendering her life devoid of cheer. Her greatest triumph, ironically, was not in her teaching but in her silent, unconscious piety toward her father. She was a mirror for his moods, anticipating his unspoken wishes with a meek obedience that bordered on the divine. These were quiet desires, often hesitated over, yet she fulfilled them with a completeness that softened the edges of their shared solitude.

Spring arrived with news from abroad: Frederick's marriage. Letters flowed between him and Dolores; hers, a blend of Spanish and English, and his, peppered with linguistic inversions that revealed how deeply his bride's culture was taking root. Upon receiving a letter from Henry Lennox detailing the slim chances of a court-martial clearing Frederick's name, Frederick responded with a vehement renunciation of England. He spoke of wishing to "unnature" himself, declaring he would reject a pardon and refuse to return to the country if permitted. At first, these words tore at Margaret, seeming unnatural and harsh. But reflection softened her blow; she saw the raw poignancy of a hope so thoroughly crushed. Patience became her only shield. Soon, Frederick's tone shifted to joyful anticipation of the future, and Margaret found within herself the capacity for the patience he craved. Dolores's letters, though penned by a young Spaniard, possessed a charming timidity, filled with erasures and feminine care designed to impress her lover's English relations. Accompanying these missives was a magnificent black lace mantilla, chosen by Dolores herself for her unseen sister-in-law—a paragon of beauty, wisdom, and virtue whom Frederick described. This union elevated Frederick's worldly station significantly, placing him as a junior partner in Barbour & Co., one of Spain's most extensive trading houses. Margaret smiled faintly, then sighed, recalling her old tirades against commerce. Here was her *preux chevalier*, her brother, transformed into a merchant trader. Yet, rebellion stirred within her, protesting the false equivalence she felt between a Spanish merchant and a Milton mill-owner. Regardless of trade or profession, Frederick was undeniably happy, and the mantilla was exquisite. Thus, she returned to the weight of the present.

Her father had begun to suffer from occasional difficulties in breathing, distressing him greatly. While Margaret was less alarmed, noting the condition vanished in intervals, her heart urged him to accept Mr. Bell's invitation to visit Oxford in April. Mr. Bell's note specifically commanded Margaret's attendance, yet she felt a profound relief in remaining quietly at home, free from any external responsibility. For over two years, she had carried the burden of caring for an invalid, planning his days and soothing his ailments. Now, with her father gone, a strange, staggering liberty descended upon her. No one depended on her for cheer; no illness required her vigilance. She could be idle, silent, forgetful—and, perhaps most liberating of all, unhappy if she chose to be. For months, her personal sorrows had been stuffed into a dark cupboard, hidden from view. Now, with leisure, she could unpack them, mourn over them, and dissect their nature. Sitting motionless in the drawing-room for hours, she reviewed the bitterness of every remembrance with an unwincing resolve. Once, a cry escaped her lips at the sting of the faithlessness that had birthed her abasing falsehood.

Yet, she refused to acknowledge the force of the temptation that had led her astray. Her plans for Frederick had failed, leaving the temptation a dead mockery, a lie so despicably foolish in retrospect that faith in truth seemed infinitely wiser. In her nervous agitation, she opened a book belonging to her father. The words that caught her eye seemed tailored to her state of acute self-abasement: *"Meurs de honte... reveilons-nous, et quittons-la pour jamais..."* ("Die of shame... let us wake up and leave it forever"). "The way of humility," Margaret thought. "Ah, that is what I have missed!" She resolved to turn back, trusting in God's help to find the lost path.

Rising, she summoned Martha, whose grave, mechanical obedience masked a depth of individual character. Through gentle probing, Margaret learned a surprising history: Martha's father, once a warehouse clerk, had been in early life connected to Mrs. Thornton's husband, even showing him kindness. Circumstances had separated the families until Martha grew up poor and destitute, facing the prospect of being "lost." Then, Mrs. Thornton and Mr. Thornton intervened. They had nursed sick Martha in their own home, sending her to the sea despite medical warnings about contagious fever. "Miss Fanny," Martha noted, referring to Mrs. Thornton's daughter, had feared the contagion but ultimately allowed the care to proceed. "And she's going to be married!" Martha exclaimed. To a wealthy gentleman named Watson, older than she, with mills beyond Hayleigh. "A very good marriage," Martha concluded, despite Watson's grey hair. Margaret listened in silence, the news settling over her like a heavy cloak, before Martha swept up the hearth and departed with her usual wooden composure. Margaret realized she must stop the habit of imagining how every event concerning Mr. Thornton would affect him, weighing his potential joy or displeasure.

The next day, after lessons with the Boucher children and a long walk, Margaret visited Mary Higgins. To her surprise, Nicholas was already home. The lengthening daylight had deceived her regarding the hour. Seemingly entering a new phase of humility, Nicholas was quieter, less self-asserting. He remarked on the old gentleman's departure, attributing the wanderlust to the weather, and mentioned that Master Thornton was also "spinning about the world." When Margaret innocently asked if this explained his early arrival, Nicholas dismissed it with contemptuous confidence. He was not a man of two faces; he owed his position to Margaret, and Thornton's mill, while decent, was merely a tool for honest labor. "Stand down, lad, and say yo'r pretty hymn to Miss Marget," he instructed, leading her to teach the little boy a Methodist hymn. Its swinging rhythm captivated him, and he recited it with the cadence of a parliamentarian, eventually requesting another, and yet another, drawn unconsciously into an interest in sacred matters he had once scorned.

Returning home late, Margaret found the house empty, a comfort that allowed her to think freely. Resolving to sort through a large packet of letters, she selected those destined for destruction. Among them were several from Henry Lennox regarding Frederick's affairs. Initially, she intended only to assess the legal merits, but as she read, the stiff, formal wording betrayed a deeper current. Lennox had never forgotten his relationship to her. The letters were clever, lacking the hearty, genial atmosphere she expected, yet they were invaluable. She preserved them. As the sorting ended, a reverie seized her. Thoughts of her absent father haunted her mind; she almost blamed herself for having felt his solitude as a relief. But these two days had restored her, filling her with new strength and brighter hope. Tasks that had previously seemed burdensome now appeared as pleasures. The morbid scales had fallen from her eyes, revealing her position and work more clearly. If Mr. Thornton would restore their lost friendship, or merely visit to cheer her father, she felt a sense of clarity returning to her future, even if it were not brilliant. A sigh escaped her as she prepared for bed. Despite the single, plain duty of devotion to her father, an anxiety and a pang of sorrow lingered at her heart.

Meanwhile, across town, Mr. Hale thought of Margaret with the same persistent strangeness. Fatigued by revisiting old friends and familiar places, he had harbored exaggerated fears of how his altered opinions might be received. Yet, as he anticipated, shock and grief quickly melted into a tender gravity. Those who had once loved him penetrated the silence beneath his reserve, taking him to their hearts with a protecting kindness reminiscent of how they would treat a beloved woman. This renewal of affection, after so many years and changes, overwhelmed any roughness or disapproval.

"I'm afraid we've done too much," Mr. Bell warned, concerned by Hale's apparent frailty. "You're suffering from living so long in that Milton air."

"When some beloved voice that was to you
Both sound and sweetness, faileth suddenly,
And silence, against which you dare not cry,
Aches round you like a strong disease and new,—
What hope? what help? what music will undo
That silence to your sense?"

The shock had been terrible. Margaret sank into a state of prostration that offered no outlet in sobs or tears, finding no solace in words. She lay upon the sofa, her eyes closed, silent save for a whisper when compelled to answer. Mr. Bell sat beside her, perplexed and paralyzed. He dared not leave her alone, yet he dared not urge her to return to Oxford; her physical exhaustion was absolute, rendering any journey impossible, regardless of the horrors she would inevitably face upon her return. Sitting by the fire, he considered his options while Margaret lay motionless and breathless beside him. He refused the invitation to dine downstairs with Dixon, whose hospitality, though tinged with sobbing, he declined. A plate of food was brought up to him; usually, Mr. Bell was fastidious, discerning every nuance of flavor, but now the devilled chicken tasted like sawdust. He minced some chicken for Margaret, seasoning it carefully, but when Dixon attempted to feed her, a languid shake of her head signaled that food would only choke her, offering no sustenance.

With a heavy sigh, Mr. Bell rose, his stiffened legs protesting the movement, and followed Dixon out of the room.

"I cannot leave her," he muttered, pacing. "I must write to Oxford immediately to ensure preparations are underway; they can proceed until I arrive. Can Mrs. Lennox not come? I will instruct her to do so. This girl needs a female friend nearby, if only to coax her into a proper fit of weeping."

Dixon, weeping as if for two, wiped her eyes and steadied her voice to inform Mr. Bell that Mrs. Lennox was confined and unable to travel.

"Well!" Mr. Bell sighed, rubbing his chin. "We shall have to summon Mrs. Shaw. Has she returned to England?"

"Yes, sir, she has returned, but..." Dixon hesitated, disapproving of a stranger intruding upon her mistress's domain to oversee Margaret. "...she fears leaving Mrs. Lennox at such a critical juncture."

"Critical time, be—" Mr. Bell cut himself off with a cough. "She could easily reside in Venice or Naples, or some of those Catholic locales, during the 'critical time' that occurred in Corfu. And what does that little prosperous woman's predicament amount to, compared to this poor, helpless, friendless creature lying still as a stone upon an altar-tomb? I tell you, Mrs. Shaw shall come. Ensure a room is prepared for her by tomorrow night. I will see to it personally."

True to his word, Mr. Bell penned a letter. Mrs. Shaw, reading it, shed many tears, remarking that it sounded exactly like the General when he threatened a gout attack, promising to cherish it forever. Had he merely suggested or gently urged her visit, implying refusal were an option, she likely would have remained true to her natural reserve. Instead, the sharp, ungentle command shattered her *vis inertia*, compelling her to pack her trunks with the assistance of her maid. Edith, dressed in caps and shawls, emerged from the carriage to greet her mother as Captain Lennox assisted her down.

"Do not forget, Mamma," Edith cried, clutching her mother's arm. "Margaret must come and live with us. Sholto departs for Oxford on Wednesday; you must send word via Mr. Bell when we expect you. If you require Sholto, he can travel from Oxford directly to Milton. Remember, Mamma—you must bring Margaret back."

Upon returning to the drawing-room, Mr. Henry Lennox was seated, cutting open a fresh issue of a review. Without raising his gaze, he remarked, "If you do not wish Sholto to remain absent from you for so long, Edith, pray let me descend to Milton to render whatever aid I can."

"Oh, thank you," Edith replied, her spirits lightening. "Old Mr. Bell will undoubtedly manage everything, and further assistance may prove unnecessary. However, it seems fortunate that Uncle should pass away precisely now, when we have returned home and are fully prepared to receive Margaret. Poor soul! How drastically her life will change from Milton!" She looked around dreamily. "I shall procure new chintz for her bedroom, brighten the space, and lift her spirits."

Meanwhile, Mrs. Shaw traveled to Milton, oscillating between dread of the initial encounter and fervent plans to whisk Margaret away from "that dreadful place" to the comforting warmth of Harley Street.

"Oh, dear!" she murmured to her maid, staring at the smokestacks. "Look at those chimneys! My poor sister Hale! I could not have slept in Naples had I foreseen this misery. I ought to have fetched her sooner." Privately, she conceded that she had always viewed her brother-in-law as somewhat frail, yet never so weak as in witnessing the trade-off he had made for the lovely Helstone home.

Margaret remained unchanged: pale, motionless, speechless, tearless. They informed her that Aunt Shaw was arriving, but she displayed neither surprise, delight, nor distaste. Mr. Bell, his appetite restored and appreciative of Dixon's efforts, vainly urged her to sample sweetbreads stewed with oysters; she shook her head with the same quiet obstinacy as before, forcing him to consume the entire dish himself. Yet, Margaret was the first to notice the cab halting outside, bringing her aunt from the station. Her eyelids quivered, her lips flushed and trembled. As Mr. Bell descended to greet Mrs. Shaw, Margaret rose, steadying her dizzy form. Upon seeing her aunt, she moved forward, seeking the embrace extended to receive her, and instantly found the passionate relief of tears soaking her aunt's shoulder. All notions of habitual affection, tender memories spanning years, the bond of kinship to the dead, and that inexplicable familial resemblance in look, tone, and gesture—which had so vividly recalled her mother to Margaret's mind—melted together, softening her numbed heart into an overflowing torrent of warm tears.

Mr. Bell slipped quietly out of the room and descended to the study, where he lit a fire and distracted his agitated thoughts by examining various volumes. Each book evoked a memory or suggestion of his departed friend. While this marked a shift from his two days of vigil, it represented no change in his thoughts. Hearing Mr. Thornton's voice inquire at the door, he welcomed him. Dixon, however, dismissed Mr. Thornton with cavalier indifference; the arrival of Mrs. Shaw's entourage had awakened visions of former grandeur, the Beresford lineage, and the "station" (as she termed it) from which her young mistress had fallen, only to be restored by divine providence. These fantasies, which she had indulged in her conversations with Mrs. Shaw's maid (skillfully extracting details regarding the Harley Street establishment for Martha's edification), rendered her inclined toward snobbism. Thus, though she usually stood in awe of Mr. Thornton, she treated him as curtly as her courage permitted, denying him access to the house that evening. Her statement was abruptly contradicted when Mr. Bell opened the study door and called out, "Thornton! Is that you? Enter for a moment; I wish to speak with you." Mr. Thornton entered, forcing Dixon to retreat to the kitchen and bolster her pride with a prodigious tale of Sir John Beresford's coach and six during his tenure as High Sheriff.

"Honestly, I cannot recall why I wished to speak to you," Mr. Bell admitted, looking around the dim room. "It is sufficiently dull sitting in a chamber where everything speaks of a lost friend. Nevertheless, Margaret and her aunt must possess the drawing-room exclusively."

"Has Mrs.—has her aunt arrived?" asked Mr. Thornton.

"Arrived? Indeed! Maid and all. One would have expected her to travel alone under such circumstances! Now I am reduced to finding my way to the Clarendon."

"You must not go to the Clarendon. We have five or six vacant bedrooms at home."

"Freshly aired?"

"I trust my mother's judgment on that score."

"Then I shall merely ascend to bid that wan girl goodnight, pay my respects to her aunt, and depart with you immediately."

Mr. Bell lingered upstairs for some time. Mr. Thornton, preoccupied with business and having barely spared time to visit Crampton and inquire after Miss Hale, began to wonder at the delay.

Once they were walking, Mr. Bell observed, "Those women detained me in the drawing-room. Mrs. Shaw is eager to return home—due to concerns for her daughter, she states—and wishes for Margaret to accompany her forthwith. Yet, she is scarcely fit for travel, no more so than I am for flight. Furthermore, she argues, and with justice, that she possesses acquaintances she must visit, friends to whom she owes farewells. Then her aunt harried her regarding old claims, accusing her of forgetting old friends. Finally, she erupted in tears, expressing how gladly she would depart from a place where she has endured so much suffering."

He paused, as if seeking confirmation, but received no answer. His companion's internal echoes repeated endlessly, "Where she had suffered so much." Alas! That was precisely how this eighteen-month

Mrs. Shaw harbored a visceral, almost physical aversion to Milton, a sentiment born of the city's oppressive atmosphere. To her gentle sensibilities, the town was a cacophony of noise and smoke, a place where poverty manifested in the grime of the streets and wealth in the garish excess of the gentry. Above all, she detested the disarray of male attire; not a single man, high or low, wore clothing that fitted him properly. Convinced that Margaret could never recover her shattered strength within these walls, and fearing a relapse of her own nervous ailments, Mrs. Shaw insisted on immediate departure. Her urgency was so compelling that Margaret, frail and broken-spirited, eventually yielded a reluctant promise. By the following Wednesday, she would depart with her aunt, leaving Dixon to manage the final arrangements for settling debts and closing the house. That Wednesday marked a somber milestone: the burial of Mr. Hale, a man now interred far from any home he had ever known, leaving behind a wife isolated among strangers—a prospect that tormented Margaret deeply. She feared that if she had not succumbed to her grief-stricken stupor in those initial, devastating days, she might have orchestrated a different future for her father.

Before the funeral, however, a letter arrived from Mr. Bell.

"My Dear Margaret: I intended to return to Milton on Thursday, but unfortunately, circumstances arise requiring me to fulfill certain duties as a Fellow of Plymouth College, preventing my absence from my post. Captain Lennox and Mr. Thornton are currently in town. The former appears to be a sensible, benevolent individual who has offered to travel to Milton to assist you in searching for a missing will; naturally, since you have not found it thus far, I suspect it does not exist, or you would have located it had you adhered to my previous instructions. The Captain further proposes transporting you and his mother-in-law home, and given her current condition, I perceive that he cannot remain away beyond Friday. However, your Dixon is trustworthy and capable of managing affairs until my arrival. Should there prove to be no will, I shall entrust the matter to my attorney in Milton, as I harbor doubts regarding this 'smart' Captain's acumen in business matters, notwithstanding his magnificent moustaches. A sale of effects will inevitably ensue; therefore, please designate any items you wish to reserve, or transmit a list subsequently.

Two further points remain. You are, I am confident, aware—or if not, your late father certainly did—that upon my demise, you shall inherit my monies and possessions. While I harbor no intention of dying prematurely, I mention this merely to clarify the impending transfer. The Lennox family appears exceedingly fond of you at present, though whether this affection persists remains uncertain. Consequently, it is prudent to establish a formal agreement stipulating an annual payment of two hundred and fifty pounds from you to them, contingent upon the mutual enjoyment of residing together. Naturally, this sum encompasses Dixon; ensure you are not unduly influenced into offering a higher figure for her services. Such an arrangement safeguards you against being cast adrift should the Captain desire sole possession of his residence in the future, allowing you to relocate with your assets elsewhere, unless, of course, I have already secured your services to maintain my household. Regarding attire, domestic staff, personal expenditures, and confectionery—which all young ladies consume until maturity brings wisdom—I intend to consult a distinguished acquaintance to determine your allowable budget, considering your father's estate.

Now, Margaret, have you not already fled the page, bewildered by the audacity of an elderly bachelor to dispose of your affairs so casually? I am certain you have. Yet, he possesses a rightful authority. He has cherished your father for thirty-five years, stood sentinel at his nuptials, and witnessed his passing. Furthermore, he is your godfather; lacking spiritual efficacy due to his concealed awareness of your superior moral stature, he seeks to compensate by providing material security. Lacking earthly kin, 'Who shall mourn for Adam Bell?' his entire existence revolves around this singular devotion, and Margaret Hale is not the sort of girl to deny him. Compose a reply by return post, containing merely two lines to indicate your decision. However, offer no thanks."

Margaret seized a pen, her hand trembling as she inscribed, "Margaret Hale is not the girl to say him nay." In her debilitated state, no other words presented themselves, yet she felt a pang of resentment at their necessity. Exhausted by even this minor effort, she collapsed onto the bed, striving to silence her thoughts.

"My dearest child, has this letter distressed you?"

"No!" Margaret murmured feebly. "I shall feel better once tomorrow has passed."

"I am convinced, darling, that you will find no respite until I remove you from this dreadful air. I struggle to comprehend how you have endured it for two years."

"To where could I possibly go? I cannot abandon Papa and Mama."

"Do not torment yourself, my love. Perhaps it was all for the best, though I failed to grasp the severity of your situation. Even our butler's wife resides in a more comfortable dwelling."

"It is occasionally charming, particularly in summer; one cannot judge it by its current state. I have been quite happy here," Margaret whispered, closing her eyes to forestall further discourse.

The house, though stripped of most furnishings, exuded a semblance of comfort compared to its former desolation. Evenings had grown chilly, and fires burned brightly in every chamber per Mrs. Shaw's orders. Her aunt doted on Margaret, procuring every delicacy and soft luxury she deemed necessary for her recovery. Yet, Margaret remained largely indifferent to these comforts; if they registered in her mind, it was solely as grounds for gratitude toward her aunt, who labored so tirelessly to accommodate her needs. Restlessness plagued her despite her weakness. Throughout the day, she wandered aimlessly from room to room, idly setting aside objects she wished to retain. Dixon trailed in her wake, ostensibly seeking instructions but privately charged with soothing Margaret into repose.

"These volumes, Dixon, I shall keep. The remainder may be dispatched to Mr. Bell; they possess intrinsic value, both for themselves and for my father's memory. As for this item... I request you deliver it to Mr. Thornton after I have departed. Wait; I shall compose a brief note." She seated herself abruptly, seemingly terrified of lingering contemplation, and penned:

"DEAR SIR,—The enclosed volume I am certain will be appreciated by you, particularly in honor of my father, to whom it originally belonged.

Yours sincerely,

Resuming her perambulations through the house, Margaret touched upon familiar artifacts from her childhood, embracing them with a reluctant tenderness. Though old-fashioned, worn, and shabby, they held a poignant weight. She spoke scarcely a word, and Dixon reported to Mrs. Shaw that "she suspected Miss Hale heard not a syllable spoken, despite her constant chatter, merely to distract from her intentions." The consequence of remaining upright throughout the day was profound physical exhaustion by evening, affording her the deepest sleep she had enjoyed since learning of Mr. Hale's demise.

At breakfast the following morning, Margaret expressed a desire to bid farewell to one or two acquaintances. Mrs. Shaw demurred:

"I am assured, my dear, that you possess no friends here sufficiently intimate to warrant such a visit so promptly, prior to attending church."

"But today is my only opportunity. If Captain Lennox arrives this afternoon, and if we must—and if I truly must depart tomorrow..."

"Oh, yes, we shall proceed tomorrow. I am increasingly convinced that this climate is detrimental to your health, rendering you so pallid and ill-composed. Besides, Edith awaits my arrival, and you cannot remain unattended at your age. No; if you insist on these calls, I shall accompany you. Dixon can arrange a carriage, I presume?"

Thus, Mrs. Shaw accompanied Margaret, bringing Dixon to attend to shawls and air-cushions. Margaret's countenance was too sorrowful to soften into a smile amidst these preparations for two visits she had frequently undertaken alone at all hours. She hesitated, nearly admitting that one of her destinations was Nicholas Higgins' lodging, hoping fervently that her aunt would decline alighting from the carriage and traversing the courtyard, risking exposure to damp laundry strung between houses.

A subtle internal conflict arose within Mrs. Shaw between her desire for ease and a sense of maternal propriety, but the former prevailed. With numerous admonitions to preserve her health and avoid fevers endemic to such quarters, her aunt granted Margaret permission to visit locations she had frequented previously without precautions or authorization.

Nicholas was absent; only Mary and a few of the Boucher children remained. Margaret reproached herself for failing to time her visit more judiciously. Mary possessed a direct intellect, though her emotions were warm and compassionate. Upon comprehending Margaret's intent, Mary burst into tears with uninhibited sobbing, rendering Margaret's attempts at consolation futile. She could only vaguely suggest the possibility of reunion at some indeterminate time and place, urging Mary to convey to her father how ardently she wished, should circumstances permit, for him to visit her after his evening labors.

As she departed, Margaret paused, glancing around, then hesitated before speaking:

"I crave a small token to remind me of Bessy."

Mary's generosity flared instantly. "What might we provide?" Margaret identified a modest drinking cup, recalling it as the vessel invariably placed beside Bessy's fever-racked lips. Mary exclaimed,

"A dull rotation, never at a stay,
Yesterday's face twin image of to-day."

"Of what each one should be, he sees the form and rule,
And till he reach to that, his joy can ne'er be full."

For Margaret, the profound silence of the Harley Street residence, maintained throughout Edith's convalescence from childbirth, offered a necessary sanctuary. It afforded her the rare luxury of time to assimilate the seismic shift in her fortunes over the past two months. She found herself an inhabitant of a palace, where the mere awareness of any burden or anxiety seemed to have evaporated entirely. The machinery of domestic life ran with a frictionless, oil-lubricated smoothness. Mrs. Shaw and Edith showered Margaret with affection, insisting it was her home, yet she harbored a secret, gnawing guilt. The vicarage at Helstone—the humble cottage at Milton with her worried father and frail mother, surrounded by the modest chores of relative poverty—remained the truest definition of home in her spirit. Edith, eager to regain health, planned to flood Margaret's chamber with the soft comforts and decorative curiosities that adorned her own. Meanwhile, Mrs. Shaw and her maids labored tirelessly to restore Margaret's wardrobe to a state of elegant opulence. Captain Lennox, gentle and gentlemanly, spent hours playing with his young son, lingering in Edith's dressing room, or drifting away to his club whenever he was not dining out.

Just as Margaret had begun to crave quietude and reprieve, Edith descended the stairs, resuming her role in the household. Margaret reverted to her old instincts, observing and serving her cousin, gladly assuming the trivialities of duty—answering correspondence, managing schedules, and offering tender care—which led her to suspect she was feigning illness herself. Yet the rest of the family was immersed in the frenetic business of the London Season, leaving Margaret frequently solitary. In these moments, her thoughts drifted back to Milton, struck by a jarring contrast between that earnest, striving life and her current existence. She became increasingly bored by an uneventful ease that demanded no struggle or effort. A creeping fear took hold: she dreaded becoming sleepily deadened to everything outside the gilded cage of luxury surrounding her. Though London teemed with laborers and strugglers, she never saw them; the servants existed in a subterranean realm, invisible save when summoned by a whim of their masters. A strange, unsatisfied vacuum yawned within Margaret. Once, she had tentatively hinted at this malaise to Edith. Wearied from a night of dancing, Edith had languidly stroked Margaret's cheek as the younger woman sat on a footstool beside her resting cousin.

"Poor child!" Edith had sighed. "It is somewhat melancholy to be left alone night after night when the whole world is so gay. But soon we shall host dinner parties—as soon as Henry returns from circuit—and there will be a delightful variety for you. No wonder you are in such a mood, my darling!"

Margaret did not believe dinner parties could cure her ennui. Yet Edith took pride in her social gatherings, boasting they were "so different" from the staid, dowager-led meals of her mother's regime. Even Mrs. Shaw seemed to derive a peculiar pleasure from these modern, informal entertainments suited to Captain and Mrs. Lennox's tastes, mirroring her enjoyment of the more formal, ponderous events she herself used to organize. Captain Lennox remained consistently kind and brotherly toward Margaret, though a flicker of irritation sparked within her whenever he turned his anxious attention to Edith's attire, scrutinizing her beauty with the intent to impress the world. Then the dormant Vashti within Margaret rose, and she struggled to suppress her feelings.

Her days followed a predictable rhythm: a couple of quiet hours before a late breakfast, an unpunctual meal eaten lazily by weary, half-asleep guests, at which she was nonetheless expected to preside due to the subsequent discussions of plans that followed—discussions she could influence with sympathy but rarely with advice. An endless stream of notes awaited her pen, accompanied by Edith's effusive praise of her *eloquence du billet*. There were playful interludes with Sholto returning from his walks, the care of the children during the servants' dinner, drives, calls, and engagements for her aunt and cousins. These activities, while freeing, left her drained, succumbing to depression and delicate health amidst the inactivity of the day.

She looked forward with a silent, yearning interest to Dixon's return from Milton, where the old servant had been busy winding up the Hale family's affairs. This complete cessation of news regarding her former community felt like a sudden famine to her heart. True, Dixon's business-like letters occasionally quoted opinions from Mr. Thornton regarding furniture or landlords, but references to Milton itself were sparse. Sitting one evening alone in the Lennox drawing-room, holding Dixon's letters but unable to read them, Margaret pondered the bustling life she had left behind, wondering if it continued unchanged as if she and her father had never existed. She questioned whether anyone in that throng truly missed her—or Higgins, whom she did not consider at that moment. Suddenly, the door opened and Mr. Bell was announced. Margaret hastily tucked the letters into her work-basket, rising with a blush that suggested she had committed some transgression.

"Oh, Mr. Bell! I never thought of seeing you!"

"But you give me a welcome, I hope, as well as that very pretty start of surprise."

"Have you dined? How did you come? Let me order you some dinner."

"If you're going to have any. Otherwise, you know, there is no one who cares less for eating than I do. But where are the others? Gone out to dinner? Left you alone?"

"Oh yes! and it is such a rest. I was just thinking—But will you run the risk of dinner? I don't know if there is anything in the house."

"Why, to tell you the truth, I dined at my club. Only they don't cook as well as they did, so I thought, if you were going to dine, I might try and make out my dinner. But never mind, never mind! There aren't ten cooks in England to be trusted at impromptu dinners. If their skill and their fires will stand it, their tempers won't. You shall make me some tea, Margaret. And now, what were you thinking of? You were going to tell me. Whose letters were those, god-daughter, that you hid away so speedily?"

"Only Dixon's," replied Margaret, turning crimson.

"Whew! Is that all? Who do you think came up in the train with me?"

"I don't know," said Margaret, resolving against guessing.

"Your what d'ye call him? What's the right name for a cousin-in-law's brother?"

"Mr. Henry Lennox?" asked Margaret.

"Yes," replied Mr. Bell. "You knew him formerly, didn't you? What sort of a person is he, Margaret?"

"I liked him long ago," said Margaret, glancing down briefly before lifting her gaze and speaking naturally. "You know we have been corresponding about Frederick since; but I have not seen him for nearly three years, and he may be changed. What did you think of him?"

"I don't know. He was so busy trying to find out who I was, in the first instance, and what I was in the second, that he never let out what he was; unless indeed that veiled curiosity of his as to what manner of man he had to talk to was not a good piece, and a fair indication of his character. Do you call him good-looking, Margaret?"

"No! certainly not. Do you?"

"Not I. But I thought perhaps you might. Is he a great deal here?"

"I fancy he is when he is in town. He has been on circuit now since I came. But—Mr. Bell—have you come from Oxford or from Milton?"

"From Milton. Don't you see I'm smoke-dried?"

"Certainly. But I thought that it might be the effect of the antiquities of Oxford."

"Come now, be a sensible woman! In Oxford, I could have managed all the landlords in the place, and had my own way, with half the trouble your Milton landlord has given me, and defeated me after all. He won't take the house off our hands till next June twelve-month. Luckily, Mr. Thornton found a tenant for it. Why don't you ask after Mr. Thornton, Margaret? He has proved himself a very active friend of yours, I can tell you. Taken more than half the trouble off my hands."

"And how is he? How is Mrs. Thornton?" asked Margaret hurriedly, lowering her voice despite her effort to project.

"I suppose they're well. I've been staying at their house till I was driven out of it by the perpetual clack about that Thornton girl's marriage. It was too much for Thornton himself, though she was his sister. He used to go and sit in his own room perpetually. He's getting past the age for caring for such things, either as principal or accessory. I was surprised to find the old lady falling into the current, and carried away by her daughter's enthusiasm for orange-blossoms and lace. I thought Mrs. Thornton had been made of sterner stuff."
