"She would put on any assumption of feeling to veil her daughter's weakness," said Margaret in a low tone.

"Perhaps so. You've studied

For Mr. Bell, the vision of Helstone had taken root in the quiet hours following his conversation with Mr. Lennox, blooming uncontrollably throughout the sleepless nights that followed. In these dreams, he returned to the college where he once served as a tutor, now holding the distinguished rank of Fellow. Again, it was a long vacation; again, he was a guest at the home of his newly married friend, the proud husband and happy Vicar of Helstone. Together, they leaped across babbling brooks, impossible jumps that seemed to suspend them whole days in the air, defying gravity and logic. Time and space dissolved, leaving only the emotional truth of the moment. Every event was measured not by clockwork or geography, but by the depth of feeling.

In this realm, the trees blazed with the glorious crimson and gold of autumn, heavy with leaves that whispered secrets of the past. The warm, heady scents of flowers and herbs drifted through the open windows, filling the senses with a sweetness that felt ancient and real. Within the house, the young wife moved with a specific, poignant grace—a mixture of mild annoyance at her station regarding wealth, tempered by a deep, abiding pride in her handsome and devoted husband. This was exactly as Mr. Bell had observed her nearly forty years prior, a ghost from a lifetime ago.

The dream was so vivid, so indistinguishable from reality, that when he finally awoke, his waking life seemed like a phantom illusion. Where was he? In a cramped, handsomely furnished room of a London hotel. Who were those who had spoken to him moments before, moved around him, and touched his skin? They were gone—dead, buried, lost forever, vanishing beyond the reach of any earthly eternity. He was an old man, so recently exulting in the full, roaring strength of manhood. The crushing solitude of his existence became unbearable to contemplate. With a start, he rose, hurrying to dress, desperate to bury himself in the frantic preparations for breakfast at Harley Street.

Yet, the lawyer's business awaited him, a mountain of documents that caused Margaret's eyes to widen in terror and her lips to turn pale. As page after page was read, fate seemed to decree that every single piece of evidence capable of clearing Frederick's name slipped from her grasp, crumbling into dust. Even Mr. Lennox, whose professional voice had always been steady and regulated, softened. His tone grew tender, almost broken, as he approached the final extinguishing of their last hope.

Margaret had known the likely outcome before this hour, but the relentless, minute-by-minute detailing of each successive failure drained the light from her spirit until she could hold back no longer. Tears fell freely. Mr. Lennox paused, lowering his gaze from the papers.

"I had better not proceed further," he said, his voice thick with concern. "It was a foolish proposal on my part." He looked at her, offering a gentle gesture. "Lieutenant Hale..." He hesitated, granting him the title of the service from which he had been so harshly cast aside, a small kindness that seemed to soothe Margaret's raw nerves. "...Lieutenant Hale is happy now. More secure in fortune and future prospects than he could ever have hoped within the navy. And he has, doubtless, adopted his wife's country as his own."

"That is it," Margaret whispered, forcing a tremulous smile. "It seems so selfish in me to regret it... and yet, he is lost to me, and I am so terribly lonely."

Mr. Lennox turned the pages of his folder, silently wishing that he possessed the riches and prosperity he believed awaited him someday. Mr. Bell blew his nose, remaining silent beside them. In a few minutes, however, Margaret appeared to regain her usual composure. She thanked Mr. Lennox with profound courtesy, all the more graciously because she knew her behavior might lead him to believe he had needlessly pained her. Yet, it was pain she would not have been without.

Then, Mr. Bell stepped forward to bid her farewell.

"Margaret!" he said, fumbling slightly with his gloves. "I am planning to go down to Helstone tomorrow to inspect the old place. Would you care to join me? Or would it cause you too great a pain? Speak plainly, do not fear."

"Oh, Mr. Bell," she began, but her voice failed her. Instead, she reached out and took his old, gout-ridden hand, pressing a kiss to it.

"Come, come; that is quite enough," he said, flushing red with awkwardness. "I suppose your Aunt Shaw will trust you with me. We shall depart tomorrow morning and arrive there by two o'clock, I fancy. We'll take a snack, then order dinner at the little inn—the Lennard Arms, it used to be—and wander off to get an appetite in the forest. Can you manage it, Margaret? I know it will be a trial for both of us, but it will be a pleasure to me, at least. There, we shall dine—it will be nothing but doe-venison, if we can even procure it—and then I shall take my nap while you go out and visit old friends. I shall return you safely and sound, barring any railway accidents, and I shall insure your life for a thousand pounds before we start, which may offer some solace to your relations. Otherwise, I promise to bring you back to Mrs. Shaw by lunchtime on Friday. So, if you agree, I shall just ascend to propose it formally."

"It is no use for me to try and explain how much I shall enjoy it," Margaret murmured, tears still glistening in her eyes.

"Well, then, prove your gratitude by keeping those fountains dry for the next two days," Mr. Bell chided gently. "If you do not, I shall feel quite queasy about the lachrymal ducts, and I dislike that sensation."

"I won't cry a single drop," Margaret promised, winking vigorously to shake the moisture from her lashes and forcing a radiant smile.

"There is my good girl." With that, they ascended together to settle the arrangements. Margaret stood in a state of almost trembling eagerness as Mr. Bell discussed his plan with her aunt Shaw. First, the aunt was startled; then, doubtful and perplexed; and finally, she yielded—not necessarily to her own conviction, but to the rough, forceful charm of Mr. Bell's words. To the very end, whether it was right or wrong, proper or improper, she could not find peace in her heart until Margaret's safe return and the happy fulfillment of the project gave her enough certainty to declare, "She was sure it had been a very kind thought of Mr. Bell's, and precisely what she herself had been wishing for Margaret, affording her the very change she required after all the anxious times she had endured."

"So on those happy days of yore
Oft as I dare to dwell once more,
Still must I miss the friends so tried,
Whom Death has severed from my side.

But ever when true friendship binds,
Spirit it is that spirit finds;
In spirit then our bliss we found,
In spirit yet to them I'm bound."

Margaret rose before the appointed hour, granting herself a brief, private space to weep silently, to compose her countenance, and to summon a brightness that would not betray her grief. Her lingering anxiety concerned only the timeliness of their departure, lest they miss the train; yet relief washed over her as they arrived promptly, seated within the carriage opposite Mr. Bell. As the vehicle whirled past the familiar stations, the landscape unfolded beneath a warm, pure sun that rendered the tiles of the southern towns a deeper, ruddier hue than the slate roofs of the North. Flocks of pigeons circled the quaint, peaked gables, settling softly amidst the heat, fluffing their iridescent feathers as if welcoming the warmth. The platforms were devoid of the bustling crowds Margaret had encountered on her previous journeys to London; instead, solitary figures lounged with hands in pockets, absorbed in the static observation of trains arriving and departing, leaving behind only tracks, sheds, and distant fields. The air danced over a golden stillness, transforming farm after farm into scenes reminiscent of German idylls and medieval romances.

Suddenly, the dreamlike reverie shattered as the carriage halted at the junction for Helstone. Sharper emotions pierced her heart, a mixture of pain and pleasure she struggled to define. Every mile marked a return to a world of association, a journey she would not trade for anything, yet one that evoked an ineffable longing for "days that are no more." The last time she traversed this road, it had been a somber season, her spirits broken, accompanied by her parents. Now, she traveled alone, an orphan, as if the world itself had conspired to erase them from existence. The sight of the Helstone road bathed in sunlight, the trees standing in identical summer glory, struck her deeply; nature remained eternally young, indifferent to the passage of time.

Mr. Bell, attuned to her turmoil, wisely maintained a respectful silence. They approached the Lennard Arms, a establishment that stood slightly apart from the road, suggesting a reliance on local custom rather than the pursuit of travelers. Its façade faced the village green, dominated by an ancient lime tree encircled by benches, beneath whose foliage hung the faded heraldry of the Lennard family. Though the inn door stood open, there was no hurried reception. When the landlady eventually appeared, offering an apology for her delay due to the demands of hay-making, her demeanor shifted instantly upon recognizing Margaret. She rushed to the shade of the parlour, her eyes widening as the sunlight caught Margaret's face.

"It's Miss Hale!" the landlady cried, calling to her daughter before embracing Margaret with maternal affection. She inquired after the Vicar and Miss Dixon, expressing sorrow that the clergyman had departed. Margaret attempted to speak of her father's passing and her mother's demise, but the words lodged in her throat. She could only offer a single, heavy word: "Papa."

Mrs. Purkis, sensing the gravity of the situation, turned to Mr. Bell for confirmation. "Surely, sir, it's never so!" she exclaimed, recalling a gentleman who had visited weeks prior, speaking of Mrs. Hale's passing but failing to mention the Vicar's illness. Mr. Bell confirmed the tragedy gently, noting the suddenness of the death and describing the deceased as a man of profound calm. He introduced himself as a longtime friend of the Hale family and godfather to Margaret, explaining that they had traveled together specifically to honor the old place.

In Margaret's chamber, the casement window was framed by wild roses and vines. Pushing them aside, she glimpsed the chimney pots of the parsonage above the trees, tracing familiar lines through the leaves. Downstairs, Mrs. Purkis prepared for the guests' arrival, lamenting the changes in the village. The new Vicar, a teetotaler and magistrate with seven children, was constructing a nursery where the old arbour once stood. He and his wife, described as energetic and vocal, had installed new grates and plate-glass windows, sparking local debates over ginger-beer versus malt liquor and medicinal practices versus confectionery remedies. Mrs. Purkis departed briefly to tend to the inn, promising to return soon.

Upon descending, Mr. Bell presented a rustic luncheon of strawberries, cream, brown bread, milk, Stilton cheese, and port wine. Following this meal, they ventured forth for a walk, hesitating on the path as so many familiar landmarks beckoned. They bypassed the vicarage initially, choosing a route that allowed them to circle back later. As they walked, the landscape revealed subtle transformations: ancient trees felled the previous autumn, squatter cottages replaced by tidy, respectable dwellings, and gardens sprouting where the venerable, lightning-struck beech tree once stood. Margaret grieved for these losses, mourning the old man who inhabited the ruined cottage and the specific beauty of the vanished grove.

"I did not think I had been so old," Margaret murmured, turning away with a sigh.

"Yes!" Mr. Bell agreed. "It is the first changes among familiar things that create this mystery of time for the young. Later, we accept instability as a matter of course. For you, it remains new and oppressive."

Guided by a desire to connect with the past, Margaret led him toward the cottage where Susan's widowed mother resided. However, Susan was absent, having enrolled in the parochial school. Disappointment settled over Margaret, mirrored by the mother's apologetic silence. The mother recalled teaching Susan scripture nightly, lamenting the loss of a helpful girl who had surpassed her in learning. Mr. Bell expressed skepticism regarding formal schooling, preferring the simplicity of a home-based education rooted in faith and domestic duty.

Their conversation turned to the village gossip surrounding Betty Barnes, whom the mother accused of stealing her cat. Through further questioning, a harrowing narrative emerged. Induced by a gypsy fortune-teller to lend her husband's Sunday clothes, Betty had grown terrified when they failed to return. According to local superstition, the cries of a cat boiling or roasting alive compelled the powers of darkness to fulfill the wishes of an executioner. Consequently, the woman claimed she had resorted to roasting her cat to ensure Goodman Barnes recovered his missing garments. The account, delivered with unflinching sincerity, horrified both visitors. Margaret, unable to comprehend the lack of imagination or empathy required to justify such cruelty, walked away sickened.

"You are a good girl not to triumph over me," Mr. Bell remarked.

"Oh! I remember," Margaret replied, shifting the topic to Little Susan. "I must see her at the school."

Arriving at the schoolhouse, they were welcomed by the Vicar's wife, a brisk lady in black who moved with the confident authority of a hostess. She recognized Mr. Bell as the father of the deceased vicar and extended a polite greeting. Margaret, overwhelmed by the sentimentality of her visit, felt ill-equipped for inspection, yet the teacher's request for a grammar lesson brought her into contact with the eager faces of the students. Many were former parishioners baptized by her father, their features rapidly maturing beyond her memory. Seizing a fleeting moment, Margaret held Susan's hand privately before the lesson commenced.

During the instruction, the teacher struggled to explain the pronunciation of "a," prompting the children to correct her collectively by identifying it as an "adjective absolute." Abashed, Margaret sat in silence as the lesson concluded. Afterward, she lingered with a few favorites, observing how swiftly the children were growing into young women, just as she herself was fading from their collective recollection.

As the school session ended, the Vicar's wife proposed a tour of the parsonage to inspect the "alterations." Reluctantly, Margaret followed, expecting the modifications to jar against her cherished memories. Instead, the interior and exterior had transformed so thoroughly that the sting of familiarity was dulled. The meticulously trimmed garden, once a sanctuary of propriety, was strewn with toys and hoops, evidence of a lively household. The matted hall echoed with the chaotic energy of seven children. The former study, a place of green gloom conducive to meditation, now featured a new window opening onto the road, designed to monitor the Vicar's flock. The atmosphere was one of loud, brisk activity, lacking the delicate perception Margaret had valued in her father's home.

Walking back to the inn, Margaret chided Mr. Bell for his excessive praise of the new arrangements, fearing the Vicar's wife would interpret his admiration as mere politeness. Tired from the day's exertions, she declined another ramble, retreating to her chamber where she sat by the open window as twilight descended. The silence of the room amplified her inner turmoil.

"Mr. Bell, you remember what we were saying about Frederick last night?" she asked abruptly, her voice trembling.

Mr. Bell recalled the conversation. Margaret revealed a confession she had carried for months: a lie she had told to protect Frederick Hale following his mother's death. She recounted the terrifying encounter at the railway station, where a drunk man, Leonards, had fallen and died. Terrified that Frederick might be implicated, Margaret had lied to the authorities, claiming she was not present. She had withheld the crucial detail

Something Wanting.

"Experience, like a pale musician, holds / A dulcimer of patience in his hand;"
So ran the verse that lingered in Margaret's mind, framing the quiet rhythm of her days since returning to Thornton House. The air within the manor still hummed with the echoes of the summer wedding, a vibrant, chaotic symphony now played down to a gentle, domestic cadence. Dixon, fresh from Milton, became the vessel for these stories, carrying them in with the dust of the road and the chatter of the market. She spoke of Martha's departure, the opulent trappings of the ceremony, and the whispered judgments of the town regarding Mr. Thornton's financial state—a man who had lost heavily on the strike yet celebrated with a flourish that seemed to mock his precarious balance.

Dixon recounted the auction with a relish that bordered on the gossipy, detailing how Mrs. Thornton had secured bargains before Mr. Thornton arrived, only to bid against his own wife in a theatrical display of rivalry that amused the bystanders. "If Mrs. Thornton pays too little," Dixon noted, "Mr. Thornton pays too much," a balancing act that somehow satisfied Dixon's need for order. Yet, beneath the surface of these tales lay a deeper, more troubling silence. Dixon spoke little of the Higginses, her aristocratic biases blurring the details of their lives, though she mentioned Nicholas's persistent inquiries for Miss Hale. It was a peculiar rumor, however, that caught Dixon's imagination—that Mary Higgins had taken employment at Mr. Thornton's mill, compelled by her father to learn the trade of cooking. Margaret found the tale incoherent, a dream-like absurdity, yet it offered a fleeting connection to the world outside her window.

More than the gossip of neighbors, however, Margaret harbored a strange, undefined longing for Mr. Bell. Since their conversation at Helstone, it had been tacitly agreed that any necessary explanation must wait for a personal audience with Mr. Thornton, delivered by word of mouth. Letters held no weight in this matter, yet Mr. Bell continued to write, if not with the warmth of before, then with a brittle, complaining edge. His words carried a bitterness unusual for him, suggesting a weariness with the present and a regret for the past. Margaret, reading between the lines, feared for his health, though his brief replies dismissed ailments as merely the "spleen," inviting her indulgence in his grumblings without demanding medical updates.

The stagnation of Mr. Bell's movements at Oxford began to weigh on her spirit. Then came a fragment of truth dropped by Edith, revealing a conversation Mr. Bell had held in London. He had half-conceived the notion of traveling to Cadiz in the autumn, to witness the aftermath of the mutiny and, perhaps, to introduce Margaret to her new sister-in-law. To Edith, this plan sounded like a mere fantasy, a "Château en Espagne," yet to Margaret, it ignited a spark of desperate hope. It promised an escape from the monotonous circle of her existence, a chance to breathe air different from the stale atmosphere of Thornton House.

Yet, Edith's reaction was one of wounded affection. She cried, convinced that any pleasure Margaret might find away from home was a tacit affront, a proof of indifference. "Do you not care?" Edith sobbed, her jealousy a mirror reflecting Margaret's own isolation. Margaret, trying to soothe her friend, found herself confessing to Dixon, while dressing for dinner, a longing she could not suppress. Would Dixon not wish to accompany her to see Master Frederick and his wife?

"She's a Papist, Miss, isn't she?" Dixon asked, her voice dropping to a conspiratorial whisper.
"I believe—oh, yes, certainly!" Margaret replied, a flicker of apprehension dimming her spirits.
"And they live in a Popish country?"
"Yes."
Dixon's face fell. "Then I'm afraid I must say that my soul is dearer to me than even Master Frederick, his own dear self. I should be in a perpetual terror, Miss, lest I should be converted."

"Oh," Margaret said gently, "I do not know that I am going. And if I go, I am not such a fine lady as to be unable to travel without you. You shall have a long holiday, if we go."
"But I'm afraid it is a long 'if,'" Dixon murmured, her eyes betraying a hidden curiosity about Spain and its mysteries.
Margaret, sensing Dixon's reluctance, pushed the thought aside. The Spanish venture remained a phantom, a bright outlet that slipped through her fingers, just as the reality of explaining her feelings to Mr. Thornton remained elusive. Mr. Bell seemed stationary, bound by some unseen restraint, and the mist of misunderstanding hung heavier than ever.

Amidst these uncertainties, a small joy anchored Margaret's daily life: Edith's son. He was a creature of strong will, prone to stormy passions that left Edith exhausted and sighing for help. Yet, Margaret found a profound sweetness in battling him out in private rooms, where she wielded a firm, gentle power to subdue his tantrums. As he rubbed his tear-streaked face against hers, seeking solace, those moments tasted of a future she felt denied to her forever. In his trust, she found a glimpse of the unconditional bond she craved.

Another figure had entered the household with a cold brilliance: Mr. Henry Lennox. He spoke with a sharp intellect that flavored the otherwise insipid conversations of the house. Margaret detected a subtle contempt in his gaze for his brother's frivolous lifestyle, a disdain that manifested in sharp questions about whether Captain Lennox truly intended to abandon his profession. When his brother replied that he had enough to live upon, Mr. Lennox curled his lip, dismissing the material security as a hollow foundation.

Though the brothers were tightly bound by a dynamic of leadership and obedience, Mr. Lennox stood somewhat apart from the rest of the family. He was calculating, far-seeing, and proud, cultivating connections with profound intent. Their interactions, though frequent, carried a distance born of divergent paths. They had drifted apart from their former alignment in opinion and taste, separated by the chasm of Frederick's misfortunes. Yet, when Mr. Lennox spoke with epigrammatic force, his eyes would seek Margaret's countenance, listening with a deference that was both reluctant and deeply concealing. In her, he found an intellectual resonance that transcended the superficialities of their shared domestic sphere, a silent acknowledgment of a kindred spirit drifting in separate currents.

"Ne'er to be Found Again."

"My own, my father's friend!
I cannot part with thee!
I ne'er have shown, thou ne'er hast known,
How dear thou art to me."

The gatherings hosted by Mrs. Lennox were characterized by a specific alchemy: her friends supplied the beauty, Captain Lennox offered an effortless command of current affairs, and young Henry Lennox, surrounded by a select circle of rising men, furnished the wit and the sharp, encyclopedic knowledge they possessed only because they knew how to wield it without appearing pedantic. These dinners were ostensibly delightful. Yet, beneath the surface, Margaret harbored a quiet, gnawing dissatisfaction. To her, every talent, every emotion, and indeed every inclination toward virtue was consumed as fuel for fireworks; the sacred, hidden flame burned down to nothing but a fleeting sparkle and a hollow crackle. They discussed art in a purely sensual manner, fixated on external effects rather than seeking wisdom within the work itself. They fanned themselves into a theatrical enthusiasm for lofty subjects while in company, only to let those thoughts vanish the moment solitude arrived. Their capacity for deep appreciation was squandered in a mere torrent of appropriate, superficial words.

One evening, after the gentlemen had ascended to the drawing-room, Mr. Lennox approached Margaret. It was nearly the first voluntary address he had made to her since her return to Harley Street.

"You did not seem pleased by what Shirley was saying at dinner."

"Did I not?" Margaret replied. "My countenance must be remarkably expressive."

"It always has been," he observed gently. "It has not lost its facility for eloquence."

"I did not like," Margaret said quickly, "his manner of championing what he knew to be wrong—so glaringly wrong, even in jest."

"But it was remarkably clever," Mr. Lennox countered. "How every word landed! Did you recall the apt epithets?"

"Yes."

"And yet, you would likely despise them if you thought to add them yourself. Please do not hesitate, though he is my friend."

"There!" She paused abruptly, realizing the implication. "That is precisely the tone you adopt."

She stammered, turning away with a flush, unable to complete her thought. For a moment, Mr. Lennox waited to see if she would continue, but silence fell save for the beating of her heart. Before she could retreat further, he spoke again, his voice low and resonant.

"If my tones, or the nature of my thoughts, displease you, I beg you to tell me so plainly. Give me the opportunity to learn how to please you."

For several weeks, there remained no word regarding Mr. Bell's departure for Milton. He had hinted at it in Helstone as a potential journey within a fortnight, yet business could surely be conducted through correspondence. Margaret reasoned that if he could avoid travel, he certainly would; she understood his aversion to the place, and she felt certain he would not wish to undertake a trip where he might fail to grasp the private, oral significance of the explanation she required. She knew he felt the necessity of the meeting, yet whether in summer, autumn, or winter mattered little. It was August now, and despite his vague allusions to a Spanish voyage mentioned to Edith, there had been no concrete indication of movement. Margaret attempted to reconcile herself to the fading of this hope.

However, one morning a letter arrived. It announced that Mr. Bell intended to come to town the following week to discuss a plan forming in his mind. Furthermore, he planned to indulge in some treatment, having conceded to her view that it was preferable to attribute irritability and crossness to failing health rather than to his character. As Margaret read, she later noted a quality of forced cheerfulness in the letter. At the time, however, her attention was captured by Edith's dramatic outbursts.

"He is coming back to town! Oh, dear! And I am utterly exhausted by this heat; I doubt I possess the strength for another dinner. Besides, everyone has departed save for our dear, dull selves, who cannot decide where to go. There will be no one to welcome him."

"I am certain he would far prefer dining with us privately, away from any strangers, however agreeable," Margaret interjected. "Moreover, if he is unwell, he will not seek invitations. I am glad he has finally admitted it. I was convinced he was ill from the tone of his letters, yet he refused to answer my inquiries, leaving me without anyone to whom I could turn for news."

"Oh! He is not seriously ill, or he would not consider Spain."

"He never mentions Spain."

"No!" Edith insisted, her eyes wide. "But the plan he intends to propose clearly concerns that region. However, would you truly venture out in weather like this?"

"Oh, it will cool with each passing day. Yes! Consider it. I am only terrified that I have allowed myself to think and wish too deeply—with that absorbing, stubborn intensity that inevitably leads to disappointment, or perhaps fulfillment that, in spirit, brings no real pleasure."

"But that is superstitious, I'm sure, Margaret."

"No, I do not believe it is. It ought to serve as a warning, checking me from surrendering to such passionate desires. It feels like the ancient prayer, 'Give me children, or else I die.' I fear my cry is, 'Let me go to Cadiz, or else I die.'"

"My dear Margaret! You will be persuaded to remain, and then what shall I do? Oh! I wish I could find someone for you to marry here, so that I could be assured of you!"

"I shall never marry."

"Nonsense! Double nonsense! Why, as Sholto remarks, you are such an attraction to the house that he knows many men who would gladly visit next year solely for your sake."

Margaret straightened her posture with a haughty dignity. "Do you know, Edith, I sometimes think your life at Corfu has imparted..."

"Well?"

"Just a shade, or two, of coarseness."

Edith immediately burst into bitter sobs, declaring vehemently that Margaret had lost all affection for her and no longer regarded her as a friend. Overwhelmed by the shock of her own harshness and wounded pride, Margaret soon capitulated, becoming Edith's devoted slave for the remainder of the day. Meanwhile, the little lady, devastated by her perceived rejection, lay like a sacrificial victim upon the sofa, heaving profound sighs until, eventually, sleep claimed her.

Mr. Bell failed to appear even on the rescheduled day. The following morning, a letter from Wallis, his servant, revealed that his master had not been in good health for some time—the true cause of his delay—and that just as he was about to depart for London, he had suffered an apoplectic stroke. Wallis added that the physicians' consensus was grim: his master could not survive the night, and it was highly probable that by the time Miss Hale received this missive, her former tutor would already be gone.

Margaret read the letter at breakfast and turned so pale that her complexion nearly vanished. Silently, she passed the note to Edith and retreated from the room.

Edith was profoundly shaken, reading the letter with a sobbing, frightened, childlike terror that distressed her husband greatly. Mrs. Shaw was eating breakfast in her own chamber, and now the responsibility fell to him to reconcile his wife to the terrifying proximity of death for the first time in her life. A man who was to dine with them today lay either dead or dying! It took considerable time before she could summon the resolve to think of Margaret. Then, she sprang up and followed her upstairs to Margaret's room.

Dixon was packing a few toiletries, and Margaret was hastily donning her bonnet, tears streaming down her face, her hands trembling so violently she could barely tie the strings.

"Oh, dear Margaret! How shocking! What are you doing? Are you going out? Sholto would telegraph or do anything you desire."

"I am going to Oxford. There is a train departing in half an hour. Dixon has offered to accompany me, but I could have traveled alone. I must see him once more. Besides, he may be recovering and require care. He has been like a father to me. Do not hinder me, Edith."

"But I must stop you! Mother will not approve at all. Come and ask her about it, Margaret. You do not know where you are going. I would not mind if he had his own residence, but his rooms as a Fellow! Come to Mother, and please ask her before you leave. It will not take a single minute."

Margaret yielded, missing her connection. In the suddenness of the tragedy, Mrs. Shaw became bewildered and hysterical, causing the precious moments to slip away. However, another train was scheduled to depart in two hours. After various debates concerning propriety and impropriety, it was determined that Captain Lennox should accompany Margaret, for the one constant in her nature was her absolute resolution to proceed, alone or accompanied, by the next available vehicle, regardless of what others might say regarding the correctness of her step. Her father's friend, her own confidant, was lying at the point of death. The vividness of this thought surprised her with the firmness with which she asserted her right to act independently, and five minutes before departure, she found herself seated in a railway carriage opposite Captain Lennox.

It was a solace to her to believe that she had gone, even if only to hear the news that he had passed away during the night. She visualized

Breathing tranquillity.

"And down the sunny beach she paces slowly,
With many doubtful pauses by the way;
Grief hath an influence so hushed and holy."
— Hood.

The room in London was quiet, the heavy shadows of the journey to Oxford having finally settled around them. After the news, Edith leaned forward, pulling her husband's tall frame down until their faces were near. Standing on tiptoe, she implored him not to be shocked, her voice trembling slightly as she voiced the question that had haunted her since the revelation. "Is not Margaret the heiress?" she whispered.

Captain Lennox blinked, the news hitting him with the force of a sudden fog. If he had ever heard rumors, they had long since evaporated from his mind. A fellow of a small college? Leaving behind nothing more substantial than a pension? Two hundred and fifty pounds a year, considered in the context of a girl who barely touched wine, sounded absurd, almost comical. Yet, Edith's smile faded, replaced by a melancholy that crushed the romantic illusions she had built around her friend's future. Her vision of a grand, sweeping destiny had been blown to pieces by the mundane arithmetic of a lawyer's account.

A week later, however, the mood shifted dramatically. Edith bounded into the room, curtsying low with a radiant energy that belied her previous despondency. "I am right, and you are wrong, most noble Captain," she announced, her voice dripping with triumph. "Margaret has received a letter from a lawyer. She is the residuary legatee. The specific legacies total about two thousand pounds, but the remainder amounts to roughly forty thousand, calculated at current property values in Milton."

"Indeed!" Captain Lennox stammered, his composure cracking. "And how does she take her good fortune?"

"Oh, it seems she suspected it all along," Edith explained, her eyes dancing. "She simply didn't realize the magnitude. She looks terribly pale and white, claiming she is frightened by it, but that is nonsense. It will vanish soon enough. I left Mother pouring congratulations down her throat and slipped away to bring you this tidings."

To the general consensus of the circle, it became inevitable that Mr. Lennox assume the role of Margaret's legal advisor. Entirely unversed in the machinery of commerce, Margaret found herself constantly referring to him for guidance. He selected her attorney, presented documents requiring her signature, and derived a peculiar joy from instructing her in the arcane symbols of the law. He treated the process not merely as work, but as a courtship of sorts, delighting in teaching her the alphabet of business.

One afternoon, Edith teased him, her gaze arch and playful. "Henry, do you know what I hope and expect all these long conversations with Margaret to eventually lead to?"

"No, I do not," he replied, flushing slightly, though he quickly masked the emotion. "And I pray you do not tell me."

"Oh, very well; then I need not warn Sholto to stop inviting Mr. Montagu so frequently."

"Just as you wish," he responded, feigning a cool detachment that failed to reach his eyes. "Whatever you envision may or may not come to pass. However, before I stake my position, I must ensure the ground beneath me is firm. Go ahead and ask whomever you please. It may not seem polite, Edith, but if you interfere, you will ruin it. She has remained fiercely resistant to me for quite some time; she is only just beginning to thaw from her 'Zenobia' mannerisms. There is a Cleopatra within her, waiting to emerge, provided she allows a touch of paganism to surface."

"For my part," Edith countered with a touch of malice, "I am delighted she remains a Christian. I know so few who truly understand the faith!"

That autumn held no Spain for Margaret. Though she clung to the slender hope that a fortunate circumstance might summon Frederick to Paris, where a convoy awaited, reality grounded her to Cromer instead of Cadiz. It was here that Aunt Shaw and the Lennoxes intended to reside. Having always desired her company, they had exerted only sluggish efforts to facilitate her separate wishes, a trait characteristic of their collective nature. Perhaps Cromer, in a certain sense, was the ideal sanctuary. It offered physical fortification, bracing air, and the necessary rest that her spirit required.

Amidst the vanishing hopes, one particular trust lingered with painful weight: the belief that Mr. Bell might have disclosed the simple truths surrounding the family circumstances preceding Leonards' tragic death to Mr. Thornton. Regardless of how his opinions had shifted since the event, Margaret had wished for any judgment he formed to be rooted in a genuine understanding of her actions and motives. It would have brought her peace, a resolution to a point on which she would otherwise remain perpetually restless throughout her life. Now, so many years had passed that explanation was impossible save through the lens of Mr. Bell's demise. She could submit to being misunderstood, resigning herself to a common human lot, yet her heart ached with a longing that persisted. She prayed that, in time—perhaps years hence, before his eventual passing—he might come to know how deeply she had been tempted. She did not necessarily seek the truth laid bare if she could be assured he would know it, but that prayer proved vain, akin to many others. Thus, she schooled herself into acceptance, turning her entire heart and strength toward the immediate future, vowing to strive and make the best of whatever lay ahead.

Her days at Cromer were often spent sitting for hours upon the beach, staring intently as the waves battered the pebbly shore in their ceaseless motion, or gazing toward the distant horizon where water sparkled against the sky. Unconsciously, she heard the eternal psalm rising from the sea, soothing her without her realizing why. She sat listlessly on the sand, hands clasped around her knees, while Aunt Shaw engaged in minor shopping expeditions and Edith and Captain Lennox roamed far and wide across the coast and countryside. Nurses sauntered past with their charges, whispering in wonder at what could possibly hold Margaret's gaze so transfixed, day after day. When the family gathered for dinner, Margaret was so silent and absorbed that Edith declared her "moped," seizing upon her husband's proposal with great satisfaction: they would invite Mr. Henry Lennox to stay for a week upon his return from Scotland in October.

Yet, amidst the distraction of seaside idleness, Margaret began to reorder the events of her life, placing them correctly regarding their origins and significance, both for her past and future. Those solitary hours by the sea were not wasted; anyone perceptive enough to read the gradual transformation in Margaret's countenance would have seen it clearly. Mr. Henry Lennox was profoundly struck by the change.

"The sea has done Miss Hale an immense deal of good, I should imagine," he remarked upon her first departure from the room following his arrival. "She appears ten years younger than she did in Harley Street."

"That's the bonnet I procured for her!" Edith interjected triumphantly. "I knew instantly it would suit her."

"I beg your pardon," Mr. Lennox replied, adopting a tone that blended half-contempt with indulgence—a habit he usually reserved for Edith. "However, I believe I distinguish between the charms of a dress and the charms of a woman. No mere bonnet could render Miss Hale's eyes so lustrous yet so soft, nor her lips so ripe and red, nor fill her face with such profound peace and light. She resembles, and indeed surpasses," he lowered his voice, "the Margaret Hale of Helstone."

From that moment, the clever and ambitious man devoted all his faculties to winning Margaret's heart. He adored her sweet beauty and discerned the latent breadth of her mind, convinced it could easily be guided to embrace every object upon which he had set his affections. He viewed her fortune merely as a component of her complete and superior character and position, yet he acutely understood the social elevation it would immediately afford him, a poor barrister seeking advancement. Ultimately, he believed he would earn such success and honors as would allow him to repay her, with interest, the initial financial advance he owed her. He had recently visited Milton on business concerning her property, returning from Scotland, and with the keen eye of a seasoned lawyer attuned to contingencies, he observed that additional value accrued annually to the lands and tenements she owned in that thriving, expanding town. He was pleased to note that the current dynamic of client and legal advisor was gradually supplanting the memory of that ill-fated, botched day at Helstone. This arrangement afforded him unusual opportunities for intimate intercourse with her, complemented by the existing connection between their families.

Margaret was all too willing to listen, provided he spoke of Milton. Although he had encountered none of the individuals she particularly knew, the tone of her aunt and cousin—who spoke of Milton with dislike and contempt—was something Margaret was ashamed to recall expressing upon first settling there. Yet, Mr. Lennox surpassed even her in his appreciation of Milton and its inhabitants. Their energy, their power, and their indomitable courage in struggle captivated him. The vivid, lurid intensity of their existence arrested his attention. He never tires of speaking of them, failing to perceive how many of their ends were merely selfish and material until Margaret, even amidst her gratification, possessed the candor to point out this tainting sin in what was otherwise noble and admirable. Nevertheless, when other subjects grew tedious and her responses shortened, Henry Lennox discovered that an inquiry into some Darkshire peculiarity of character would instantly restore light

The smokestacks of Milton belched black plumes into the gray sky, joining the deafening chorus of roaring engines and clattering iron. Within the mills, wood, steam, and metal labored in a blind, endless rhythm, driven by the tireless energy of the crowds outside. Yet there were no strollers, no idlers wandering the streets for pleasure. Every face was etched with urgency or dread; the air buzzed with a fierce hunger for news. Men shoved past one another in the Mart and the Exchange, mirroring the ruthless competition that defined their lives. A heavy gloom settled over the town. Buyers were scarce, and those who appeared were viewed with suspicion by the sellers, for credit was a fragile illusion. Rumors swirled of failures sweeping through the neighboring ports, threatening to drag down even the most solid establishments. Though Milton itself had hitherto remained untouched by collapse, the shadows of massive, disastrous speculations loomed large. Whispers circulated in hushed tones: "Who has fallen?" "How does it touch me?" People clung to the names of the safe, like "Mr. Thornton," whose vast enterprises and prudent daring seemed like a shield against disaster. Yet behind closed doors, harsh truths were whispered between confidants. One man would lean in, voice lowered, to warn his neighbor: "Do not trust Thornton. He has poured every penny of his profit back into expansion. He holds no reserve. His machinery, barely two years old, cost him a fortune. If the tide turns, he will drown."

Such whispers cut deeper than they could know, for the truth was that Mr. Thornton *was* under severe pressure. It gnawed at his most vulnerable point: his pride. He had built his reputation as a self-made architect of fortune, believing that commerce offered the brave and honest a unique path to power, allowing them to command influence unseen by kings. He dreamed of a name echoing in distant lands, a legacy that would endure for generations. It had taken decades of silent toil to reach even a glimpse of this vision, only to find himself trapped in his own town, amidst the people he had once treated as strangers. The barrier between master and man had finally cracked, forged by the accidental intimacy of knowing Higgins. Until recently, Thornton had viewed his workers merely as labor units, but their recent contact had revealed a shared humanity, a "single heart" beating beneath the social divide. Now, however, the stakes had shifted. He faced the terrifying prospect of losing the trust of the very men he had begun to respect, jeopardizing delicate experimental plans that lay close to his heart. The fear was not of financial ruin alone, but of the isolation that came from failing those he cared for.

He reflected on the strike of a year prior, a wound that still bled. During those turbulent times, he had locked his capital into expensive new machinery and purchased vast quantities of cotton to fulfill contracts. The lack of skill among his imported Irish hands had damaged much of the finished product, rendering it unsellable to a firm proud of its quality. For months, this embarrassment had weighed heavily on him. Often, looking at Higgins, Thornton felt a surge of angry resentment, a desire to lash out at the source of his troubles. But he forced himself to curb this impulse. Avoidance would not suffice; he needed to prove to himself that he could master his anger. So, he allowed Higgins access, granting him audiences whenever business or schedule permitted. Slowly, the resentment faded, replaced by a bewildered admiration. How could two men, bound by the same trade, view their duties so differently? This new understanding softened the air between them, fostering a patience and charity that might prevent future clashes, or at least ensure they were borne with kindness. Furthermore, this bridge of communication revealed facts previously hidden from one side or the other, illuminating the true state of affairs.

But now, the trade had collapsed. Market values plummeted, halving the worth of Thornton's stock. Orders dried up, leaving his machinery idle and his capital frozen. Payments for completed orders were delayed, yet expenses continued to bleed away. Bills for cotton piled up, and with money scarce, he was forced to borrow at exorbitant rates, unable to liquidate any assets. Despair threatened to consume him, but he pushed forward, working day and night to anticipate disasters. At home, he remained calm and gentle with his wife, and in the mill, his brief, decisive answers carried a weight of burden that his workers sensed. They saw the strain pressing down on him, replacing former antagonism with a quiet sympathy. Higgins noted how the master sat, looking ashen and pale, like a sheet of dull wool. Higgins, moved by compassion, stayed late after hours, unknown to anyone, to finish a neglected piece of work that Thornton had ordered. The overseer, perhaps to save face, claimed credit for the job, but Higgins and a friend had done it in secret, hoping to spare their master further worry.

Later, as Higgins approached Thornton on Marlborough Street, a thought crossed his mind: *I wonder who would be more broken-hearted to see our master sitting like this?* Higgins stopped, forcing Thornton to halt his brisk walk. The merchant looked up, startled, as if woken from a trance.

"Haven't you heard anything of Miss Margaret lately?" Higgins asked.

"Miss... who?" Thornton replied, momentarily confused.

"Miss Margaret Hale—the old parson's daughter. You know who I mean?" Higgins pressed, his tone respectful despite the closeness of their proximity.

"Oh, yes!" Suddenly, the icy frost of worry vanished from Thornton's face, replaced by a warmth that lit his eyes like a summer sun breaking through winter clouds. Although his lips remained compressed, a genuine smile softened his expression. "She is my landlord now, Higgins. I hear of her through her agent occasionally. She is well, surrounded by friends. Thank you, Higgins." The "thank you" lingered, heavy with emotion, catching Higgins off guard. It was a small, golden thread woven into the dark tapestry of his fortunes. Might it lead somewhere?

"And hasn't she married?" Higgins ventured.

"Not yet," Thornton said, the cloud returning to his brow. "There is some talk of a match with a relative."

"If so, she won't be coming back to Milton, I suppose?"

"No."

"Hold on a moment, sir." Higgins stepped closer, lowering his voice. "Has the young gentleman been cleared?" He winked, adding a layer of mystery to his query.

"The young gentleman? Master Frederick?"

"Yes, her brother who was here at the lady's passing. Mary and I knew the whole story from the start, thanks to Mary working in the house. We held our tongues, but..."

"He was here? It was her brother?" Thornton confirmed, the realization settling over him.

"Sure enough. I only wished to know if they've cleared him."

"Not that I know of. I know nothing. I only hear of Miss Hale as my landlord, via her lawyer."

Thornton broke away, resuming his business, leaving Higgins baffled. Later, alone, Thornton murmured to himself, "It was her brother. I am glad. I may never see her again, but it is a relief. I knew she could not be unmaidenly, yet I yearned for certainty. Now I am content!"

This knowledge was a tiny beacon in his growing darkness. His agent had placed a fortune with an American trading house, and now that house, along with others, had collapsed like a house of cards. Engagements multiplied, debts mounted, and the interest on his locked-up capital evaporated. Night after night, he sat in his private study, buried in ledgers and papers, long after his family had retired. One morning, as dawn crept through the cracks of his shutters, he found himself exhausted, contemplating the futility of sleep. Then the door opened, and his mother stood there, dressed as she had worn the previous day. She had slept as little as he.

"Mother! Why are you not in bed?"

"Son John," she said softly, "do you think I can sleep with an easy mind while you keep awake with care? You have not told me what troubles you, but I have seen sore distress in you these many days."

"Trade is bad."

"And you dread—"

"I dread nothing," he interrupted, lifting his chin with stubborn resolve. "I know no man will suffer by me. That was my greatest fear."

"But how do you stand? Will it be a failure?" Her voice trembled with a rare fragility.

"Not a failure. I must give up business, but I will pay every man. I might redeem myself—I am sorely tempted—"

"How? Oh, John! Keep your name! Try all risks for that sake. How will you redeem it?"

"Through a speculation offered to me, fraught with danger. If it succeeds, it will lift me far above water, hiding my straits from all. But if it fails—"

"And if it fails?" She stepped forward, placing a hand on his arm, her eyes blazing with urgent light. She held her breath, waiting for the conclusion.

"Honest men are ruined by rogues," he said grimly. "As things stand, my creditors' funds are safe; every farthing is accounted for. But I do not know where my own money is. It may all be gone, leaving me penniless. Thus, it is my creditors' money I must risk."

"But if it succeeds, they need never know. Is it truly so desperate? I am sure it is not, or you would never have conceived it. If it works—"

"I would be a rich man, and my peace of conscience would vanish!"

"Bear up, brave heart! We'll keep our composure, strong and steady;
Sure, we can hold back eyes, cheeks, tongues from betraying a secret,
Let no small, telling sign reveal
That she was, is, and will forever be dear."

The air was heavy with the heat of a late summer afternoon. Edith stepped into the bedchamber, having already removed her outdoor gown for the evening's rest, though she would need it again shortly for dinner. Initially, the room stood empty save for the shadows lengthening across the floor. Moments later, Dixon appeared, arranging a dress on the bed, yet Margaret Hale herself was nowhere to be found. Edith paced, her impatience mounting.

"Oh, Dixon!" Edith cried, her voice tinged with irritation. "Do not lay out those dreadful blue blossoms against that gown of faded gold. It clashes horribly. Wait, please, and I shall fetch some pomegranate blooms instead."

"It is not a dead gold, madam," Dixon protested, her patience worn thin by the mistress's whims. "It is a straw hue. And blue pairs best with straw." Before Dixon could finish her objection, Edith had already returned with a cluster of brilliant scarlet flowers, pinning them to Margaret's dark hair with a flourish.

"Where is Miss Hale?" Edith demanded, testing the arrangement. "I cannot fathom how Aunt allowed her to drift into such erratic habits in Milton! I am constantly fearing she has stumbled upon some horror amidst those desolate streets. I would never venture down them unescorted; they are unfit for a lady."

Dixon, stung by the insult to her aesthetic judgment, replied curtly. "It is no wonder, in my opinion, that the world speaks so much of 'ladies' and treats themselves with such excessive delicacy. If women remain so fragile and dainty, I say there will be no saints left upon earth."

"Oh, Margaret!" Edith exclaimed, turning to find her cousin standing by the window. "Here you are! I have been searching everywhere. Look at you, child! Your cheeks are burning with the heat. Truly, that tiresome Mr. Henry has gone beyond all bounds. Just as I had finalized my guests so carefully, fitting them precisely for Mr. Colthurst, he arrives with an apology, using your name as an excuse, and asks if he may bring Mr. Thornton of Milton—the gentleman who rents from you, I presume—who is currently in London handling some legal affairs. It will ruin my arrangement entirely."

"I do not care for dinner," Margaret said softly, her voice lacking its usual warmth. "Dixon, prepare some tea here. I shall join you in the drawing-room by the time you return upstairs. Honestly, I feel I require lying down."

"No, no!" Edith hurried forward, smoothing the skirts of her dress. "That is absolutely impossible. You look dreadfully pale, yes, but surely it is merely the weather, and we cannot function without you. [Adjusts flowers] Those blooms look like glorious flames framing your black hair, Margaret. Besides, consider the irony: we intended for you to discuss Milton with Mr. Colthurst. Now, behold, a man directly from Milton has arrived. I believe it will prove fortuitous, after all. Mr. Colthurst can interrogate him thoroughly on every topic that interests him, and it will be delightful to trace your past experiences alongside Mr. Thornton's insights for Mr. Colthurst's upcoming speech in the House. I confess, I think Henry's intervention was a stroke of luck. I asked him if he was a man whose acquaintance one would deem shameful, and he retorted, 'Not if you possess any sense within you, my little sister.' Therefore, I assume he possesses the ability to pronounce his 'h's correctly—a rare accomplishment for a native of Darkshire—do you agree, Margaret?"

"Did Mr. Lennox mention why Mr. Thornton has ascended to London?" Margaret asked, her tone tight and controlled. "Is it legal business concerning the estate?"

"Oh! He has failed, or something similar," Edith dismissed lightly, recalling a previous conversation. "Henry mentioned it to you the day you suffered that severe headache—which was it? [To Dixon] There, that is perfect. Miss Hale looks magnificent, does she not? I wish I were as tall as a queen and as tanned as a gypsy, Margaret."

"But regarding Mr. Thornton?"

"Oh! I truly lack the capacity for legal minutiae. Henry delights in recounting every detail. From what I gathered, he appears to be in dire straits, yet a man of considerable respectability, and I was instructed to treat him with the utmost civility. Since I was unsure how to proceed, I sought your counsel. Now, descend with me and recline upon the sofa for fifteen minutes."

Captain Henry Lennox arrived punctually. As he sat, Margaret, flushing slightly, inquired about the circumstances surrounding Mr. Thornton.

"He has come to London regarding the sub-leasing of the property—Marlborough Mills, along with the adjacent houses and grounds. He finds himself unable to maintain the lease, and various deeds and agreements must be reviewed and renegotiated. I trust Edith will receive him with propriety; indeed, she seemed somewhat displeased by my eagerness to secure an invitation for him. However, I believed you would appreciate some attention being shown to him. One must exercise particular scruple in honoring a man who is falling from prosperity." He lowered his voice, addressing Margaret intimately, though as he concluded, he sprang up and presented Mr. Thornton, who had just entered, to Edith and Captain Lennox.

Margaret watched Mr. Thornton with an anxious gaze as he was introduced. It had been well over a year since their last encounter, and time had altered him profoundly. Though his stature remained commanding, elevating him above the average man, and granting him a distinguished bearing derived from the natural grace of his movements, his visage appeared aged and etched with worry. Yet, a noble composure rested upon his features, imparting to observers who had learned of his financial collapse a profound sense of innate dignity and masculine fortitude. Aware from his first survey of the room that Margaret was present—he had noted her intent, occupied expression as she listened to Captain Lennox—he approached her with the measured poise of an old friend. His first, calm words sent a flush of vivid color rushing into her cheeks, which refused to fade throughout the evening. She possessed little to say to him. She disconcerted him by asking, quietly, what seemed to him merely necessary inquiries about her former acquaintances in Milton. Others, more intimate within the household, interjected, pushing him into the background where he conversed intermittently with Captain Lennox.

"You perceive that Miss Hale is in good health, do you not?" Captain Lennox remarked. "Milton did not agree with her constitution, I surmise; when she first arrived in London, I am convinced I had never witnessed anyone so transformed. Tonight, she radiates vitality. She is significantly stronger. Last autumn, she succumbed to exhaustion from a walk of several miles. On Friday evening, we trekked up to Hampstead and returned. Yet, on Saturday, she appeared as robust as she does tonight."

"We?" Who? The two of them alone?

Mr. Colthurst, a man of sharp intellect and rapid rise in Parliament, possessed a keen eye for character. He was struck by a remark Mr. Thornton made during dinner. Curious, he questioned Edith regarding the identity of that gentleman. To Edith's surprise, the tone of Mr. Colthurst's "Indeed!" suggested that Mr. Thornton of Milton was not an obscure figure to him as she had presumed. Edith's dinner proceeded well. Captain Henry was in high spirits, deploying his dry, caustic wit with skill. Mr. Thornton and Mr. Colthurst discovered a few mutual topics of interest, touched upon briefly, reserving deeper discussion for private conversation after meals. Margaret appeared resplendent in her pomegranate flowers; though she leaned back in her chair and spoke sparingly, Edith felt no annoyance, for the conversation flowed smoothly around her absence. Margaret, however, was studying Mr. Thornton's face. He never looked directly at her; thus, she observed him unobserved, noting the subtle changes wrought by this brief interval. Occasionally, prompted by an unexpected word from Captain Lennox, his face flared with the old look of intense enjoyment: the merry brightness returned to his eyes, his lips parted to suggest the brilliant smiles of yesteryears; for an instant, his gaze instinctively sought hers, seeking her sympathy. But when their eyes met, his entire countenance shifted; he became grave and anxious once more, and he resolved to avoid looking near her again during the meal.

Only two ladies accompanied their party, and as these were engaged in conversation with her aunt and Edith, Margaret idly attended to some needlework in the drawing-room. Soon, the gentlemen joined them, Mr. Colthurst and Mr. Thornton deep in discourse. Captain Lennox approached Margaret, whispering, "I genuinely believe Edith owes me gratitude for my contribution to her gathering. You have no concept how agreeable and sensible this tenant of yours is. He has been the ideal interlocutor for coaching Mr. Colthurst on the facts he required. I cannot comprehend how he managed to mismanage his affairs."

"With his talents and opportunities, you would have succeeded," Margaret replied. She detected a slight distaste in his tone, though her words echoed a thought that had recently

"Pack clouds away."

"For joy or grief, for hope or fear,
For all hereafter, as for here,
In peace or strife, in storm or shine."

The next morning, Edith moved through the house on tiptoe, silencing Sholto whenever he raised his voice, terrified that a sudden clamor would shatter the intense quietude of the drawing room. By two o'clock, the doors remained shut. Then, the sharp rhythm of a man's footsteps echoed down the staircase. Edith peeked out from behind the curtains.

"Well, Henry?" she asked, her gaze questioning.

"Well!" he replied, briefly.

"Come in to lunch!"

"No, thank you. I've squandered far too much time here already."

"Then it isn't resolved?" Edith asked, her spirits sinking.

"Not at all. Never will it be, if 'it' refers to what I suspect you mean. That path is impossible, Edith. Give up thinking about it."

"But it would be so wonderful for everyone," Edith pleaded. "I'd rest easy knowing the children were safe with Margaret nearby. As it stands, I'm constantly afraid she'll drift off to Cadiz."

"When I wed, I shall seek a young lady who understands child-rearing. That is all I can offer. Miss Hale refuses me, and I shall not press her."

"And what exactly have you discussed?"

"A thousand matters you wouldn't grasp: investments, leases, the valuation of land."

"Oh, leave if that's all. If you've spent this long debating such weary subjects, you and she will be unbearably dull company."

"As you wish. I return tomorrow, bringing Mr. Thornton, to discuss further with Miss Hale."

"Mr. Thornton? Why does he matter?"

"He is Miss Hale's tenant," Mr. Lennox said, turning aside. "And he wishes to surrender his lease."

"Oh! Very well. I cannot fathom the details, so please spare me."

"The only detail that counts is this: ensure the back drawing room remains undisturbed as it was today. The children and servants come and go incessantly, making satisfactory business explanation impossible. Tomorrow's arrangements are of critical importance."

No one understood why Mr. Lennox failed to appear the following day. Yet Mr. Thornton arrived punctually. After waiting nearly an hour, Margaret entered, her complexion pale, her features etched with anxiety.

"I am deeply sorry Mr. Lennox is absent," she began hurriedly. "He could manage this far better than I. He serves as my adviser in this..."

"I am sorry to have intruded if I cause you trouble. Should I proceed to Mr. Lennox's chambers to locate him?"

"No, thank you. I wished to express my sorrow at losing you as a tenant. However, Mr. Lennox assures me circumstances will inevitably brighten—"

"Mr. Lennox knows little of the truth," Mr. Thornton interrupted, his voice low and steady. "A man blessed in every regard he cherishes fails to comprehend the weight of finding oneself no longer youthful, yet thrust back to the starting line demanding the hopeful vigor of youth alone. To feel half a life vanished, with nothing accomplished save the bitter memory of wasted opportunities. Miss Hale, I prefer not to hear Mr. Lennox's counsel regarding my affairs. Those who are themselves happy and prosperous often trivialize the misfortunes of others."

"You are unfair," Margaret said softly. "Mr. Lennox merely spoke of the high probability he sees in your redemption—not just of what you lost, but surpassing it. Please, do not speak until I finish." Gathering herself, she quickly flipped through legal documents and account statements, her hands trembling. "Ah, here it is! He drafted a proposal... showing that if you accept eighteen hundred and fifty-seven pounds of mine, currently idle in the bank yielding a mere two and a half percent, you could offer me superior interest and continue operating Marlborough Mills." Her voice had steadied. Mr. Thornton remained silent as she searched for the document outlining security proposals, desperate to view this solely as a commercial transaction where the advantage lay squarely with her.

As she hunted for the paper, her pulse seized at the timbre of Mr. Thornton's voice. It was rough, quivering with a tenderness that transcended business.

"Margaret!"

For a fleeting second, she lifted her gaze, then veiled her luminous eyes by bowing her forehead onto her hands. Stepping closer, he beseeched her again, his voice trembling with urgent eagerness.

"Margaret!"

Her head sank lower, her face concealed, resting almost upon the table. He approached, kneeling beside her to align his face with her ear, whispering, panting out the words:

"Take care.—If you remain silent, I shall claim you as my own in some strange, presumptuous fashion.—Send me away immediately if I must depart;—Margaret!—"

At that third summons, she turned her face, still masked by her small, white-clad hands, toward him, laying it upon his shoulder, hiding within its embrace. It was too exquisite a sensation to feel her soft cheek against his for him to desire seeing deep blushes or loving eyes. He drew her close. They held their silence. Finally, she murmured in a fractured voice:

"Oh, Mr. Thornton, I am not worthy enough!"

"Not worthy! Do not mock my own profound sense of unworthiness."

After a brief pause, he gently freed her hands from her face, arranging them as he had once positioned them to shield him from the rioters.

"Do you recall, love?" he murmured. "And how I repaid you with insolence the following day?"

"I remember how wrongly I spoke to you—that is all."

"Look here! Raise your head. I have something to show you!" She slowly faced him, burning with a beautiful, radiant shame.

"Do you recognize these roses?" He produced his pocket-book, revealing dried flowers carefully preserved within.

"No!" she replied, with innocent wonder. "Did you receive them from me?"

"No! Vanity prevented you. Perhaps you wore similar blossoms."

She studied them, puzzled for a moment, then smiled faintly.

"They are from Helstone, are they not? I know the deep indentations around the leaves. Oh! Have you visited there? When?"

"I sought to visit the place where Margaret grew into what she is, even during the darkest hours, when I harbored no hope of ever calling her mine. I went upon my return from Havre."

"You must give them to me," she said, attempting to pluck them from his hand with gentle firmness.

"As you wish. Provided you pay me for them!"

"How shall I inform Aunt Shaw?" she whispered after a moment of delicious, heavy silence.

"Let me address her."

"Oh, no! I am indebted to her—but what will she say?"

"I can surmise. Her first cry will be, 'That man!'"

"Hush!" said Margaret, "or I shall attempt to emulate your mother's indignant tones as she cries, 'That woman!'"
